The massless sine-Gordon model with logarithmic correlations in arbitrary dimension
Sami Vihko

Abstract
In this article, we study the sine-Gordon model with logarithmic correlations in arbitrary dimension d ≥ 1 d\geq 1 . The model is defined as a Euclidean field theory whose interaction term in the classical action is S int ​ ( φ ) := 2 ​ z ​ ∫ Λ cos ⁡ ( β ​ φ ) ​ d ⁡ x , S_{\mathrm{int}}(\varphi):=2z\int_{\Lambda}\cos(\sqrt{\beta}\,\varphi)\,\operatorname{d}\!x, where φ \varphi is a logarithmically correlated Gaussian field on a compact domain Λ ⊂ ℝ d \Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} that coincides with the Gaussian free field when d = 2 d=2 , and where z ∈ ℝ z\in\mathbb{R} and β > 0 \beta>0 . We treat both the massive and massless cases, with the main emphasis on the massless regime. We construct the field without imposing boundary conditions, together with its charge and gradient correlation functions, and we show that the partition function is renormalizable. The results are non-perturbative, holding for all z ∈ ℝ z\in\mathbb{R} . If d = 1 d=1 , our results are valid for the full subcritical range β ∈ ( 0 , 4 ​ π ) \beta\in(0,4\pi) and if d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 , the results are valid for β ∈ ( 0 , ( d + 1 ) ​ 2 ​ π ) \beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi) . Our analysis is carried out directly in the continuum: we perform renormalization via a scale decomposition and control the partition function by controlling the renormalized potential that arises in this procedure, using the iterated Mayer expansion of Brydges and Kennedy [ 13 ] .



1
Introduction

In two dimensions, the canonical sine-Gordon model is formally defined by the measure

\mathcal{Z}^{-1}\exp(2z\int_{\R^2}\cos(\sqrt{\beta}\vp(x))\dx)\nu(\,\mathrm{d}\varphi),

(1.1)

where
\mathcal{Z}
is a normalizing constant called the partition function, the reference measure
\nu
is the law of the two-dimensional Gaussian free field (GFF), either massive or massless, and
\beta\in(0,8\pi)
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
are the fundamental parameters of the theory. If we study the model on a bounded domain, we may also consider its restriction to the boundary. In this sense, the one-dimensional model can be thought of as a
boundary model
of the two-dimensional case. For simplicity, we consider only the case in which the boundary has a non-empty intersection with the real line, and we confine our constructions to this intersection.

In this paper, we consider only interactions restricted to compact domains
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, rather than to the whole space. We renormalize the model on
\Lambda
in the UV-regime and do not attempt to take the limit
\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. However, our massive reference field is defined on the whole space, so our approach does not specify any boundary conditions for the model.

Since the GFF is logarithmically correlated in two dimensions, the sine-Gordon model is naturally logarithmic in the settings above. However, if
d\geq 3
, the canonical model is no longer logarithmically correlated, and to obtain a logarithmic model we will replace the two-dimensional GFF with another logarithmically correlated reference field. This logarithmic model no longer corresponds to any canonical model of quantum field theory (QFT), but it can be thought of as a representation of a
d
-dimensional log-gas; see
[
35
, Section 1.2]
. Thus, the model is also interesting from the point of view of statistical mechanics, and we view it as a statistical rather than a quantum field theory. The main physical examples of log-gases are the two-dimensional Coulomb and Yukawa gases. The equivalence is at the level of partition functions if we take the grand canonical partition function on the Coulomb or Yukawa side. Furthermore, the parameter
\beta
has the physical meaning of an inverse temperature, when viewed through this equivalence. Thus, the correspondence maps the high-temperature regime of the gases to the small-
\beta
regime of the sine-Gordon model, and vice versa. Other examples also arise mainly in dimensions
d=1
or
d=2
; see
[
23
,
45
]
and references therein. We note that the correspondence of the Coulomb/Yukawa gas to the canonical sine-Gordon model (with the
d
-dimensional GFF as the free field) holds in arbitrary dimension.

In dimensions
d=1
or
2
, the log-gases exhibit a countable sequence of collapse points
\beta=\beta_{N}:=(1-1/N)4d\pi
, where
N
is even. Physically, these correspond to particles clumping into larger and larger multipoles, and the final transition point
\beta_{\infty}=4d\pi
is analogous to the Kosterlitz–Thouless (KT)
[
33
,
26
,
18
,
21
]
transition point in
d=2
, where the gas can be thought of as collapsing onto itself. This multipole picture was first proposed in
[
27
]

and recently established rigorously for the two‑dimensional Coulomb gas in the canonical ensemble
[
11
]
. On the sine-Gordon side, the dipole point
\beta_{2}=2d\pi
is where the model stops being a finite field theory. In the regime
\beta\in(d,2d)2\pi
, the model is super-renormalizable, but as
\beta
crosses the thresholds
\beta_{N}
, more and more counterterms are needed to renormalize the partition function. For
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
,
N
counterterms are needed. At the final transition point
\beta=4d\pi
, the model becomes strictly renormalizable, and for
\beta>4d\pi
the model is non-renormalizable or trivial in the RG-sense. In two dimensions, the point
\beta=8\pi
has been analyzed in
[
39
]
. The non-renormalizability for
\beta>8\pi
, or the RG-triviality, has not been rigorously established mathematically, as far as the author is aware. However, in the physics literature, Coleman, for example, has established that for
\beta>8\pi
the theory has no ground state and is therefore physically nonsensical
[
14
]
. With a UV-cutoff, the effective theory has been shown to be asymptotically free in the IR-regime
[
18
,
17
]
.

Mathematically, it is an interesting question whether such phenomenology persists for log-gases if
d\geq 3
. This is suggested in the introduction of
[
35
]
. However, our analysis shows that, at least for our canonical choice of reference field on the sine-Gordon side, something already happens at the point
\beta=(d+1)2\pi<\beta_{4}=3d\pi
if
d\geq 3
; see also
Remark

2.12
.

The two-dimensional sine-Gordon model is also interesting from other points of view. It is an example of a non-conformal integrable field theory, where integrability means that its correlation functions can be explicitly computed. Physicists have many predictions for these correlation functions; see for example
[
46
,
10
,
36
,
22
]
and the references therein. Besides the correlation functions, much about the model has been conjectured by physicists; see for example the book
[
37
, Part 4]
and references therein. However, a lot less has been mathematically proven. The classical sine-Gordon equation is also integrable; see for example
[
20
]
. The model also exhibits a phenomenon called bosonization. Bosonization means that there is a duality or a canonical mapping between bosonic and fermionic field theories. For massless sine-Gordon, this is known as the Coleman correspondence
[
14
]
, and the corresponding fermionic model is known as the massive Thirring model, see
[
16
,
8
,
6
]
and references therein. This correspondence is at the level of correlation functions. The sine-Gordon model also has connections, or parallel features, to imaginary multiplicative chaos, the Ising model, and imaginary Liouville CFT; see for example
[
31
,
5
,
42
,
29
]
and references therein.

Our analysis follows the construction of the model in two dimensions with
\beta\in(0,6\pi)
given in
[
6
]
. Thus, it is natural to consider a fixed canonical reference field
\varphi
, which recovers the model treated in
[
6
]
when
d=2
. The canonical log-correlated reference field we introduce is the field whose covariance operator is given by the (rescaled) Green’s function of the fractional massive Laplacian

K^{m}(x,y):=[\lambda_{d}(-\Delta+m^{2})^{\frac{d}{2}}]^{-1}(x,y)=\int_{0}^{\infty}C_{s}^{m}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!s,

(1.2)

where
C_{\cdot}^{m}\colon[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
denotes the massive kernel

C_{s}^{m}(x,y):=e^{-m^{2}s}C_{s}(x,y):=\frac{1}{4\pi s}e^{-m^{2}s-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4s}}.

(1.3)

The rescaling constant is
\lambda_{d}=(2^{\,2-d}\,\pi^{\,1-\frac{d}{2}})/\Gamma\!\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)
, but this is unimportant for our analysis; we adopt this rescaling merely to obtain, in arbitrary dimension, kernels of the same form as those in
[
6
]
for
d=2
. For
d=2
, the kernels are simply the heat kernels. We also introduce the notation
C_{s}
for the massless kernel, as it will be needed later. Note that these kernels are themselves covariance functions. One may also write
K^{m}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{0}(m|x-y|)
, where
K_{0}
denotes the modified Bessel function of order zero. The well-known asymptotic formula
K_{0}(x)=-\gamma-\log(\half x)+\mathcal{O}(x)
shows that the covariance indeed has a logarithmic singularity
K^{m}(x,y)\sim-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(m|x-y|)
both at the diagonal of the spatial variables and as
m\to 0
.

Remark 1.1
.

In the canonical model, for
d>2
the reference field would be the
d
-dimensional massive
GFF
with covariance given by the Green’s function of the ordinary massive Laplacian

\begin{split}(-\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}(x,y):=\int_{0}^{\infty}H_{s}^{m}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!s=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\left(\frac{m}{|x-y|}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}K_{\frac{d}{2}-1}(m|x-y|)\end{split}

(1.4)

where
H_{t}^{m}(x,y):=(4\pi t)^{-d/2}e^{-m^{2}t-|x-y|^{2}/(4t)}

is the massive heat kernel and
K_{\nu}
again denotes the modified Bessel function of order
\nu
.

This covariance has a power-law singularity
(-\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}(x,y)\sim C_{d}|x-y|^{-d+2}
rather than a logarithmic one. From the Coulomb point of view,
d=3
would be the physically natural dimension, but unfortunately our methods do not work for
d\geq 3
in this case.

Remark 1.2
.

From now on, we will abuse terminology and refer to the reference field as the free field of the theory or model, or simply as the free field.

We adopt the following regularization. Consider a Gaussian field
\varphi_{m,\epsilon}
with covariance function
K_{\epsilon}^{m}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

K_{\epsilon}^{m}(x,y):=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{m,\epsilon}(x)\varphi_{m,\epsilon}(y)]\equiv\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x)\varphi(y)]:=\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\infty}C_{s}^{m}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!s.

(1.5)

We have introduced two equivalent notations: either the dependence on the parameters
m,\epsilon
is attached to the field itself, or it is encoded in the expectation symbol. In the latter case, the subscripts specify the probability measure, and
\varphi
is then merely a placeholder for the field with this law. A similar convention will be used for the associated measures and the truncated expectation symbol
\mathbb{E}^{T}
to be introduced in the subsection below. When expectations involve more than one type of field, we will explicitly indicate with respect to which field the expectation is taken. A Kolmogorov–Chentsov-type argument (see Proposition B.2 in the appendix of
[
34
]
) shows that this field is smooth; that is, the law
\nu_{m,\epsilon}
of
\varphi_{m,\epsilon}
is a Gaussian measure supported on
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Moreover, this measure is symmetric, translation invariant, and rotation invariant.

The regularized sine-Gordon measure is then defined by

\nu_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}(\,\mathrm{d}\varphi):=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}\exp\!\Bigl(2z\int_{\Lambda}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\cos(\sqrt{\beta}\vp(x))\operatorname{d}\!x\Bigr)\nu_{m,\epsilon}(\,\mathrm{d}\varphi),

(1.6)

and the corresponding expectation will be denoted by
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}
. In the expression for the bare (or non-renormalized) regularized partition function,

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon):=\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[e^{2z\int_{\Lambda}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\cos(\sqrt{\beta}\vp(x))\operatorname{d}\!x}\right],

(1.7)

we have omitted the
\mathrm{sG}
subscript for brevity. Since a renormalized partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{R}
will also appear later, we shall simply refer to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
as the partition function.

The charge and gradient correlation functions (and their mixtures) that we study are correlations of the objects
\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sigma\sqrt{\beta}\varphi(x)}
and
D\varphi(x)
, where
\sigma\in\{-1,1\}
and
D
denotes a first-order constant-coefficient linear partial differential operator (or simply the derivative when
d=1
). More precisely, we consider smeared versions of the truncated correlations of these quantities; see the next subsection for the precise definitions.

Remark 1.3
.

Note that the factor
\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}
is just Wick ordering and an additional multiplicative mass renormalization. That is,

\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma\varphi_{m,\epsilon}(x)}:=:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma\varphi_{m,\epsilon}(x)}:\Delta(m),

(1.8)

and the cosine is a sum of such terms. The factor
\Delta(m)
can be derived explicitly, but we do not need it. Alternatively, one can think of
\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}
simply as the way
1/\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\varphi(x)}]
diverges as
\epsilon\to 0
.

1.1
Main results

To make sense of the
\epsilon\to 0
and
m,\epsilon\to 0
limits of the objects above beyond the region
\beta\in(0,2d\pi)
, we need to specify the renormalization scheme we will use.

First, we introduce some notation and terminology. For a map
F
(of the field
\varphi
below) and a suitable test function
f
, we denote

F(\varphi)(f):=\int F(\varphi(x))f(x)\operatorname{d}\!x,

(1.9)

where the integration is always over the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In the case of functions
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\}\to\mathbb{C}
,

F(\varphi)(f):=\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\int F_{\sigma}(\varphi(x))f(x,\sigma)\operatorname{d}\!x.

(1.10)

Note that if
F
is the identity function, we simply have
\varphi(f)=\int\varphi(x)f(x)\operatorname{d}\!x
. In addition, on the left-hand side of
Equation

1.9
we may also let
F
be a first-order constant-coefficient differential operator. In this case, we interpret it in the distributional sense unless otherwise specified. Thus, for a first-order (constant-coefficient) differential operator
D
we define

(D\varphi)(g):=-\int\varphi(x)(Dg)(x)\operatorname{d}\!x

(1.11)

for test functions
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

By smeared correlation functions, or moments of objects
F_{i}(\varphi(\cdot))
, we mean the expectations

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}F_{i}(\varphi)(f)\right]:=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\int F_{i}(\varphi(x_{i}))f(x_{i})\operatorname{d}\!x_{i}\right]

(1.12)

or analogously if
\sigma
dependence is present. Here the expectation is taken with respect to the law of the field
\varphi
.

The term truncated correlation functions refers to cumulants; see Appendix
A
for definitions and crucial properties. We will use the following notation for them. Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a collection of generic random variables and let
\kappa(\cdot)
denote their joint cumulants. For
J:=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{j}\}\subset I
, we denote

\mathbb{E}^{T}[X_{j}\mid j\in J]:=\kappa(X_{i_{1}};X_{i_{2}};\dots;X_{i_{j}}).

(1.13)

Note that this should not be confused with the notation for conditional expectations, which do not appear in this article. For two different collections
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\{Y_{j}\}_{j\in J}
that need to be distinguished, we use the formal product notation

\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[\prod_{i\in\tilde{I}}X_{i}\prod_{j\in\tilde{J}}Y_{j}\right]:=\kappa(X_{i_{1}};\dots;X_{i_{n}};Y_{j_{1}};\dots;Y_{j_{k}})

(1.14)

for arbitrary finite sets
\tilde{I}=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\}\subset I
and
\tilde{J}=\{j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{k}\}\subset J
. An analogous notation may also be used if one of the products is replaced by a power of a single random variable.

We are interested in the
m,\epsilon\to 0
limits of the correlation functions

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}^{n,k}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}):=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{k}[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})\right],

(1.15)

where
f_{j}\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
(compactly supported, essentially bounded functions),
j=1,2,\dots,n
, and
g_{l}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
l=1,2,\dots,k
. We choose the differential operators
D_{i}
to be partial derivatives with respect to arbitrary components of
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, or simply the derivative when
d=1
.

We call the limits of the above correlation functions the smeared and truncated mixed charge and gradient correlation functions of our sine-Gordon model. Possible further restrictions or conditions on the test functions will be specified in the theorems below. We also note that the linear constant‑coefficient partial differential operators mentioned above can be constructed from the partial derivatives. The natural operators for
d=2
are the holomorphic or Wirtinger derivatives
\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\pm i\partial_{y})
. Analogous correlation functions of the free field will play a crucial role in the analysis, and these are discussed in Appendix
B
.

Next, we define the renormalized partition function and the setup in which most of our analysis will take place. First, let
\beta\in(0,4d\pi)
and let
N\equiv N(\beta)
be such that
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
, where, as mentioned in the introduction,
\beta_{N}:=(1-1/N)4d\pi
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\leq N/2
and
\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
put

Z_{k}(\eta):=\exp(-\frac{1}{(2k)!}\sum_{\Si\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\1_{\{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_i=0\}}\int_{\R^{2kd}}\rbr{\prod_{i=1}^{2k}\eta(x_i,\sigma_i)}\E_{m,\eps}^T\abr{\eps^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_j\vp(x_j)}\,\big|\, j\in[2k]}\d\xx),

(1.16)

where
[2k]:=\{1,2,\dots,2k\}
is the discrete interval from
1
to
2k
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}:=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{2k})
, and
\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}:=\operatorname{d}\!x_{1}\operatorname{d}\!x_{2}\dots\operatorname{d}\!x_{2k}
, with
\operatorname{d}\!x_{j}
denoting the
d
‑dimensional Lebesgue measure.

Then put

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon):=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}Z_{k}(-z\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}).

(1.17)

The notation above is chosen so that we may carry out the general analysis with arbitrary
\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
. The generalized partition function introduced at the beginning of
Section

2
will then depend on
\eta
instead of
z
and
\Lambda
.

Remark 1.4
.

For
d=1
, we will always first fix
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
and then let
N
be as above. For
d\geq 2
, we will always have
N=0
or
N=2
, since we will be working with
\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
.

Now we are ready to state the main results.

Theorem A

(Renormalizability of the partition function)

Fix
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
if
d=1
and
\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
if
d\geq 2
, and let
N\equiv N(\beta)
be such that
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. Also assume that
z\in\mathbb{R}
,
m\in(0,\infty)
, and that
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is compact. Then the limits

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m)&:=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)\\
\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z,\Lambda)&:=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)\end{split}

(1.18)

exist and are finite.

Furthermore, the maps
z\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z,\Lambda)
and
z\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m)
can be extended to entire functions on the complex plane
\mathbb{C}
, and they are positive for
z\in\mathbb{R}
. The map
z\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z,\Lambda)
is also an even function.

Proof.

This is precisely the content of the first three statements of
Theorem

3.1
, proved in
Section

3.3
, for
\eta=-z\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}
.
∎

Theorem B

(Existence of the correlation functions)

Fix
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
if
d=1
and
\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
if
d\geq 2
, and let
N\equiv N(\beta)
be such that
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. Let
z\in\mathbb{R}
,
n,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
m\in(0,\infty)
, and let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be compact. Suppose
f_{j}\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
,
g_{l}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
j=1,2,\dots,n
,
l=1,2,\dots,k
. Then we have:

1.

If either
n>N(\beta)
,
k\geq 1
, or
d\geq 2
, and, in the last case,
n=N(\beta)=2
and
\supp(f_{1})\cap\supp(f_{2})=\emptyset
, then the limit

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda)}^{n,k}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})&\,\,\equiv\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda)}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}:(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{k}[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})\right]\\
&:=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}^{n,k}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})\end{split}

(1.19)

exists and is finite.

2.

Assuming the conditions in part 1. above,
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda)}^{n,k}
has an analytic extension (in
z
) to some neighborhood
U\equiv U(\Lambda)
of the real axis
\mathbb{R}
.

3.

Finally, assuming the conditions of part 1. above, we have, for any
q\geq 0
,

\begin{split}+\frac{d^{q}}{dz^{q}}\bigg|_{z=0}&\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda)}^{n,k}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})\\
&=\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[\left(:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\varphi}:(\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda})+:e^{-i\sqrt{\beta}\varphi}:(\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda})\right)^{q}\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}:(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{k}(D_{l}\varphi)(g_{l})\right]\\
\end{split}

(1.20)

where the expectation is now with respect to the law of the free field, and the RHS is defined by an analogous limit of the regularized free field, as in part 1.

Furthermore, analogous statements hold for fixed
m
.

Remark 1.5
.

The
:(\cdot):
notations above are formal analogues of Wick ordering adapted to this non-Gaussian setting, as used in
[
6
]
.

Theorem C

(Existence of the field)

Fix
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
if
d=1
and
\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
if
d\geq 2
, and let
N\equiv N(\beta)
be such that
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. Let
z\in\mathbb{R}
,
m\in(0,\infty)
, and let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be compact. Suppose
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda)\equiv\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)
. Then the limit

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}\left[e^{w\varphi(f)}\right]

(1.21)

exists and defines an entire function of
w
. If, in addition,
\int f=0
, then the limit

\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}\left[e^{w\varphi(f)}\right]

(1.22)

also exists and defines an entire function of
w
that is an even function of
z
.

Furthermore, for
w=i
these are Fourier transforms or characteristic functions of probability measures on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Lambda)
and
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Lambda)}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
, respectively.

Remark 1.6
.

We will prove the above theorems for
m\in(0,1)
, since other values can be obtained by rescaling space. Also, the main difficulties arise in the limit
m\to 0
, where our main interest also lies. Similarly, we will restrict
\epsilon\in(0,1)
in our analysis.

1.2
Strategy of the proofs, main novelties and the structure of the rest of the article

We closely follow the strategy used in
[
6
]
to prove the analogous results for
d=2
and
\beta\in(0,6\pi)
(which is exactly our parameter range
\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
when
d=2
). We also make crucial use of the Onsager-type inequality introduced in
[
35
]
in our inductive arguments, especially in the case
d=1
.

In
Section

2
, we introduce a crucial tool, the renormalized potential. First, we use the scale decomposition
\varphi_{m,\epsilon}\overset{d}{=}\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}+\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
, where the fields on the right-hand side are independent Gaussian fields with covariances
K_{\epsilon}^{m}-K_{\sqrt{t}}^{m}
and
K_{\sqrt{t}}^{m}
, respectively; recall the notation from
Equation

1.5
. We then integrate out the UV-rough part, that is, scales from
\epsilon^{2}
to
t
, and introduce the so-called renormalized potential
V_{t}
, which has a series representation known as the iterated Mayer expansion discovered in
[
13
]
. This series is written in terms of recursively defined functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}\colon(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, which turn out to be nothing more than the charge cumulants of the field
\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
. We control the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
inductively using the recursion. The Mayer expansion needs to be renormalized for
\beta>2d\pi
. The counterterms used in the renormalized partition function produce terms involving
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}
, which are equal to the full free field charge cumulants. Thus, we also need to control the difference
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
when
n
is even and less than or equal to
N
. These yield geometric convergence of the iterated Mayer expansion for suitable values of
t
.

In
Section

3
we use the iterated Mayer expansion for the renormalized potential to control the renormalized partition function. Furthermore, we derive an everywhere convergent series representation, in powers of
z
, of the renormalized partition function. Using the estimates for
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
and
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
obtained in
Section

2
, we prove that this power series also has a limit as we first take
\epsilon\to 0
and then take
m\to 0
. This yields
A
.

In
Section

4
, we prove
B
and
C
. In both proofs, after the crucial step of using the Cameron-Martin-Girsanov transformation, we write everything in terms of the renormalized partition function and the free field correlation functions. Controlling these allows us to prove the theorems.

The free field correlation functions are introduced and analyzed in Appendix
B
. We control the charge cumulants of the free field by writing them as
\tilde{V}_{1}^{n}+[\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}-\tilde{V}_{1}^{n}]
and separately controlling the contribution from each term, using the estimates established in
Section

2
.

Below, we list the main novelties of this work:

•

In
d=1
, we improve the results in
[
35
]
by studying the analytic properties of the partition function and proving the existence and analyticity of the mixed correlation functions. In particular, we treat the gradient correlation functions, which are not considered in
[
35
]
at all, and we work with smeared rather than pointwise correlation functions. We also explicitly control the
m\to 0
limit. Since
N
is arbitrary, the inductive arguments controlling the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
must be generalized from the case
N=2
in
[
6
]
. Here we use the Onsager-type inequality from
[
35
]
for terms with
n\leq N+1
. Moreover, the control of the terms
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
becomes more difficult in this case, since they can no longer be computed explicitly and we do not have integrable majorants for them that are independent of the mass
m
.

•

In
d=2
, as mentioned, we reproduce results analogous to those in
[
6
]
. However, we also make the analysis of the renormalized potential more robust and easier to generalize. First of all, we avoid using the infinite-dimensional Polchinski equation in the proof that the renormalized potential coincides with the iterated Mayer expansion. The induction controlling the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
is also made simpler: we do not need to treat the term
n=4
separately, nor do we need to treat separately the cases where the recursion for higher-order terms contains the term
n=2
. Furthermore, all of our estimates are uniform over the charge configuration. We could control the terms
\tilde{V}_{t}^{2}
and
\tilde{V}_{t}^{3}
as in
[
6
]
, but we choose to use the Onsager-type inequality here as well, to remain consistent with our analysis in
d=1
. The analysis used here generalizes straightforwardly to
d\geq 3
, and is in fact carried out simultaneously for all
d\geq 2
.

•

To the best of our knowledge, the construction is completely novel for
d\geq 3
. Most notably, we establish that the first collapse point
\beta_{2}
, that is, the point where the smeared charge two-point function of the free field diverges, scales linearly with the dimension. Our results also imply that the higher collapse points do not scale with the dimension. Indeed, as discussed in
Remark

2.12
, even the charge two-point function cannot be made locally integrable above
(d+2)2\pi<\beta_{4}
by our methods.

The guiding philosophy of this article is to use the case
d=1
as a testing ground for the procedure introduced above, while allowing an arbitrary number of counterterms. Consequently, if the analysis in
Section

2
can be refined to cover the full regime
\beta\in(0,8\pi)
in
d=2
, then analogous results would follow from our computations. Our analysis of the partition function and the correlation functions treats all dimensions equally up to the point where more refined estimates would be needed. Everything rests on the estimates in
Section

2
, in particular
Propositions

2.7
and
2.10
. Thus, if we can prove analogues of these in the full range
\beta\in(0,8\pi)
for
d=2
, everything else should follow readily. As they stand, they do not utilize possible cancellations arising from summing over
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
. These are difficult to propagate in the induction using the recursion
Equations

2.7
and
2.8
. However, we can also represent the functions defined by such a recursion as an expansion over certain types of trees, where the
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
-summation might be more tractable. We leave this analysis for future work.

1.3
Earlier results

The mathematically rigorous study of the two-dimensional Euclidean sine-Gordon field theory began with Fröhlich in 1976
[
24
]
, where he constructed the field theory in the finite regime
\beta<4\pi
. There, the only renormalization required is Wick ordering. He also investigated connections to one- and two-dimensional Coulomb and Yukawa gases. In the early 1980s, Benfatto, Gallavotti and Nicolò initiated a program to study the UV-behavior of the two-dimensional massive sine-Gordon model on the full range
(0,8\pi)

[
9
,
38
,
40
]
. These results use non-perturbative renormalization techniques, but they do not explicitly construct the model or its correlation functions. Instead, they prove the UV-stability of the problem by establishing upper and lower bounds for the partition function.

In the latter half of the 1980s, cluster expansion methods for the model were developed in
[
7
,
13
]
. In particular, Brydges and Kennedy initiated the development of several tools that we also use in this article. Dimock and Hurd were the first to apply rigorous renormalization group methods to construct the two-dimensional sine-Gordon model
[
19
,
17
]
; see also
[
43
]
for a recent account of similar methods. In
[
19
]
they construct the model for the full range
(0,8\pi)
and prove analyticity of the field correlations for sufficiently small
|z|
. Dimock and Hurd also observed that the UV- and IR-behavior of the model exhibit a form of duality when the threshold
\beta=8\pi
is crossed: the model is asymptotically free in the UV-regime with an IR-cutoff for
\beta<8\pi
, and the situation is reversed for
\beta>8\pi
.

The papers
[
16
,
8
]
construct the model for
\beta<16\pi/3
and prove the Coleman correspondence at the free fermion point
\beta=4\pi
and, respectively, for the full range
\beta\in(0,16\pi/3)
. Both articles treat only the case of small
|z|
. All of the constructions of the model above work in finite volume for the massless case. However, Fröhlich already constructed the free energy (or equivalently the pressure in the coulomb gas picture) of the massless model in the finite regime (
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
) and infinite volume in
[
24
]
.

We now arrive at the line of work to which this article belongs methodologically. In
[
6
]
Bauerschmidt and Webb construct the model and its charge and gradient correlation functions in finite volume for
\beta\in(0,6\pi)
, and for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
, using renormalization techniques originating in
[
13
]
. They also establish the Coleman correspondence at the free fermion point
\beta=4\pi
and use this to extend the massless model (as a probability measure on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
) to the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{2}
at this point. They also construct the massless model as a probability measure on
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
without the Coleman correspondence using correlation inequalities from
[
25
]
for the range
\beta\in(0,6\pi)
. Relying on similar techniques and together with collaborators, they have also proved other important instances of bosonization at the free fermion point
[
5
,
42
]
.

For the two-dimensional model, the non-perturbative construction in the full range
\beta\in(0,8\pi)
(even in finite volume) for both the massless and massive models, and the full plane construction for the massless model outside the free fermion point (even in the perturbative regime), are still elusive in rigorous mathematical literature.

In one dimension, we mention the construction by Lacoin, Rhodes and Vargas of the model for the full subcritical range
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
in finite volume. They consider a more general log-gas, which leads to a wider class of covariance kernels for the reference field in the sine-Gordon representation. As a consequence, they do not distinguish between the massive and massless models. They also construct the model and the pointwise charge correlation functions for arbitrary
z\in\mathbb{R}
, but they do not address the analyticity of these in
z
.

One may also consider various extensions or modifications of the two-dimensional model, as well as approaches based on techniques quite different from those employed in the present article, see for example
[
3
,
2
,
28
,
41
,
32
,
30
,
12
]
, and references therein. Finally, we stress that this list of earlier work is far from complete, as the mathematical literature on the sine-Gordon model is extensive.

1.4
Notation

In addition to the notation introduced thus far, we collect some further notation in this section.

For index sets we will use the following notation:
[n]:=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and
[[n,k]]:=\{n,n+1,\dots,k\}
for
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k>n
. Arbitrary subsets of
[n]
will be denoted by capital letters like
I
or
J
, and we will use sets as indices for the spatial variables
x
and the charges
\sigma\in\{-1,1\}
. That is, for
I:=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{j}\}
, by
(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})
we mean the vector
(x_{i_{1}},x_{i_{2}},\dots,x_{i_{j}},\sigma_{i_{1}},\sigma_{i_{2}},\dots,\sigma_{i_{j}})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{j}\times\{-1,1\}^{j}
. If
I=[n]
we omit the subscripts so that
(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}\times\{-1,1\}^{n}
. If there is only one type of field (
\varphi
) or test function
\eta
involved in the computation, we will use the shorthand notations
\varphi_{j}:=\varphi(x_{j})
and
\eta_{j}:=\eta(x_{j},\sigma_{j})
.

In addition to the regularized covariance
K_{\epsilon}^{m}
introduced in
Equation

1.5
, we will also encounter the integrals

\begin{split}K_{s,t}^{m}(x,y)&:=\int_{s}^{t}C_{r}^{m}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!r\\
K_{s,t}(x,y)&:=\int_{s}^{t}C_{r}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!r\end{split}

(1.23)

for any
t>s>0
. Furthermore, we will use the following shorthand notations

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}(x,x)&=:\mathcal{K}(0)\\
\mathcal{K}(x_{i},x_{j})&=:\mathcal{K}(i,j)\\
\mathcal{C}_{s}(x,x)&=:\mathcal{C}_{s}(0)\\
\mathcal{C}_{s}(x_{i},x_{j})&=:\mathcal{C}_{s}(i,j).\end{split}

(1.24)

Above,
\mathcal{K}
represents
K_{\cdot}^{m}
,
K_{\cdot,\cdot}^{m}
or
K_{\cdot,\cdot}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{s}
represents
C_{s}^{m}
or
C_{s}
.

We will denote constants in the estimates by
C
or
B
, and these may have subscripts or variables (
C_{\beta}
and
B(\beta)
would both denote a constant depending on
\beta
). We allow the constants to change from (in)equality to (in)equality in deriving estimates or making computations, to avoid naming new constants that are unnecessary to distinguish and which we do not need in the future.

The author wishes to thank Christian Webb and Eero Saksman for useful discussions during this work.

CONFSTAT funded by the European Union. Views and opinions expressed are however those
of the authors only and do not necessarily reflect those of the European Union or ERC. Neither
the European Union nor ERC can be held responsible for them.

2
The renormalized potential

We begin the analysis of the construction of our model by introducing an approach that was first used in
[
13
]
and has recently been employed by Bauerschmidt and Webb, and their collaborators in
[
3
,
4
,
6
,
5
,
42
]
. First let
\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
and
\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and define

V_{0}(\beta,\eta,\varphi|\epsilon):=\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta(x,\sigma)e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma\varphi(x)}\operatorname{d}\!x.

(2.1)

Next we define the generalized partition function

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon):=\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[e^{-V_{0}(\beta,\eta,\varphi|\epsilon)}\right],

(2.2)

which will play a crucial role in our analysis. Note that choosing
\eta=-z\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}
yields exactly the partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)
of our sine-Gordon model. In the proofs of the existence of the sine-Gordon correlation functions and of the field itself, we will choose
\eta
to depend suitably on certain parameters. The correlation functions will then be obtained by taking logarithmic derivatives of this generalized partition function with respect to these parameters.

To analyze the generalized partition function, we decompose the regularized free field
\varphi_{m,\epsilon}
into two independent components living on different scales. More precisely, recalling the notation from
Equations

1.5
and
1.23
, we write
\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
for the Gaussian field with covariance
K_{\epsilon^{2},t}^{m}
and
\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
for the Gaussian field with covariance
K_{\sqrt{t}}^{m}
. Since these are independent Gaussians and
K_{\epsilon}^{m}=K_{\epsilon^{2},t}^{m}+K_{\sqrt{t}}^{m}
, we have
\varphi_{m,\epsilon}\overset{d}{=}\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}+\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
. The field
\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
contains the UV-rough part (in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
) at small scales, while
\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
is the UV-regular part.

Next, as is customary in renormalization theory, we integrate out the rough part and write

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)=\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[e^{-V_{t}(\beta,\eta,\varphi|\epsilon)}\right],

(2.3)

where

e^{-V_{t}(\beta,\eta,\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}|m,\epsilon)}:=\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}\left[e^{-V_{0}(\beta,\eta,\varphi_{m,\epsilon}|\epsilon)}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}\left[e^{-V_{0}(\beta,\eta,\varphi+\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}|\epsilon)}\right],

(2.4)

where we have written
\varphi\equiv\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
, and
\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
denotes expectation with respect to the law of
\varphi
. The quantity
V_{t}
above can be defined for an arbitrary sufficiently regular field
\psi
independent of
\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
(or deterministic) in place of
\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
by the rightmost formula. Note that, since the test functions
\eta
are complex-valued, a priori only the exponential
e^{-V_{t}}
is well-defined. However, by looking at the map
z\mapsto e^{-V_{0}(\beta,z\eta,\varphi+\psi|m,\epsilon)}
(and its expectation with respect to the law of
\varphi\equiv\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
) we can prove that for small enough
\eta
also

V_{t}(\beta,\eta,\psi|m,\epsilon):=\log(\E_{m,(\eps^2,t)}\abr{e^{-V_0(\beta,\eta,\vp+\psi|m,\eps)}})

(2.5)

is well-defined; see the beginning of the proof of
Theorem

2.1
part (ii) from
Equation

2.59
onwards for details. Alternatively, considering the equivalence with the series
S_{t}
introduced in
Equation

2.6
below, we can view
V_{t}
as well-defined for any
\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
if
t
is chosen small enough depending on
\eta
.

The quantity
V_{t}
is called the renormalized potential in
[
6
]
. We will adhere to this terminology even though it is somewhat at odds with the rest of our usage of the word “renormalized,” since every other renormalized quantity involves explicit counterterms. Instead of studying the partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
directly, it is more convenient to study the renormalized potential
V_{t}
. Moreover, in order to analyze
V_{t}
, it turns out to be advantageous to consider the following series. For
\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d})
put

S_{t}(\beta,\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{nd}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}\right)e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},

(2.6)

where we have used the shorthand notation
\eta_{i}:=\eta(x_{i},\sigma_{i})
and
\varphi_{i}:=\varphi(x_{i})
, and we have omitted the dependence on
\beta
of the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
. The functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
satisfy the following recursion. For
n=1
,

\begin{split}\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(x,\sigma|m,\epsilon)\equiv\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)&:=\exp\!\Big(-\frac{\beta}{2}\left[K_{\epsilon^{2},t}^{m}(0)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(\eps^2)\right]\Big)\\
&=\exp\!\Big(-\frac{\beta}{2}\left[\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{t}C_{s}^{m}(0)\operatorname{d}\!s+\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(\eps^2)\right]\Big),\end{split}

(2.7)

where we have used the notations from
Equation

1.24
. For any
n\geq 2
,

\begin{split}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon):=\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{t}&\bigg\{\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}^{m}(i,j)\bigg)\tilde{V}_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m,\epsilon)\tilde{V}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m,\epsilon)\\
&\qquad\times\exp\!\Big(-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}^{m}(k,l)\Big)\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!s,\end{split}

(2.8)

where we have denoted by
[n]:=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
the discrete interval from
1
to
n
, by
I^{c}
the complement of
I
with respect to
[n]
, and by
|I|
the cardinality of
I
. Furthermore, we have indexed variables by sets: for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
J=\{j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{k}\}
we denote
\mathbf{z}_{J}=(z_{j_{1}},z_{j_{2}},\dots,z_{j_{k}})
for
z=x,\sigma
. We have also written
\sigma_{pq}:=\sigma_{p}\sigma_{q}
for
\sigma_{p},\sigma_{q}\in\{-1,1\}
.

We note that the sum
\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}
over non-empty proper subsets
I
of
[n]
accounts for all ordered bipartitions
\{A,A^{\prime}\}
of the set
[n]
such that
A\uplus A^{\prime}=[n]
, where
\uplus
denotes disjoint union. This means that for fixed
A,A^{\prime}\varsubsetneq[n]
satisfying
A\uplus A^{\prime}=[n]
, the sum contains both
\{A,A^{\prime}\}
and
\{A^{\prime},A\}
.

We readily see that

\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)=\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sigma\sqrt{\beta}\varphi(x)}\right],

(2.9)

where
\sigma=\pm 1
. This is the first charge correlation function with respect to the regularized free field at small scales below
t>0
. The equivalence above will generalize to
n>1
with the truncated correlation functions as stated in
Theorem

2.1
below. To obtain convergence of
S_{t}
in the limit
m,\epsilon\to 0
for all relevant values of
\beta
, it needs to be renormalized. First fix
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
for
d=1
or
\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
for
d\geq 2
, and pick
N\equiv N(\beta)
such that
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. Then define

\begin{split}S_{t}^{R}&(\beta,\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)\\
&:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{nd}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}\right)\left(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\sigma|m,\epsilon)-\mathbf{1}_{n\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0\}}\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.\end{split}

(2.10)

Note that the subtracted
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}
terms turn out to produce exactly the multiplicative counterterms
Z_{k}
in the definition
Equation

1.17
of the renormalized partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{R}
when we put
\eta=-z\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}
. As already mentioned, for finite
t
the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
are just the pointwise charge cumulants of the regularized free field at scales below
t
. Furthermore, the full (
t=\infty
) regularized free field charge cumulants exist, so we define
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}
through this equivalence.

Now we are ready to state the main result of this section.

Theorem 2.1

Let
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
for
d=1
and
\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
for
d\geq 2
,
\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
. Then there exists
t^{*}(\eta)\equiv t^{*}>0
such that for
t<t^{*}
(this restriction is needed only for the first two claims) the following statements are true.

1.

For
\varphi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the renormalized series
S_{t}^{R}(\beta,\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)
defined in
Equation

2.10
converges absolutely and uniformly in
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
.

2.

For
\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and fixed
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
the series
S_{t}(\beta,\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)
converges absolutely and equals the renormalized potential
V_{t}(\beta,\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)
.

3.

Finally, for fixed
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
and any
t>0
we have

\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)=(-1)^{\,n-1}\,\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mid j\in[n]\right].

(2.11)

Remark 2.2
.

The regularity assumptions on the field
\varphi
in the above theorem are minimal requirements for this exact argument, but are not necessarily optimal. The Gaussian field
\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
to which the result will be applied is in fact almost surely smooth for
t,m>0
.

For the proof of the above theorem we will need additional tools. First we have the basic lemma that serves as the prototype for the behavior of the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
.

Lemma 2.3

For any
\beta>0
we have

\sup_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}|\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)|\leq C(t\wedge m^{-2})^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}

(2.12)

with some constant
C>0
independent of
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
t>0
. Furthermore, for any
t>0
we can also obtain a constant upper bound, that is,

\sup_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}|\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)|\leq C^{\prime}

(2.13)

for some different constant
C^{\prime}>0
still independent of
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
.

The constants
C,C^{\prime}
may depend on
\beta
, but are finite for all
\beta>0
.

Proof.

Firstly, we have

\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)=\underbrace{\exp(\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{\eps^2}^1\frac{1-e^{-m^2s}}{4\pi s}\ds)}_{\leq C}\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\int_1^t\frac{e^{-m^2s}}{4\pi s}\ds),

(2.14)

where we have simply written the logarithm in the definition as an integral. The constant
C>0
is finite and can be chosen independent of
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Consider then the second factor. For
t<m^{-2}
we have

\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\int_1^t\frac{e^{-m^2s}}{4\pi s}\ds)=\exp(\frac{\beta}{2}\int_1^t\frac{1-e^{-m^2s}}{4\pi s}\ds)\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\int_1^t\frac{\ds}{4\pi s})\leq e^{\frac{\beta}{2}m^{2}t}t^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\leq Ct^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}},

(2.15)

where the constant
C>0
is independent of
m
and
t
if
t<m^{-2}
. Consider then the opposite case
t\geq m^{-2}
:

\begin{split}\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\int_1^t\frac{e^{-m^2s}}{4\pi s}\ds)&=\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{m^2}^1\frac{e^{-s}}{4\pi s}\ds)\underbrace{\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\int_1^{m^2 t}\frac{e^{-s}}{4\pi s}\ds)}_{\leq 1}\\
&\leq\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{m^2}^1\frac{\du}{4\pi u})\underbrace{\exp(\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{m^2}^1\frac{1-e^{-s}}{4\pi s}\ds)}_{\leq C\text{ for all }m\in(0,1)}\\
&\leq Cm^{\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\\
&=C(m^{-2})^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}},\end{split}

(2.16)

where the constant
C>0
is independent of
m
. Thus, by choosing the largest constant in the above analysis, we have

\sup_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}|\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)|\leq C(t\wedge m^{-2})^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}.

(2.17)

Finally, to obtain a constant bound we simply bound the second exponential in
Equation

2.14
by
1
.
∎

In the analysis of
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
for general
n
, we will need the following norms. For
f\colon\Lambda^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
we define the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}
for functions on
\Lambda^{n}
with
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}:=\begin{cases}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Lambda)}:=\esssup_{x\in\Lambda}|f(x)|,&\text{ if $n=1$}\\
\esssup_{x_{1}\in\Lambda}\int_{\Lambda^{(n-1)}}|f(\mathbf{x})|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{n-1},&\text{ if $n\geq 2$}\end{cases}

(2.18)

for suitable functions
f\colon\Lambda^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
.

If
\Lambda=\mathbb{R}^{d}
we omit it from the notation, that is,
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{n,\mathbb{R}^{d}}\equiv\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{n}
. Above, we have denoted
\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{n-1}:=\operatorname{d}\!x_{2}\operatorname{d}\!x_{3}\dots\operatorname{d}\!x_{n}
. If the variables are indexed by a set
I
of size
n
other than
[n]
, then the supremum is taken with respect to the smallest index, and for permutation-invariant functions we may take it with respect to any of the variables
x_{j}
and integrate with respect to the rest. In these cases, we omit the corresponding variables from the integration and use the same notation
\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{n-1}
for the integration measure.

Let us then introduce an auxiliary function that we will need in the analysis. Recall that
N\equiv N(\beta)
is such that
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. Then set

H_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\sigma):=\begin{cases}\frac{d(\mathbf{x})}{\sqrt{t}}\wedge 1,\quad&\text{if $n\leq N$ and $\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0$}\\
1,\quad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(2.19)

Above,
d(\mathbf{x})
is the
1
-Wasserstein distance between the distributions of positive and negative charges. First let us denote by
\nu
the charge distribution

\nu\equiv\nu(n,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sigma_{k}\delta_{x_{k}},

(2.20)

where
\mathbf{x}:=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}:=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{n})\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
and
\delta_{x_{k}}
is the Dirac measure concentrated at the point
x_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We denote the positive (negative) charge distribution by
\nu_{+}
(
\nu_{-}
). Next, assume that the configuration is neutral, that is,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0
. Furthermore, w.l.o.g. we may assume that the first
p=n/2
charges have signs different from the rest. Then we define the
1
-Wasserstein distance between the positive and negative charge distributions by

d_{1-\mathrm{Wass}}(\nu_{+},\nu_{-})\equiv d(\mathbf{x}):=\min_{\rho\in\mathbb{S}_{p}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}|x_{k}-x_{p+\rho(k)}|,

(2.21)

where
\mathbb{S}_{p}
is the symmetric group, that is, the group of permutations on
[p]=\{1,2,\dots,p\}
.

The function
H_{t}^{n}
arises naturally when we apply the Onsager-type inequality established in
[
35
]
to the exponential in the definition of
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
. We will present this result, adapted to our parametrization of the problem, in
Section

2.2.1
. Our task is then to recover the same function in the remaining estimates, so that we can propagate the induction through the recursion for
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
and prove the results stated below, which are central to our analysis.

We can now state the auxiliary results needed in the proof of
Theorem

2.1
and in our proofs of the main results. First, we have

Lemma 2.4

Let
d=1
, fix
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
, and let
N\equiv N(\beta)
be s.t.
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. Suppose also that
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}
is compact. Then for all
t\in(0,\infty)
,
n\leq N
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\Lambda^{n}
outside a Lebesgue null set containing the intersection of
\Lambda^{n}
with the diagonal
\{\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid x_{i}=x_{j}\text{ for some }i\neq j\}
, the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)
are differentiable with respect to
m
on the interval
(0,1)
. The above holds for any
\epsilon\in[0,1)
. Furthermore, if
\epsilon=0
, then
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
is also continuous in
m
at
m=0
. In particular, in this situation
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
is continuous in
m
on any closed subinterval of
[0,1)
.

Furthermore, there exist functions
\tilde{h}_{\cdot}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot)\equiv\tilde{h}_{\cdot}^{n}\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{-1,1\}^{n})\to[0,\infty]
and
\tilde{g}_{\cdot}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot)\equiv\tilde{g}_{\cdot}^{n}\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{-1,1\}^{n})\to[0,\infty]
such that
|\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|<\tilde{h}_{t}^{n}
and
|\partial_{m}[\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|_{\Lambda}]|<\tilde{g}_{t}^{n}|_{\Lambda}
for all
t>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In addition,
\tilde{h}_{t}^{n}
and
\tilde{g}_{t}^{n}
are permutation-invariant, independent of
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and satisfy the following estimates

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\left\lVert H_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\tilde{h}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}\leq[B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)]^{n-1}C^{n}n^{n-2}\begin{cases}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n},&\text{ if }t<1\\
\max(1,[\log(t)]^{n-1}),&\text{ if }t\geq 1.\\
\end{cases}

(2.22)

and

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\left\lVert\tilde{g}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}\leq[\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)]^{n-1}C^{n}n^{n-2}\begin{cases}t^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n},&\text{ if }t<1\\
t\max(1,[\log(t)]^{n-1}),&\text{ if }t\geq 1.\\
\end{cases}

(2.23)

where
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}
is compact.

Remark 2.5
.

For
d=1
, we need the mass derivatives of the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
in order to estimate terms of the form

e^{-m^{2}s}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon=0)-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m=0,\epsilon=0)=\int_{0}^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\left[e^{-\mu^{2}s}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|\mu,\epsilon=0)\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mu

(2.24)

in the proof of
Corollary

2.10
, when estimating the error term. This is needed because, in this case, we do not have mass-independent majorants for the differences
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
when
n>2
. Furthermore, the uniform majorants
\tilde{g}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
are needed to justify differentiating in the mass under the
s
-integral in the definition
Equation

2.8
of the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
.

Remark 2.6
.

By the third claim of
Theorem

2.1
, the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
are precisely the truncated charge correlation functions of the free field at small scales below
t>0
. The proof of this claim does not rely on any of the estimates in this section. Furthermore, the existence of the limit
\epsilon\to 0
(and similarly the limit obtained by taking
m\to 0
after
\epsilon\to 0
) holds for the truncated charge correlation function of the full free field (that is,
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}
) by
Lemmas

B.3
and
B.4
. The same holds for
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
by
Lemma

B.5
. We define
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)=:\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot|m)
and
\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)=:\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot)
by these limits of the truncated charge correlation functions. Moreover, since a priori we have
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
, we also define the values of the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
at
\epsilon=0
and at
\epsilon=0=m
by these limits, that is

\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m=0,\epsilon=0):=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\quad\text{ and }\quad\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon=0)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon).

(2.25)

All the above statements hold for any charge configuration
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
.

The proofs of the norm estimates for
\tilde{h}_{t}^{n}
in
Lemma

2.4
are straightforward modifications of the proofs of the corresponding estimates for
h_{t}^{n}
in
Proposition

2.7
below. The differentiability claim and the existence of the functions
\tilde{g}_{t}^{n}
must be established iteratively and simultaneously, but the estimates are again analogous to those for the functions
g_{t}^{n}
in
Proposition

2.7
. Since
Proposition

2.7
is the main driving force in our analysis, and we also need the techniques from its proof in the proof of
Corollary

2.10
, we prove it first, assuming
Lemma

2.4
, and postpone the proof of the latter to the final subsection of this section. Furthermore,
Lemma

2.4
is needed only in the case
d=1
. The proof of
Lemma

2.4
does not use
Proposition

2.7
itself, but only techniques developed in its proof. Thus, this is not a circular argument.

Proposition 2.7

Fix
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
if
d=1
and
\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
if
d\geq 2
, and let
N\equiv N(\beta)
be s.t.
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. Suppose also that
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is compact.

Then for all
t>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exist functions
h_{\cdot}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot|m)\equiv h_{\cdot}^{n}\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathbb{R}^{dn}\times\{-1,1\}^{n})\to[0,\infty]
such that
|\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|<h_{t}^{n}
. In addition,
h_{t}^{n}
is permutation-invariant and independent of
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Furthermore,
h_{t}^{n}
is independent of
m
for
t<m^{-2}
. The functions
h_{t}^{n}
satisfy the following norm estimates for all
t>0
,

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\left\lVert H_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n}\leq[B_{d}(\beta,N)]^{n-1}C^{n}n^{n-2}\begin{cases}t^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n},&\text{ if $t<m^{-2}$}\\
(m^{-2})^{(n-1)\frac{d}{2}-\frac{n\beta}{8\pi}},&\text{ if $t\geq m^{-2}$ and $d=1$}\end{cases}

(2.26)

with some constants
B_{d}(\beta,N),C>0
, independent of
m,\epsilon,t
and
n
, to be chosen suitably during the proof.

Furthermore, if
d=1
,
n\leq N
and
\beta
are as above, then there also exist functions
g_{\cdot}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot|m)\equiv g_{\cdot}^{n}\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{-1,1\}^{n})\to[0,\infty]
that are also permutation-invariant, independent of
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and s.t.

|\partial_{m}[\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)]|<g_{t}^{n}
.

The functions
g_{t}^{n}
satisfy the following estimates for all
t>0

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\left\lVert g_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)\right\rVert_{n}\leq[\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-1}C^{n}n^{n-2}\begin{cases}m\,t^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n},&\text{ if $t<m^{-2}$}\\
(m^{-2})^{n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)},&\text{ if $t\geq m^{-2}$}\end{cases}

(2.27)

with some constants
\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N),C>0
independent of
m,\epsilon,t
and
n
.

The constant
C
may initially be different in the estimates for the functions
h_{t}^{n}
and
g_{t}^{n}
, but we may choose the maximum of these. The bounds for
t\geq m^{-2}
are simply the same as for
t<m^{-2}
with the replacement
t\to m^{-2}
.

Remark 2.8
.

The statement
Equation

2.26
for
t\geq m^{-2}
is also true for
d\geq 2
, but we do not need it. Indeed, the estimates for
t\geq m^{-2}
are used in the proof of
Corollary

2.10
, but as discussed right after stating this corollary, the case
d\geq 2
can be treated using results from
[
6
]
.

Remark 2.9
.

These bounds imply that the mass derivative of
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)
behaves better than
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)
itself as
\epsilon\to 0
, since the function
H_{t}^{n}
is needed to cancel the singularity for
h_{t}^{n}
, but not for
g_{t}^{n}
. However, the opposite is true for the limit
m\to 0
when
t
is large. Indeed, the estimate for
h_{t}^{n}
in the regime
t\geq m^{-2}
can be made independent of
m
provided the total exponent
(n-1)d/2-n\beta/8\pi
is non-positive, that is,
\beta\geq(1-1/n)4\pi
for all
n\leq N
. This is guaranteed by the assumption
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. In contrast, the upper bound for
g_{t}^{n}
in the same regime diverges as
m\to 0
for all
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
.

Finally, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 2.10

Let
d=1
so that
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
and let
N\equiv N(\beta)
be such that
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. Suppose also that
\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\{-1,1\})
,
n\leq N
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0
.
Then there exist functions
F_{\cdot}^{1,n}(\cdot,\cdot|m)\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{-1,1\}^{n})\to[0,\infty]
independent of
\epsilon
such that

|\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)|\leq F_{t}^{1,n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)

(2.28)

and for any compact
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}

\sup_{m\in(0,1)}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\Lambda^{n}}F_{t}^{1,n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}<\infty.

(2.29)

Next let
d\geq 2
and
\beta\in[2d\pi,(d+1)2\pi)
, so that
n=N=2
. Suppose also that
\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
and
\sigma_{1}=-\sigma_{2}
. Then there also exist functions
F_{\cdot}^{d,2}(\cdot,\cdot)\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\{-1,1\}^{2})\to[0,\infty]
independent of
\epsilon
and
m
such that for any compact
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

|\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{t}^{2}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)|\leq F_{t}^{d,2}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}),

(2.30)

and

\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2}}\int_{\Lambda^{2}}F_{t}^{d,2}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}<\infty.

(2.31)

Furthermore, for any
d\geq 1
we have

\begin{split}\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}&\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{dn}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}\right)\mathbf{1}_{n\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0}(\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon))\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{dn}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}\right)\mathbf{1}_{n\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0}(\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}))\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\end{split}

(2.32)

where
\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m=0,\epsilon=0):=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)
. The claim in
Equation

2.32
also holds for fixed
m
with obvious changes. Lastly, we also have
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}\in L_{loc}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{nd}\times\{-1,1\}^{n})
.

Remark 2.11
.

The limit in
Equation

2.32
is always finite by the bounds
Equations

2.30
,
2.31
and
2.29
.

Proof of the case
d\geq 2
.

This case does not actually utilize
Proposition

2.7
. The first claim for the
d\geq 2
case, that is,
Equations

2.30
and
2.31
, can be established analogously to the proof of
[
6
, Lemma 5.4]
, and the bound
\beta<(d+1)2\pi
follows directly from that argument. Then dominated convergence allows us to pass both limits through the spatial integrals in this setting to obtain
Equation

2.32
. Furthermore, the function
\tilde{V}_{t}^{2}
and the corresponding pointwise limits can be computed explicitly.
∎

For
d=1
, the proof of this corollary relies on estimates similar to those used in the proof of
Proposition

2.7
and is more involved than in the case
d\geq 2
, since the majorant
F_{t}^{1,n}
cannot be chosen independently of the mass (or at least such a choice appears hard to achieve through inductive methods). For this reason, we postpone the detailed proof for the one-dimensional case and first establish
Proposition

2.7
. For now, we only give a skeleton of the argument for the proof of
Corollary

2.10
in the case
d=1
. The estimates
Equations

2.28
and
2.29
, together with dominated convergence, allow us to take the
\epsilon\to 0
limit inside the spatial integral, and the pointwise limits exist by the discussion in
Remark

2.6
.

For the
m\to 0
limit, we write

\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)=\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})+R_{t}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})

(2.33)

and show that

\lim_{m\to 0}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}|\eta_{i}|\right)|R_{t}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})|\operatorname{d}\!x=0.

(2.34)

We will now prove
Theorem

2.1
assuming
Propositions

2.7
and
2.10
. After that, we will establish
Propositions

2.7
and
2.10
assuming
Lemma

2.4
. Finally, in the last subsection, we will prove
Lemma

2.4
.

2.1
Proof of
Theorem

2.1

We will prove each of the claims separately, but first we need to rewrite the series
S_{t}^{R}
in a form that is more tractable for our analysis.

First, let us denote

S_{t}^{R}(\beta,\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{n}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)

(2.35)

Then we may write

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{n}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)&=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{nd}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}\right)\left(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}-\mathbf{1}_{n\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0}\right)\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{nd}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}\right)\mathbf{1}_{n\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0}\left(\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&\,\,=:\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\varphi}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)+\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\infty}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)\end{split}

(2.36)

where we have simply added and subtracted
\mathbf{1}_{n\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)
.

Remark 2.12
.

Analysing the term
\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\varphi}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)
explicitly for
d\geq 2
gives some indication of how far our methods might extend beyond the restrictions on
\beta
imposed in this article. In particular, it suggests limits on the renormalizability in the parameter range
\beta>(d+1)2\pi
. First of all,
\tilde{V}_{t}^{2}
can be computed explicitly (see
[
6
, Equation 4.60]
). We only consider the neutral part (
\sigma:=\sigma_{1}=-\sigma_{2}
) of
\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\varphi}
, since the non-neutral part is bounded for all
\beta>0
. Using this exact form, the neutral part of
\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\varphi}
becomes

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma[\varphi(x_{1})-\varphi(x_{2})]}-1\right)[\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)]^{2}\left(1-e^{\beta K_{\epsilon^{2},t}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})}\right)\operatorname{d}\!x_{1}\operatorname{d}\!x_{2}

(2.37)

By applying the cancellation from the
\sigma
sums (which we do not use in the proofs in this article), we can estimate the expression in
Equation

2.37
. For simplicity, suppose
\eta(\cdot,1)=\eta(\cdot,-1)
, which yields

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}(1-\cos(\sqrt{\beta}[\vp(x_1)-\vp(x_2)]))[\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)]^{2}\left(1-e^{\beta K_{\epsilon^{2},t}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})}\right)\operatorname{d}\!x_{1}\operatorname{d}\!x_{2}

(2.38)

Then, using

1-\cos(\sqrt{\beta}[\vp(x_1)-\vp(x_2)])\leq C_{\beta}(\left\lVert\gradient\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\Lambda})}|x_{1}-x_{2}|)^{2-\varepsilon(\beta)}

(2.39)

for some
C_{\beta}>0
and any
\varepsilon(\beta)>0
(note that the choice
\varepsilon(\beta)=0
would cause problems when we apply
Lemma

3.2
). Above, we used arguments and notation similar to those in
Lemma

2.13
below. Thus, using
[
6
, Lemma 4.4]
and
Lemma

2.3
, we obtain for
t<m^{-2}

|\mathcal{A}_{2,neutral}^{\varphi}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)|\leq C_{\beta,t,\Lambda}\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\left\lVert\gradient\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\Lambda})}^{2-\varepsilon(\beta)}\int_{B_{\Lambda}}|x|^{2-\varepsilon(\beta)}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sqrt{t}}\wedge 1\right)^{-\frac{\beta}{2\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!x.

(2.40)

for some constant
C_{\beta,t,\Lambda}
independent of
m
and
\epsilon
. By choosing
\varepsilon(\beta)
suitably, the above integral can be made to converge arbitrarily close to
\beta=(d+2)2\pi
, but not at that value. For
d=2
,
(d+2)2\pi=8\pi
is the KT-point. Therefore, it is at least in principle possible to extend our methods to the whole subcritical range
\beta\in(0,8\pi)
. However, for large enough
d
(in fact for
d\geq 5
),
(d+2)2\pi<\beta_{4}:=3d\pi
if we assume that the behavior remains scalable beyond the first collapse point, as explained in the introduction. Therefore, there is no hope of extending our methods even up to the second projected collapse point for all
d>2
.

We need one more lemma before we begin the actual proof.

Lemma 2.13

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be compact and
\varphi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then we have

\left|e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}-\mathbf{1}_{n\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0}\right|\leq\begin{cases}2([\sqrt{\beta t}\left\lVert\gradient\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\Lambda})}]\vee 1)\,H_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}),&\text{if }n\leq N,\\
1,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(2.41)

for all
(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\in\Lambda^{n}\times\{-1,1\}^{n}
. Here
B_{\Lambda}:=\overline{B_{2R(\Lambda)}(0)}
and
R(\Lambda)>0
is chosen so that
\Lambda\subset B_{R(\Lambda)}(0)
, and
H_{t}^{n}
is defined in
Equation

2.19
.

Proof.

First assume that
n\leq N
and that the configuration is neutral, that is,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0
. Without loss of generality, we may again assume that the first
n/2=:p
charges
\sigma_{i}
differ from the remaining ones. Then the left-hand side of the claim becomes

\begin{split}F(\varphi,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})&:=e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}-1=e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sigma_{j}(\varphi_{j}-\varphi_{p+\pi(j)})}-1,\end{split}

(2.42)

where
\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{p}
is a permutation, which we may choose to be the one realizing the minimum in the Wasserstein distance
d(\mathbf{x})
defined in
Equation

2.21
.

Using the basic estimate
|e^{ix}-1|=\left|\int_{0}^{x}e^{iy}\,\operatorname{d}\!y\right|\leq|x|
together with the triangle inequality and the mean value theorem, we obtain

\begin{split}|F(\varphi,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})|&\leq\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{p}|\varphi_{j}-\varphi_{p+\pi(j)}|\leq\sqrt{\beta}\left\lVert\nabla\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\Lambda})}\sum_{j=1}^{p}|x_{j}-x_{p+\pi(j)}|=\sqrt{\beta}\left\lVert\nabla\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\Lambda})}\,d(\mathbf{x}).\end{split}

(2.43)

We may also choose to apply this estimate only for those
\mathbf{x}\in\Lambda^{n}
satisfying
d(\mathbf{x})<\sqrt{t}
, and use the crude bound
|F(\varphi,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})|\leq 2
otherwise. Hence the final estimate becomes

\begin{split}|F(\varphi,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})|&\leq 2\bigl(\sqrt{\beta}\left\lVert\gradient\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\Lambda})}\sqrt{t}\bigr)^{\mathbf{1}_{\{d(\mathbf{x})\leq\sqrt{t}\}}}\left(\frac{d(\mathbf{x})}{\sqrt{t}}\wedge 1\right)\\
&\leq 2\bigl([\sqrt{\beta t}\left\lVert\gradient\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\Lambda})}]\vee 1\bigr)\,H_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\end{split}

(2.44)

Because of the
\vee 1
factor and the definition of
H_{t}^{n}
, the same estimate applies to all cases with
n\leq N
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
. The case
n>N
is trivial.
∎

Now we may begin the actual proof with the first statement.

Proof of
Theorem

2.1
1.

Recall the notation from
Equation

2.36

S_{t}^{R}(\beta,\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}[\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\varphi}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)+\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\infty}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)]

(2.45)

and note that there are only finitely many (
N
) non-zero
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\infty}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)
terms. Thus, the terms
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\varphi}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)
determine the convergence of the full series, and for the
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\infty}
terms we may use any uniform bound.
Corollary

2.10
provides such a bound. Indeed, we have

|\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\infty}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)|\leq\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{n}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\Lambda^{n}}F_{t}^{d,n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)\operatorname{d}\!x<C_{d}(\beta,t,N,\Lambda)<\infty

(2.46)

where the constant
C_{d}(\beta,t,N,\Lambda)>0
is independent of
m
and
\epsilon
, and where
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a compact set such that
\supp(\eta)\subset\Lambda\times\{-1,1\}
.

Thus, it remains to show that the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\varphi}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)
converges absolutely and uniformly in
m,\epsilon
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
t<m^{-2}
(at the end we take the minimum of the condition obtained in this way and
m^{-2}
). By
Lemmas

2.13
and
2.7
we have

\begin{split}|\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\varphi}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)|&\leq\frac{1}{n!}(2([\sqrt{\beta t}\left\lVert\gradient\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\Lambda})}]\vee 1)\mathbf{1}_{n\leq N}+\mathbf{1}_{n>N})\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}^{n}|\Lambda|2^{n}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\left\lVert H_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{n!}(2([\sqrt{\beta t}\left\lVert\gradient\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\Lambda})}]\vee 1)\mathbf{1}_{n\leq N}+\mathbf{1}_{n>N})|\Lambda|\frac{t^{-\frac{d}{2}}}{B_{d}(\beta,N)}[2\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}B_{d}(\beta,N)Ct^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}]^{n}n^{n-2}\\
&\leq(2([\sqrt{\beta t}\left\lVert\gradient\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\Lambda})}]\vee 1)\mathbf{1}_{n\leq N}+\mathbf{1}_{n>N})|\Lambda|\frac{t^{-\frac{d}{2}}}{B_{d}(\beta,N)}\left(\hat{B}(\beta,N)\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}t^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n},\end{split}

(2.47)

where
\hat{B}(\beta,N):=2eB_{d}(\beta,N)C
with
B_{d},C>0
from
Proposition

2.7
. Here we used the facts that
n^{-2}\leq 1
,
n^{n}/n!\leq e^{n}
, and
\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}1=2^{n}
. Then for

t<(\hat{B}(\beta,N)\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty})^{-\frac{1}{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}}=:\tilde{t}

(2.48)

we obtain a uniform geometric majorant.

Thus, for
t<t^{*}:=\min(\tilde{t},m^{-2})
the series

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\varphi}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)

(2.49)

converges absolutely and uniformly in
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
.
∎

Knowing the equivalence of the series
S_{t}
and the renormalized potential
V_{t}
would imply the equivalence of the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
with the cumulants in part 3 of this theorem, and vice versa. The proof of the former statement, without assuming the latter, would proceed through an infinite-dimensional PDE known as the Polchinski equation, while the proof of the latter, without assuming the former, can be carried out using a finite-dimensional system of ODEs. Both approaches can be traced back to
[
13
]
. We choose the latter approach and therefore prove the final claim of our theorem next.

Proof of
Theorem

2.1
3.

Consider first the moments corresponding to
\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}^{T}[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}\mid j\in[n]]
, where
\varphi_{j}=\varphi(x_{j})\equiv\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}(x_{j})
. Namely,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}\right]&=\epsilon^{-\frac{n\beta}{4\pi}}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}K_{\epsilon^{2},t}(k,l)}\\
&=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}]\right)e^{-\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\beta\sigma_{kl}K_{\epsilon^{2},t}(k,l)}\\
&=[\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(\epsilon)]^{n}\prod_{1\leq k<l\leq n}e^{-\beta\sigma_{kl}K_{\epsilon^{2},t}(k,l)}\\
&=[\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(\epsilon)]^{n}\prod_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\left(1+\left[e^{-\beta\sigma_{kl}K_{\epsilon^{2},t}(k,l)}-1\right]\right)\\
&=[\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(\epsilon)]^{n}\sum_{G\in\Gamma(n)}\prod_{e=(k,l)\in G}\left(e^{-\beta\sigma_{kl}K_{\epsilon^{2},t}(k,l)}-1\right),\end{split}

(2.50)

where
\Gamma(n)
is the set of all Mayer graphs on
[n]
, that is, all unordered pairings
(k,l)
with
k,l\in[n]
. Above we used the basic Gaussian identity
\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}[e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}]=e^{-\beta/2K_{\epsilon^{2},t}(0)}
and the definition
Equation

2.7
of
\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}
. It is not strictly necessary to factor out
(\tilde{V}_{t}^{1})^{n}
, but we do so in order to apply the results of
[
13
]
directly. The connected part of the sum above is called the Ursell coefficient or Ursell function
(e^{U([n],\epsilon,t)})_{c}
, where we denote

\displaystyle U([n],\epsilon,t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\beta\sigma_{kl}K_{\epsilon^{2},t}(k,l)

(2.51)

\displaystyle\left(e^{-U([n],\epsilon,t)}\right)_{c}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\sum_{G\in\Gamma_{c}(n)}\prod_{(k,l)\in G}\left(e^{-\beta\sigma_{kl}K_{\epsilon^{2},t}(k,l)}-1\right),&\text{if $n\geq 2$}\\
1,&\text{if $n=1$}.\end{cases}

(2.52)

Here
\Gamma_{c}(n)
denotes the set of all connected labeled graphs on
[n]
. We have suppressed the
m
-dependence in
U([n],\epsilon,t)
. We will also use an analogous definition for any
I\varsubsetneq[n]
.

The Ursell functions are known to represent the truncated correlation functions or cumulants. Thus, since we may factor out
[\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)]^{n}
from the cumulants in the representation
Equation

A.5
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}\mid j\in[n]\right]=[\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)]^{n}\left(e^{-U([n],\epsilon,t)}\right)_{c}.

(2.53)

We may likewise factor out
[\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)]^{n}
from
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)
by writing

\begin{split}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)&\,\,=[\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)]^{n}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\\
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)&:=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{t}\bigg\{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\beta\sigma_{ij}C_{s}^{m}(i,j)\right)\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m,\epsilon)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m,\epsilon)e^{-\beta\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}(k,l)}\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!s,&\text{if $n\geq 2$}\\
1,&\text{if $n=1$}.\end{cases}\end{split}

(2.54)

To prove this, we may use induction. The base case
