n=1
is immediate from the definition. For the induction step, it suffices to observe that

[\tilde{V}_{s}^{1}(m,\epsilon)]^{n}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sigma_{kk}K_{s,t}(k,k)}=\left[\tilde{V}_{s}^{1}(m,\epsilon)e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}K_{s,t}(0)}\right]^{n}=[\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)]^{n}.

(2.55)

Therefore, it remains to show that
(e^{-U([n],\epsilon,t)})_{c}=(-1)^{n-1}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)
. This follows from
[
13
, Lemma 3.3]
, but we highlight the main idea. The point is to derive a system of ODEs that both of these satisfy. We have by direct differentiation

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\beta\sigma_{ij}C_{t}^{m}(i,j)\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m,\epsilon)\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m,\epsilon)\\
\qquad\qquad-\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\beta\sigma_{kl}C_{t}^{m}(k,l)\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon),\qquad\text{if }n\geq 2\\
0,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{if }n=1.\end{cases}\end{split}

(2.56)

where we just used the Leibniz rule. Thus, for any
I\subset[n]
, the function
(-1)^{|I|-1}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m,\epsilon)
satisfies the system of ODEs

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}f_{t}(I,\mathbf{x}_{I})&=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{J\varsubsetneq I}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I\setminus J\end{subarray}}\beta\sigma_{ij}C_{t}^{m}(i,j)f_{t}(J,\mathbf{x}_{J})f_{t}(I\setminus J,\mathbf{x}_{I\setminus J})\\
&\qquad\qquad-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,l\in I\\
k<l\end{subarray}}\beta\sigma_{kl}C_{t}^{m}(k,l)f_{t}(I,\mathbf{x}_{I}),\qquad\text{if $|I|\geq 2$}\\
\frac{d}{dt}f_{t}(I,\mathbf{x}_{I})&=0,\qquad\text{if $|I|=1$}.\end{split}

(2.57)

We must show that the Ursell functions also satisfy the system
Equation

2.57
, and identify an initial condition shared by both
(-1)^{n-1}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{n}
and the Ursell functions that ensures uniqueness of the solution. The correct initial condition is

f_{\epsilon^{2}}(I,\mathbf{x}_{I})=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $|I|=1$}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2.58)

We refer to the proof of
[
13
, Lemma 3.3]
for the facts that the Ursell functions satisfy this system and that the solution is unique.
∎

Finally, we can prove the second statement.

Proof of
Theorem

2.1
2.

We will again fix
\beta
and choose
N(\beta)\equiv N
such that
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. In the following, the expectation will always be taken with respect to the law of
\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
. Formally, for deterministic (or equivalently independent of
\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
)
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we may write

-V_{t}(\beta,z\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)=\log(\E\abr{e^{-V_0(\beta,z\eta,\vp_{m,(\eps^2,t)}+\vp|\eps)}})=:\log(\E\abr{e^{\zeta Q(\eta,\vp_{m,(\eps^2,t)}+\vp|\eps)}}),

(2.59)

where

Q(\eta,\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}+\varphi|\epsilon)=\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta(x,\sigma)e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma(\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}(x)+\varphi(x))}\operatorname{d}\!x

(2.60)

and
\zeta=-z
.

For fixed
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
we have
|Q|\leq 2|\Lambda|\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}\epsilon^{-\beta/(4\pi)}
, where
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is compact and satisfies
\supp(\eta)\subset\Lambda\times\{-1,1\}
, so that
Q
is a deterministically bounded random variable. Thus,
\zeta\mapsto e^{\zeta Q}
is an entire function (pointwise in the probability space
\Omega
), and therefore
\zeta\mapsto\mathbb{E}[e^{\zeta Q}]
is also entire by Fubini’s and Morera’s theorems. Now choose
\zeta
such that
|\zeta Q|\ll 1
, say
|\zeta Q|<\varepsilon
for some very small number
\varepsilon
. Then for
|\zeta|=|z|\leq(2|\Lambda|\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}\epsilon^{-\beta/(4\pi)})^{-1}\varepsilon=:\delta(\varepsilon)
we have
|e^{\zeta Q}-1|\ll 1
. Thus,

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\zeta Q(\eta,\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}+\varphi|\epsilon)}\right]=1+\mathbb{E}[e^{\zeta Q(\eta,\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}+\varphi|\epsilon)}-1]\neq 0

(2.61)

for
\zeta\in B_{\delta(\varepsilon)}(0)
. Therefore, there exists a branch of the logarithm such that
V_{t}(\beta,z\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)
is analytic in
B_{\delta(\varepsilon)}(0)
for any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
the function
-V_{t}(\beta,-\zeta\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)
is the cumulant generating function

\begin{split}V_{t}(\beta,z\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)&=V_{t}(\beta,-\zeta\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)\\
&=-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\zeta^{n}}{n!}\kappa^{n}\left[Q(\eta,\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}+\varphi|\epsilon)\right]\\
&=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{n!}(-1)^{n-1}\kappa^{n}\left[Q(\eta,\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}+\varphi|\epsilon)\right],\end{split}

(2.62)

where
\kappa^{n}
denotes the
n^{\text{th}}
-order cumulant of the single random variable
Q
with respect to the law of
\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
. Then by boundedness and multilinearity we have

\kappa^{n}[Q(\eta,\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}+\varphi|\epsilon)]=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{dn}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}\right)e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}\mathbb{E}^{T}[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{k}\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}(x_{k})}\mid k\in[n]]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

(2.63)

By the proof of part 1, the terms

\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{nd}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}z\eta_{i}\right)e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

(2.64)

of the series
S_{t}(\beta,z\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)
have geometric majorants on the set

\{t\in[\epsilon^{2},\infty)\mid t<t^{*}\},

(2.65)

where

t^{*}:=\min\left(m^{-2},\left[\hat{B}(\beta,N)|z|\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}\right]^{-\frac{1}{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}}\right),

(2.66)

with
\hat{B}(\beta,N)=2eB_{d}(\beta,N)C
and the constants
B_{d}
and
C
from
Proposition

2.7
. For the terms
n\leq N+1
we can derive
\epsilon
- and
m
-dependent upper bounds by a similar iterative method as in the proof of
Proposition

2.7
. For fixed
t
such that
0<\epsilon^{2}\leq t<m^{-2}
we have that the series
S_{t}(\beta,z\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)
converges in
B_{R}(0)
with

R=(\hat{B}(\beta,N)t\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty})^{-\frac{1}{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}},

(2.67)

and represents an analytic function. Thus, by part 3,
V_{t}(\beta,z\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)=S_{t}(\beta,z\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)
on
\mathbb{R}\cap B_{R}(0)\cap B_{\delta(\varepsilon)}(0)
, and by analytic continuation we may extend the equivalence to all of
B_{R}(0)
. Therefore, choosing
t<t^{*}
with
t^{*}
defined in
Equation

2.66
ensures
1\in B_{R}(0)
, which yields the result.
∎

2.2
Proofs of
Lemmas

2.4
,
2.7
and
2.10

The first two subsections will introduce some preliminary estimates. Namely, we will first present the previously mentioned Onsager-type inequality established in
[
35
]
, and afterwards we will analyze the relevant sums of the covariance kernels
C_{s}
(the mass exponential can be factored out of all
C_{s}^{m}
and does not affect the estimates). The remainder of the section will then be devoted to the proofs of
Propositions

2.7
,
2.10
and
2.4
in this order.

2.2.1
Onsager-type inequality

The topic of this subsection is a tool from
[
35
]
, where the authors analyzed the boundary sine-Gordon model, which is equivalent to our setup with
d=1
, but with a more general covariance kernel in place of
C_{s}^{m}
. However, they did not explicitly consider the mass dependence or the limit
m\to 0
. This result is valid for arbitrary dimension
d
, although their other results are not.

Their parametrization of the problem uses a slightly different representation and regularization of the covariance of the reference field. More precisely, they used the regularized covariance kernels

\tilde{K}_{t}(x,y):=\int_{0}^{t}Q_{r}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!r,

(2.68)

where
Q_{r}
is a symmetric positive definite kernel such that
\tilde{K}_{t}
becomes a logarithmic covariance kernel in the limit
t\to\infty
. The prototype for such a kernel is the translation invariant case
Q_{r}(x,y)=q(e^{r}|x-y|)
with
q\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
. We are going to use the result for our massless kernel
C_{u}(x,y)
, which fits into this translation invariant framework. The result yields an estimate for the charge weighted sums of the quantities

\tilde{K}_{s,t}(x,y):=\int_{s}^{t}q(e^{r}|x-y|)\operatorname{d}\!r

(2.69)

The basic substitution
u=e^{-2r}
then yields, for our parametrization,

K_{s,t}(x,y):=\int_{s}^{t}C_{u}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!u=\int_{s}^{t}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4u}}\frac{\operatorname{d}\!u}{4\pi u}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\log(1/\sqrt{t})}^{\log(1/\sqrt{s})}q(e^{r}|x-y|)\operatorname{d}\!r

(2.70)

with
q(x)=e^{-x^{2}/4}
. This
q
certainly satisfies their assumptions.

Using the above relation we can translate the Onsager-type inequality (
[
35
, Lemma 4.3]
) to our setup. The parts 1. and 2. are valid for all
n\geq 1
, but the last part is valid only for
n\leq N+1
.

Lemma 2.14

(Onsager type inequality)

Given
n\geq 2
and
p\leq n/2
denoting the number of positive charges, there exists a constant
B_{n}
depending only on
n
such that

1.

If
n
is even and
p=n/2
, then

\begin{split}-\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}(k,l)&\leq\frac{1}{4\pi}(n-1)\log(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{s}})-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(\frac{d(\xx)}{\sqrt{t}}\wedge 1)+B_{n},\end{split}

(2.71)

where
d(\mathbf{x})
is the Wasserstein distance introduced in
Equation

2.21
.

2.

In all other cases we have

-\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}(k,l)\leq\frac{1}{4\pi}(n-1)\log(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{s}})+B_{n}.

(2.72)

3.

Furthermore, for
n\leq N+1
(the cases for which we need this result) we can write this using the functions
H_{t}^{n}
for all charge configurations simultaneously

\begin{split}-\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}(k,l)&\leq\frac{1}{4\pi}(n-1)\log(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{s}})-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(H_t^n(\xx,\Si))+B_{n}.\end{split}

(2.73)

2.2.2
Estimates for sums of covariance kernels

The first estimate in this section stems from
[
35
, pages 17–18]
, but we rewrite it in our parametrization and notation so that it can be used in our recursion
Equation

2.8
to prove
Proposition

2.7
. The second estimate is novel and is tailored to our setup.

First, let us pick a smooth monotonically decreasing decay function
f_{d}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
f_{d}(|x|)=\mathcal{O}((1+|x|)^{-(d+1)})
as
|x|\to\infty
and
f_{d}(|x|)\leq 1
for all
x
. The Gaussian obviously satisfies this in all dimensions, but we work with
f_{d}
for the first estimate since it also applies to more general kernels satisfying the derivative estimates below. Furthermore, we will continue to denote by
f_{d}
this decay function in the estimates later in this section even though it would be simply the Gaussian. In addition, we need the fact that for all
d\geq 1
there exist a function
f_{d}
as described above and satisfying

|(\partial_{y}^{\nu}\partial_{x}^{\mu}C_{s}^{m})(x,y)|=e^{-m^{2}s}|(\partial_{y}^{\nu}\partial_{x}^{\mu}C_{s})(x,y)|\leq C\frac{e^{-{m^{2}s}}}{s^{1+\frac{|\nu|+|\mu|}{2}}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x-y|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)

(2.74)

where
\mu
and
\nu
are multi-indices,
|\mu|
denotes the order of
\mu
, and
C>0
is a constant independent of
m,s,x
and
y
. The above can be proved by induction. In this section, we need it only for
|\nu|,|\mu|\in\{0,1\}
, which is similar to the condition for the kernels in
[
35
]
.

Let us define

G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}(i,j)\bigg|\Rightarrow e^{-m^{2}s}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}^{m}(i,j)\bigg|

(2.75)

where
I\subset[n]
, with
I^{c}
denoting the complement with respect to
[n]
, and we have used the shorthand notation from
Equation

1.24
, that is,
C_{s}(i,j):=C_{s}(x_{i},x_{j})
.

Now we can state the first estimate of this section

Lemma 2.15

For
s>0
we have

G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\leq\frac{C}{s}H_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})H_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right),

(2.76)

where
C>0
is a universal constant and
H_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
is the function defined in
Equation

2.19
.

Proof.

Let
p
be the number of positive charges in
I
and
p^{c}
the number of positive charges in
I^{c}
. The choice of positive rather than negative is arbitrary. If we denote
I=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{|I|}\}
and
I^{c}=\{j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{|I^{c}|}\}
, we may assume without loss of generality that the first
p
of the
\sigma_{i_{k}}
are positive, and analogously for
I^{c}
. Then, if
p=|I|/2
and
p^{c}=|I^{c}|/2
, we can write

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}(i,j)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\sum_{l=1}^{p^{c}}\sigma_{i_{k}j_{l}}(C_{s}(i_{k},j_{l})-C_{s}(i_{k},j_{p^{c}+l})-C_{s}(i_{p+k},j_{l})+C_{s}(i_{p+k},j_{p^{c}+l})).

(2.77)

If instead
p=|I|/2
but
p^{c}\neq|I^{c}|/2
(the reverse case is analogous), then we write

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}(i,j)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\sum_{l=1}^{|I^{c}|}\sigma_{i_{k}j_{l}}(C_{s}(i_{k},j_{l})-C_{s}(i_{p+k},j_{l})).

(2.78)

If
p\neq|I|/2
and
p^{c}\neq|I^{c}|/2
, we do not do any partial pairing, but use the estimate
Equation

2.81
.

We may always reorder the pairings of opposite charges so that we write
i_{p+\pi(k)}
instead of
i_{p+k}
, where
\pi
is a permutation on
I
. Moreover, we may choose
\pi
to be the permutation that realizes the minimum in the Wasserstein distance
d(\mathbf{x}_{I})
defined in
Equation

2.21
. The same can be done for
I^{c}
. For simplicity of notation, and without loss of generality, we assume that the minimizing permutation is the identity.

By a simple mean value theorem estimate (this can be reduced to a situation similar to the “second-order” mean value theorem on a square
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, treated for example in
[
44
, Theorem 9.40]
), we obtain

\begin{split}|C_{s}(i_{k},j_{l})-C_{s}(i_{k},j_{p^{c}+l})&-C_{s}(i_{p+k},j_{l})+C_{s}(i_{p+k},j_{p^{c}+l})|\\
&\leq\frac{C}{s}\frac{|x_{i_{k}}-x_{i_{p+k}}|}{\sqrt{s}}\frac{|x_{j_{l}}-x_{j_{p^{c}+l}}|}{\sqrt{s}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{k}\in[x_{i_{k}},x_{i_{p+k}}]\\
v_{l}\in[x_{j_{l}},x_{j_{p^{c}+l}}]\end{subarray}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|u_{k}-v_{l}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right),\end{split}

(2.79)

where
[a,b]
denotes the line segment between
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
C>0
is a universal constant. We also have
|x_{i_{k}}-x_{i_{p+k}}||x_{j_{l}}-x_{j_{p^{c}+l}}|\leq d(\mathbf{x}_{I})d(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}})
. If
d(\mathbf{x}_{I})\leq\sqrt{s}
, then also
|x_{i_{k}}-x_{i_{p+k}}|\leq\sqrt{s}
, so up to a universal multiplicative constant we may replace
u_{k}
by either endpoint of the segment
[x_{i_{k}},x_{i_{p+k}}]
. The same applies to
I^{c}
. Indeed, consider replacing
u_{k}
and
v_{l}
with
x_{i_{k}}
and
x_{j_{l}}

f_{d}\left(\frac{|u_{k}-v_{l}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)=f_{d}\left(\frac{|u_{k}-x_{i_{k}}+x_{i_{k}}-(v_{l}-x_{j_{l}}+x_{j_{l}})|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)\leq f_{d}\left(\left|\frac{|x_{i_{k}}-x_{j_{l}}|}{\sqrt{s}}-2\right|\right),

(2.80)

where we have used the fact that in this situation
|u_{k}-x_{i_{k}}|\leq|x_{i_{k}}-x_{i_{p+k}}|\leq\sqrt{s}
(similarly with
v_{l},x_{j_{l}}
and
x_{j_{p^{c}+l}}
), triangle inequality and monotonicity of
f_{d}
. Furthermore, there exists a constant
B
(possibly dependent on
d
) such that
f_{d}(|x-2|)\leq Bf_{d}(|x|)
.

We may symmetrize and use the above pairing estimate only on scales where
d(\mathbf{x}_{I}),d(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}})\leq\sqrt{s}
. If only one of these conditions holds, we apply the pairing estimate only to that set. If both fail, we use the naive bound

G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\leq\frac{C}{s}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)

(2.81)

for some universal constant
C>0
.

Thus, choosing the largest constant
C
among the possible cases, we may write, in the case
p=|I|/2
and
p^{c}=|I^{c}|/2
,

G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\leq\begin{cases}\frac{C}{s}\frac{d(\mathbf{x}_{I})}{\sqrt{s}}\frac{d(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}})}{\sqrt{s}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right),&\text{if }d(\mathbf{x}_{I}),d(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}})<\sqrt{s}\\
\frac{C}{s}\frac{d(\mathbf{x}_{I})}{\sqrt{s}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right),&\text{if }d(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}})\geq\sqrt{s},\text{but }d(\mathbf{x}_{I})<\sqrt{s}\\
\frac{C}{s}\frac{d(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}})}{\sqrt{s}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right),&\text{if }d(\mathbf{x}_{I})\geq\sqrt{s},\text{but }d(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}})<\sqrt{s}\\
\frac{C}{s}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2.82)

Similarly, if
p=|I|/2
but
p^{c}\neq|I^{c}|/2
(the opposite case is similar), we have

G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\leq\begin{cases}\frac{C}{s}\frac{d(\mathbf{x}_{I})}{\sqrt{s}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right),&\text{if }d(\mathbf{x}_{I})<\sqrt{s}\\
\frac{C}{s}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2.83)

If
\sum_{i\in I}\sigma_{i}\neq 0\neq\sum_{j\in I^{c}}\sigma_{j}
, we use the estimate
Equation

2.81
. Furthermore, if
|I|<N
and
|I^{c}|\geq N
(the opposite case is similar) we also choose to do the pairing argument above only with respect to
I
even though the configuration of
I^{c}
would also be neutral. Similarly, if
|I|,|I^{c}|\geq N
we may also choose to use the estimate
Equation

2.81
. Thus, we can rewrite our estimates in terms of the function
H_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
.
∎

The other estimate we need is given in the lemma below.

Lemma 2.16

If
s>0
and
n\leq N
, we have

\left|\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{s}(k,l)\right|=\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{s}(k,l)\leq n^{2}\frac{B}{s}H_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})=n^{2}\frac{B}{s}\begin{cases}\frac{d(\mathbf{x})}{\sqrt{s}},\quad\text{if }d(\mathbf{x})\leq\sqrt{s}\text{ and }\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0\\
1,\qquad\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2.84)

for some universal constant
B>0
. The first equality holds since
C_{s}
is a covariance function.

Furthermore, if in addition
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0
and
s
is bounded from below, that is,
s\geq t>0
for some fixed
t
and
\mathbf{x}\in\Lambda^{n}
with compact
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then we have

\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{s}(k,l)\leq n^{2}\frac{C(t,\Lambda)}{s^{\frac{3}{2}}}H_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})

(2.85)

with some finite constant
C(t,\Lambda)>0
. As before we use the shorthand notations in
Equation

1.24
.

Proof.

Firstly, if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\neq 0
, the claim follows readily from the fact that the sum has
n^{2}
terms and
C_{s}(\cdot,\cdot)\leq s^{-1}
so that in this case we may pick
B=1
.

Then for the rest of the proof we assume that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0
. We claim that the terms can be grouped so that there are
n
contributions of the form
C_{s}(0)-C_{s}(a,b_{a})
for
a=1,2,\dots,n
with
\sigma_{ab_{a}}=-1
, and
n^{2}/2-n
contributions of the form
\pm(C_{s}(a,b)-C_{s}(a,c))
(or either of the pairs (a,b) and (a,c) in reversed order) with
\sigma_{ab}\neq\sigma_{ac}
. In addition, for each such choice we require
|x_{a}-x_{b_{a}}|\leq d(\mathbf{x})
and
|x_{b}-x_{c}|\leq d(\mathbf{x})
.

Without loss of generality, we may again assume that
\sigma_{i}=+1
for
i=1,2,\dots,n/2=p
and
\sigma_{i}=-1
for
i=p+1,\dots,2p
. Let
\tau\in\mathbb{S}_{p}
be such that

d(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{p}|x_{i}-x_{p+\tau(i)}|.

(2.86)

The pairings can now be written explicitly. We write them for the case where
\tau
is the identity permutation, since in that case it is easiest to see that all pairs appear exactly once. The general case is obtained by replacing
p+i
with
p+\tau(i)
(and similarly with
j
) everywhere. Note that here we do not mod out by symmetries, so for instance both
C_{s}(a,b)
and
C_{s}(b,a)
will both appear in the sums below. We have

\begin{split}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}(i,j)&=\sum_{i=1}^{p}[C_{s}(0)-C_{s}(i+p,i)]+\sum_{i=1}^{p}[C_{s}(0)-C_{s}(i,i+p)]\\
&\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j\leq p\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}[C_{s}(i,j)-C_{s}(p+j,i)]\\
&\quad+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p}[C_{s}(i,p+j)-C_{s}(j,i)+C_{s}(j,p+i)-C_{s}(p+i,p+j)]\end{split}

(2.87)

The pairs
(a,b_{a})
are identified in the first row as the pairs
(i,p+i)
or
(p+i,i)
. The second and third row yield that the triplets
(a,b,c)
are either
(i,j,p+j)
or
(j,p+i,p+j)
.
Now the first claim follows by the same mean value theorem argument used in the proof of
Lemma

2.15
, together with the bound
f_{d}\leq 1
.

For the second claim, we now apply the mean value theorem explicitly since we cannot use the general decay function
f_{d}
here. We may write

C_{s}(x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi s}F\left(\frac{x-y}{\sqrt{s}}\right)

(2.88)

with
F(x)=e^{-\frac{1}{4}x^{2}}
. Since
F
is smooth, the mean value theorem gives

\begin{split}C_{s}(0)-C_{s}(a,b_{a})&=\frac{1}{4\pi s}\left(F(0)-F\left(\frac{x_{a}-x_{b_{a}}}{\sqrt{s}}\right)\right)=-\frac{1}{4\pi s}\frac{(x_{a}-x_{b_{a}})}{\sqrt{s}}\cdot(\gradient F)(u),\\
C_{s}(a,b)-C_{s}(a,c)&=\frac{1}{4\pi s}\left(F\left(\frac{x_{a}-x_{b}}{\sqrt{s}}\right)-F\left(\frac{x_{a}-x_{c}}{\sqrt{s}}\right)\right)=-\frac{1}{4\pi s}\frac{(x_{b}-x_{c})}{\sqrt{s}}\cdot(\gradient F)(v),\end{split}

(2.89)

for some
u\in[0,(x_{a}-x_{b_{a}})/\sqrt{s}]
and
v\in[(x_{a}-x_{b})/\sqrt{s},(x_{a}-x_{c})/\sqrt{s}]
.

Moreover,

(\gradient F)(u)=-\frac{u}{2}F(u)=-\frac{\tilde{u}}{2\sqrt{s}}F\left(\frac{\tilde{u}}{\sqrt{s}}\right)

(2.90)

for some
\tilde{u}\in[0,(x_{a}-x_{b_{a}})]
. For
d(\mathbf{x})<\sqrt{s}
we estimate

|C_{s}(0)-C_{s}(x_{a},x_{b_{a}})|\leq\frac{1}{s^{\frac{3}{2}}}\frac{d(\mathbf{x})}{\sqrt{s}}\sup_{x_{a},x_{b_{a}}\in\Lambda}\sup_{s\geq t}\sup_{\tilde{u}\in[0,(x_{a}-x_{b_{a}})]}\frac{|\tilde{u}|}{8\pi}F\left(\frac{\tilde{u}}{\sqrt{s}}\right).

(2.91)

For
d(\mathbf{x})\geq\sqrt{s}
we estimate

|C_{s}(0)-C_{s}(x_{a},x_{b_{a}})|\leq\frac{1}{s^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sup_{x_{a},x_{b_{a}}\in\Lambda}\sup_{s\geq t}\sup_{\tilde{u}\in[0,(x_{a}-x_{b_{a}})]}\frac{|x_{a}-x_{b_{a}}|}{\sqrt{s}}\frac{|\tilde{u}|}{8\pi}F\left(\frac{\tilde{u}}{\sqrt{s}}\right).

(2.92)

Both suprema above are finite, and the result follows by choosing
C(t,\Lambda)
to be their maximum; the remaining estimate is treated in a similar vein. For
d=1
the necessary modifications are straightforward (we simply use the ordinary derivative instead of the gradient).
∎

2.2.3
Proof of
Proposition

2.7
in the case
d\geq 2

We will begin this section by defining the functions
h_{t}^{n}
. The definition is the same in all dimensions, including
d=1
. Thus, until further notice we keep
d\geq 1
arbitrary. The functions
h_{t}^{n}
will be defined recursively, or inductively (in the case
t\geq m^{-2}
we do not even need the induction principle, since the number of terms is finite). After defining these functions we will prove
Proposition

2.7
in the case
d\geq 2
. This case is considerably simpler than the case
d=1
, which is why we treat it first.

Now we turn to defining the functions
h_{t}^{n}
. The base case
n=1
is obtained by simply choosing
h_{t}^{1}
to be the right-hand side of
Equation

2.12
in
Lemma

2.3
. Next we make the induction hypothesis that the claim holds for all
k<n
. Thus, for any
t>0
we have

|\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)|\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-m^{2}s}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}K_{s,t}^{m}(k,l)}\operatorname{d}\!s,

(2.93)

where we used the notation
G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=|\sum_{i\in I,\,j\in I^{c}}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}(i,j)|
from
Section

2.2.2
. Also recall from
Equation

1.24
that
K_{s,t}^{m}
is the integral of
C_{\cdot}^{m}
from
s
to
t
.

To make the functions
h_{t}^{n}
independent of
m
for
t<m^{-2}
we will need one more estimate before defining them. Namely, we need a good estimate for the last exponential factor involving the terms
K_{s,t}^{m}
above when
n\leq N+1
. This is provided in the lemma below.

Lemma 2.17

Let
n\leq N+1
and
t<m^{-2}
. Then we have

\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl} K_{s,t}^m(k,l))\leq\bar{B}_{N}\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}[H_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}},

(2.94)

where

\bar{B}_{N}:=\max_{n=1,2,\dots,N,N+1}\exp(\frac{n^2\beta}{8\pi}+\beta B_n).

(2.95)

and
B_{n}
is the constant from
Lemma

2.14
.

Proof.

We may write

\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl} K_{s,t}^m(k,l))=\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl}[K_{s,t}^m(k,l)-K_{s,t}(k,l)])\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}(k,l)).

(2.96)

We will apply the Onsager-type inequality from
Lemma

2.14
to the second factor, but let us first bound the first factor. We have

\begin{split}\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl}[K_{s,t}^m(k,l)-K_{s,t}(k,l)])&\leq\exp(\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\int_s^t|1-e^{-m^2r}|e^{-\frac{|x_k-x_l|^2}{4r}}\frac{\dr}{4\pi r})\\
&\leq\exp(\frac{\beta n^2m^2}{8\pi}\int_0^t\dr)\\
&\leq\exp(\frac{\beta n^2}{8\pi}),\end{split}

(2.97)

where the last step holds if
m^{2}t\leq 1
, that is,
t<m^{-2}
. The application of
Lemma

2.14
and
K_{s,t}(0):=K_{s,t}(x,x)=1/(4\pi)\log(t/s)
yields

\begin{split}\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}(k,l))&\leq\exp(-\frac{n\beta}{8\pi}\log(\frac{t}{s})+\frac{\beta(n-1)}{4\pi}\log(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{s}})-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}\log(H_t^n(\xx,\Si))+\beta B_n)\\
&=\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}[H_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{\beta B_{n}}.\end{split}

(2.98)

∎

It might be surprising that we need this estimate for the first
N+1
terms and not just the first
N
(unless
N=0
, in which case the
(N+1)
th term is covered by
Lemma

2.3
), but this is indeed the case.

Now we are ready to define the functions
h_{t}^{n}
. For
t<m^{-2}
and
2\leq n\leq N+1
we use
Lemma

2.17
for the exponential involving
K_{s,t}^{m}
terms in
Equation

2.93
and define

h_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=\frac{\beta}{2}\frac{\bar{B}_{N}[H_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}}{t^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{0}^{t}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\operatorname{d}\!s.

(2.99)

For
n>N+1
we define

h_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\operatorname{d}\!s.

(2.100)

Finally, for
t\geq m^{-2}
we only need to define the functions
h_{t}^{n}
for
n\leq N
and
d=1
. We will split the
s
-integral at
m^{-2}
. For the part where
s\geq m^{-2}
we bound the exponential involving the terms
K_{s,t}^{m}
by
1
. For the part where
s<m^{-2}
we use the following estimate

\begin{split}\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}^m(k,l))&=\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl}K_{s,m^{-2}}^m(k,l))\underbrace{\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl}K_{m^{-2},t}^m(k,l))}_{\leq 1}\\
&\leq\bar{B}_{N}[H_{m^{-2}}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}m^{\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}},\end{split}

(2.101)

where we estimated the first exponential factor in the same way as in the case
t<m^{-2}
, that is, we used
Lemma

2.17
. Thus, for
t\geq m^{-2}
define

\begin{split}h_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)&:=\frac{\beta}{2}\bar{B}_{N}[H_{m^{-2}}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}m^{\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{0}^{m^{-2}}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\quad+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{m^{-2}}^{t}e^{-m^{2}s}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\operatorname{d}\!s\end{split}

(2.102)

The permutation invariance in all cases is clear, because we sum over all ordered bipartite partitions of
[n]
. To obtain the norm estimates we need to treat the different values of
t
and
n
separately. The case
t\geq m^{-2}
is needed only in the case
d=1
as discussed in
Remark

2.8
.

For the rest of this subsection, we always have
d\geq 2
,
t<m^{-2}
and
N=0
or
N=2
. However, the first part, that is, the case
n>N+1
is similar also in
d=1
so we keep the notation
N
arbitrary for this part. This case is also sufficient in all dimensions when
N=0
, that is, when
\beta\in(0,2\pi d)
, since as mentioned above the case
N+1=1
is then covered by
Lemma

2.3
.

In the case
n>N+1
, we obtain

\begin{split}\left\lVert H_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n}&\leq\esssup_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{(n-1)d}}\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\operatorname{d}\!s\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{n-1}\\
&\leq\frac{\beta\tilde{C}}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{s}\bigg\{\esssup_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{(n-1)d}}\bigg[f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times H_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})H_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{n-1}\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!s,\end{split}

(2.103)

where we immediately used the fact that
H_{t}^{n}\equiv 1
in this case. We also used
Lemma

2.15
, and denoted the constant from it by
\tilde{C}
. Henceforth, we will always denote by
\tilde{C}
this constant in all similar estimates.

Next, consider only the part inside the
\{\}
-brackets and fix
I\varsubsetneq[n]
,
i\in I
and
j\in I^{c}
. Without loss of generality, assume that
1\in I
. We have

\begin{split}\esssup_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{(n-1)d}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)H_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})H_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{n-1}\\
&\leq\esssup_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\mathbf{1}_{i\neq 1}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)\operatorname{d}\!x_{j}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d(|I|-2)}}H_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in I\\
i\neq k\neq 1\end{subarray}}\operatorname{d}\!x_{k}\right)\operatorname{d}\!x_{i}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\esssup_{x_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d(|I^{c}|-1)}}H_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in I^{c}\\
j\neq l\end{subarray}}\operatorname{d}\!x_{l}\\
&\quad+\esssup_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbf{1}_{i=1}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)\operatorname{d}\!x_{j}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d(|I|-1)}}H_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in I\\
i\neq k\neq 1\end{subarray}}\operatorname{d}\!x_{k}\right)\operatorname{d}\!x_{i}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\esssup_{x_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d(|I^{c}|-1)}}H_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in I^{c}\\
j\neq l\end{subarray}}\operatorname{d}\!x_{l}.\end{split}

(2.104)

The remaining
x_{j}
integral yields, by simple scaling and translation, a factor proportional to
s^{d/2}
. Thus, we obtain the upper bound

C_{d}s^{\frac{d}{2}}\left\lVert H_{s}^{|I|}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})h_{s}^{|I|}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})\right\rVert_{|I|}\left\lVert H_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\right\rVert_{|I^{c}|},

(2.105)

with some constant
C_{d}>0
depending only on the dimension
d
. Henceforth, in all similar estimates we will denote by
C_{d}s^{\frac{d}{2}}
the contribution coming from the remaining integral when splitting the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{n}
into
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{|I|}
and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{|I^{c}|}
as above.

Using this and the induction hypothesis we obtain

\begin{split}\left\lVert H_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n}&\leq\frac{1}{2}\beta\tilde{C}C_{d}[B_{d}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}|I|^{|I|-2}|I^{c}|^{|I^{c}|-2}\\
&\quad\times\int_{0}^{t}s^{\frac{d}{2}-1}s^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(s^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{|I|}s^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(s^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{|I^{c}|}\operatorname{d}\!s.\\
\end{split}

(2.106)

The
s
-integral becomes

\int_{0}^{t}s^{-1-\frac{d}{2}+n\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s=\frac{1}{n-1}\frac{1}{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{n}{n-1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}t^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n}

(2.107)

if

-1-\frac{d}{2}+n\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)>-1\Leftrightarrow\beta<\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)4d\pi

(2.108)

for all
n>N+1
. The right-hand side is minimized by
n=N+2
, and since we assumed that
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
if
d=1
and
\beta\in[d2\pi,(d+1)2\pi)
for
d\geq 2
and
(d+1)2\pi\leq\beta_{4}
for
d\geq 2
, this condition is indeed satisfied. Note that here the choice not to use
Lemma

2.17
for the
n=N+1
term would fail.

Putting everything together yields

\begin{split}\left\lVert H_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n}&\leq\frac{\beta\tilde{C}C_{d}}{2(n-1)(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{n}{n-1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi})}[B_{d}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}t^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}|I|^{|I|-1}|I^{c}|^{|I^{c}|-1}\\
&\leq\beta\tilde{C}C_{d}\left(\sup_{n>N+1}{\frac{1}{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{n}{n-1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}}\right)[B_{d}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}t^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n}n^{n-2},\end{split}

(2.109)

where in the last step we used the combinatorial identity (see
[
13
, Lemma 4.2]
)

\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}|I|^{|I|-1}|I^{c}|^{|I^{c}|-1}=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\binom{n}{k}k^{k-1}(n-k)^{n-k-1}=2(n-1)n^{n-2}.

(2.110)

Estimate
Equation

2.109
is of the correct form if
B_{d}(\beta,N)
is chosen appropriately. Thus, choosing

B_{d}^{\prime}(\beta,N)=\beta\tilde{C}C_{d}\sup_{n>N+1}\frac{1}{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{n}{n-1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}

(2.111)

yields the desired result in the case
n>N+1
.

Remark 2.18
.

For the case
N=0
the above analysis indeed yields the bound
\beta<2d\pi
. Together with our later analysis this shows that the first transition or collapse point scales with the dimension
d
in the same way as the scaling from
d=1
to
d=2
, and this persists for all
d>2
.

Remark 2.19
.

We will make this type of inductive/recursive argument several times in
Section

2
, and the splitting of the spatial integrals at the inductive step together with the combinatorial part will always follow the same pattern. Thus, from now on we keep track only of the constants, the
t
or mass
m
dependence, and the convergence of the
s
-integrals.

If
N=2
we still need to consider the terms with
n=2
or
n=3
explicitly. First consider the
n=2
term. Proceeding as above, and using symmetry together with
Equation

2.12
for the
\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}
terms, we have

\left\lVert H_{t}^{2}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{t}^{2}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{\beta\bar{B}_{2}\tilde{C}}{t^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}}\int_{0}^{t}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}-1}(Cs^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}})^{2}\esssup_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)(H_{t}^{2}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}))^{1-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!x_{2}\operatorname{d}\!s.

(2.112)

For
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
the above is bounded by

\beta\bar{B}_{2}\tilde{C}C_{d}C^{2}t^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\int_{0}^{t}s^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}-1}\operatorname{d}\!s=\frac{\beta\bar{B}_{2}\tilde{C}C_{d}C^{2}}{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}t^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{2}

(2.113)

for all
\beta<4d\pi
and some constant
C_{d}>0
. This is of the expected form, noting that
n^{n-2}=1
for
n=2
.

Now let
\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}
. In this case we need to compute the
x_{2}
-integral (note that for
n=2
,
d(\mathbf{x})=|x_{1}-x_{2}|
):

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)\left(\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\sqrt{t}}\wedge 1\right)^{1-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!x_{2}&=t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\int_{|x|<\sqrt{t}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)|x|^{1-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!x+\int_{|x|\geq\sqrt{t}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)\operatorname{d}\!x\\
&\leq\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}s^{\frac{d}{2}}\int_{|x|\leq\sqrt{t/s}}f_{d}(|x|)|x|^{1-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!x+C_{d}s^{\frac{d}{2}}\\
&\leq C_{d}s^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right),\end{split}

(2.114)

where we first extended the region of integration of the second integral on the right-hand side of the first line to the whole space and then scaled to obtain the upper bound
s^{d/2}C_{d}
for some constant
C_{d}>0
. As
s
varies in
(0,t)
, the quantity
\sqrt{t/s}
ranges from
1
to
\infty
, and the last integral above is finite in the neighborhood of
\infty
for all
\beta>0
and in the neighborhood of the origin for all
\beta<(d+1)4\pi
. Therefore, we have bounded it by a constant, and again denoted the maximum of the constants above by
C_{d}
.

The contribution from the second term
C_{d}s^{d/2}
on the last row of
Equation

2.114
is treated exactly analogously to the whole contribution from the case
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
. Putting everything together for the first term, we obtain in this case

\beta\bar{B}_{2}\tilde{C}C_{d}C^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{0}^{t}s^{-1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}+\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!s=\frac{\beta\bar{B}_{2}\tilde{C}C_{d}}{\frac{d+1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}C^{2}t^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{2}

(2.115)

if
-1/2+d/2-\beta/(4\pi)>-1
, that is,
\beta<(d+1)2\pi
.
Thus, the
n=2
term satisfies the claim by choosing (note that the sum is always greater than either of the terms individually for appropriate values of
\beta
)

B_{d}(\beta,2)=\beta\bar{B}_{2}\tilde{C}C_{d}\left(\frac{1}{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+\frac{1}{\frac{d+1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\right).

(2.116)

Next consider the
n=3
term. Below we denote
I_{i}:=[3]\setminus\{i\}\equiv\{k,l\}
and use the symmetry of the kernels
C_{s}
everywhere to simplify the sums. By using the definition
Equation

2.99
first for
h_{t}^{3}
itself and then for
h_{s}^{2}
, and using
Lemma

2.15
,
Equation

2.12
and the fact that
H_{t}^{3}\equiv 1
, we obtain

\begin{split}\left\lVert H_{t}^{3}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{t}^{3}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{3}&\leq\esssup_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bigg\{\beta\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum_{j\in I_{i}\equiv\{k,l\}}\int_{0}^{t}\bigg[\tilde{C}H_{s}^{2}(\mathbf{x}_{I_{i}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I_{i}})\frac{1}{s}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)Cs^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\bar{B}_{2}\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\\
&\qquad\beta\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\tilde{C}}{r}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{k}-x_{l}|}{\sqrt{r}}\right)\bar{B}_{2}[H_{s}^{2}(\mathbf{x}_{I_{i}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I_{i}})]^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}(Cr^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}})\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!r\bigg]\operatorname{d}\!s\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!x_{2}\operatorname{d}\!x_{3}\\
&\leq\beta^{2}\bar{B}_{2}^{2}\tilde{C}^{2}C^{3}t^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum_{j\in\{k,l\}}\int_{0}^{t}s^{-1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\bigg\{\int_{0}^{s}r^{-1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\bigg[\\
&\qquad\times\esssup_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{k}-x_{l}|}{\sqrt{r}}\right)[H_{s}^{2}(\mathbf{x}_{I_{i}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I_{i}})]^{1-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!x_{1}\operatorname{d}\!x_{2}\bigg]\operatorname{d}\!r\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!s.\end{split}

(2.117)

If
\sigma_{k}=\sigma_{l}
, the spatial integrals decouple (say for
j=k
) by the changes of variables
x_{i}-x_{k}=x
and
x_{k}-x_{l}=y
. Scaling then yields the upper bound

\begin{split}6\beta^{2}\bar{B}_{2}^{2}\tilde{C}^{2}C_{d}^{2}C^{3}t^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\int_{0}^{t}s^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\int_{0}^{s}r^{\frac{d}{2}-1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!r\operatorname{d}\!s&=\frac{6\beta^{2}\bar{B}_{2}^{2}\tilde{C}^{2}C_{d}^{2}}{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}C^{3}t^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\int_{0}^{t}s^{d-1-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&=3\left(\frac{\beta\bar{B}_{2}\tilde{C}C_{d}}{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{2}C^{3}t^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{3},\end{split}

(2.118)

which is again of the correct form, since
n^{n-2}=3
for
n=3
.

For the case
\sigma_{k}\neq\sigma_{l}
we obtain the spatial integral (after the decoupling translations)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)\operatorname{d}\!x\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{d}\left(\frac{|y|}{\sqrt{r}}\right)\left(\frac{|y|}{\sqrt{s}}\wedge 1\right)^{1-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!y\leq s^{\frac{d}{2}}r^{\frac{d}{2}}C_{d}^{2}\left(\left[\frac{r}{s}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right),

(2.119)

where we used the estimate
Equation

2.114
from the
n=2
case above. The
+1
term on the right-hand side yields the same contribution as in the
\sigma_{k}=\sigma_{l}
case. Thus, it remains to check the
t
-behavior and convergence of the
r
- and
s
-integrals for the first term above. We have

t^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\int_{0}^{t}s^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{3}{2}}\int_{0}^{s}r^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!r\operatorname{d}\!s=\frac{t^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{3}}{2\left(\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)\left(\frac{d+1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}\right)}

(2.120)

again for
\beta<(d+1)2\pi
. Putting these together, we are done for the case
d\geq 2
if we choose

B_{d}(\beta,N):=\beta\tilde{C}C_{d}\max\left(\bar{B}_{2}\left(\frac{1}{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+\frac{1}{\frac{d+1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\right),\sup_{n>3}\frac{1}{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{n}{n-1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right).

(2.121)

2.2.4
Proof of
Proposition

2.7
in the case
d=1

We begin this section by proving the missing cases for estimates of the functions
h_{t}^{n}
for
d=1
. This includes the cases
t<m^{-2}
with
n\leq N+1
and
t\geq m^{-2}
with
n\leq N
. We consider the case
n\leq N+1
and
t<m^{-2}
first. Proceeding as in the case
n>N+1
we obtain by
Lemma

2.15

\begin{split}\left\lVert H_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n}&\leq\frac{\beta\bar{B}_{N}\tilde{C}}{2t^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{t}\bigg\{\\
&\quad\times\esssup_{x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\bigg[s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}-1}f_{1}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)\left(H_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right)^{1-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times H_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})H_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_{n-1}\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!s\end{split}

(2.122)

Since we are working with
d=1
and
\beta<4\pi
, we have
(H_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}))^{1-\beta/(4\pi)}\leq 1
. Therefore, repeating the analysis in the case
n>N+1
starting from
Equation

2.104
in the previous section we obtain

\begin{split}\left\lVert H_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n}&\leq\beta\bar{B}_{N}\tilde{C}C_{1}[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}t^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\int_{0}^{t}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}-\frac{3}{2}}\left(s^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\leq\frac{\beta\bar{B}_{N}\tilde{C}C_{1}}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}n^{n-2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n},\end{split}

(2.123)

provided that
-1+(n-1)(1/2-\beta/8\pi)>-1
, that is,
\beta<4\pi
.

Thus, this part satisfies the claim with the choice
B_{1}(\beta,N)=\bar{B}_{N}C_{1}\tilde{C}\beta(\frac{1}{2}-\beta/(8\pi))^{-1}
. The proof of the estimates for the functions
h_{t}^{n}
in the case
t<m^{-2}
for
d=1
is completed by choosing

B_{1}(\beta,N)=B_{1}^{\prime}(\beta,N):=\beta\tilde{C}C_{1}\max\left(\bar{B}_{N}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)^{-1},\sup_{n>N+1}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n}{n-1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)^{-1}\right).

(2.124)

Consider then the case
t\geq m^{-2}
. First we note that for
s\leq r
we have
H_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\geq H_{r}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
for all
n
. Therefore, we have

H_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})[H_{m^{-2}}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\leq[H_{m^{-2}}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]^{1-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\leq 1

(2.125)

since
\beta<4\pi
for
d=1
. We will then proceed in a similar vein to the proof of the
t<m^{-2}
case.

Then we use the definition
Equation

2.102
, and proceed as in the case
t<m^{-2}
using also
Equation

2.125
. More precisely, we first use
Lemma

2.15
and proceed analogously to
Equation

2.103
for both parts (
s\in(0,m^{-2})
and
s\in(m^{-2},t)
). After this, splitting the norms as in
Equation

2.104
, using the induction hypothesis and the combinatorial identity
Equation

2.110
yields

\begin{split}\left\lVert H_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n}&\leq\beta\tilde{C}C_{1}[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}\bigg(\bar{B}_{N}m^{\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\int_{0}^{m^{-2}}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}-\frac{3}{2}+n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(m^{-2})^{-1+n(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi})}\int_{m^{-2}}^{t}\frac{e^{-m^{2}s}}{\sqrt{s}}\operatorname{d}\!s\bigg)\\
&\leq\beta\tilde{C}C_{1}\left(\frac{\bar{B}_{N}}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+(N-1)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)\right)[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}n^{n-2}(m^{-2})^{-\frac{1}{2}+n(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi})},\end{split}

(2.126)

where
\Gamma(z,a):=\int_{a}^{\infty}s^{z-1}e^{-s}\operatorname{d}\!s
is the incomplete gamma function.

Thus, we choose

B_{1}(\beta,N)=\max\left(B_{1}^{\prime}(\beta,N),\beta\tilde{C}C_{1}\left[\frac{\bar{B}_{N}}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+(N-1)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)\right]\right),

(2.127)

where the constant
B_{1}^{\prime}(\beta,N)
came from the analysis of the
t<m^{-2}
case and was defined in
Equation

2.124
. This completes the proof of the
d=1
case for all
t>0
and thus the entire proof for the estimates of the
h_{t}^{n}
functions.

We will utilize the existence of the functions
h_{t}^{n}
and proceed with the functions
g_{t}^{n}
again inductively. The case
n=1
is clear by
Lemma

2.3
.
Indeed,

\partial_{m}\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)=\frac{\beta m}{4\pi}\underbrace{\left(\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{t}e^{-m^{2}s}\operatorname{d}\!s\right)}_{\leq(t\wedge m^{-2})}\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)\leq Cm(t\wedge m^{-2})^{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}},

(2.128)

for some universal constant
C>0
. Now assume that
\tilde{V}_{t}^{k}
is differentiable, and that the functions
\tilde{g}_{t}^{k}
with the claimed properties exist for all
k<n
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We then formally differentiate through the
s
-integral in the definition
Equation

2.8
of the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
(the proof of
Lemma

2.4
will justify this) to obtain

\begin{split}\frac{\partial}{\partial m}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)&=\beta m\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{t}\bigg\{\left(-s-\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{s}^{t}re^{-m^{2}r}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{r}(k,l)\operatorname{d}\!r\right)e^{-m^{2}s}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}(i,j)\bigg)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\tilde{V}_{t}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m,\epsilon)\tilde{V}_{t}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m,\epsilon)e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}^{m}(k,l)}\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\quad+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{t}\bigg\{e^{-m^{2}s}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}(i,j)\bigg)e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}^{m}(k,l)}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\big([\partial_{m}\tilde{V}_{t}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m,\epsilon)]\tilde{V}_{t}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m,\epsilon)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\tilde{V}_{t}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m,\epsilon)[\partial_{m}\tilde{V}_{t}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m,\epsilon)]\big)\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!s,\end{split}

(2.129)

where we used the shorthand
\partial_{m}:=\partial/\partial m
on the right-hand side

The most delicate term is the
r
-integral appearing inside the brackets of the first term. We begin by estimating this contribution. For
t<m^{-2}
we have by the first estimate of
Lemma

2.16
and
Lemma

2.17

\begin{split}\bigg(\int_{s}^{t}re^{-m^{2}r}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{r}(k,l)\operatorname{d}\!r\bigg)e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}^{m}(k,l)}&\leq\int_{0}^{t}r\left(\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{r}(k,l)\right)e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,r}^{m}(k,l)}\operatorname{d}\!r\\
&\leq\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}n^{2}\int_{s}^{t}\left(\frac{s}{r}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\underbrace{[H_{r}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]^{1-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}}_{\leq 1}\operatorname{d}\!r\\
&\leq\frac{\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}N^{2}}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}t^{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}},\end{split}

(2.130)

for some constant
\tilde{B}>0
. As with the other constants, we will henceforth denote the constant coming from the application of the first estimate of
Lemma

2.16
by
\tilde{B}
. We have also first estimated

\begin{split}\exp(-\tfrac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}^m(k,l))&=\exp(-\tfrac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl}K_{s,r}^m(k,l))\underbrace{\exp(-\tfrac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl}K_{r,t}^m(k,l))}_{\leq 1}\\
&\leq\exp(-\tfrac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^n\sigma_{kl}K_{s,r}^m(k,l))\end{split}

(2.131)

before applying
Lemma

2.17
.

For
t\geq m^{-2}\geq s
we obtain similarly

\begin{split}\bigg(\int_{s}^{t}r&e^{-m^{2}r}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{r}(k,l)\operatorname{d}\!r\bigg)e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}^{m}(k,l)}\\
&\leq\int_{s}^{m^{-2}}r\left(\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{r}(k,l)\right)e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,r}^{m}(k,l)}\operatorname{d}\!r\\
&\quad+\int_{m^{-2}}^{t}re^{-m^{2}r}\left(\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{r}(k,l)\right)e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,m^{-2}}^{m}(k,l)}\operatorname{d}\!r\\
&\leq\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}n^{2}\bigg(\int_{s}^{m^{-2}}\left(\frac{s}{r}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}e^{-m^{2}r}\underbrace{[H_{r}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]^{1-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}}_{\leq 1}\operatorname{d}\!r\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\int_{m^{-2}}^{t}\left(\frac{s}{m^{-2}}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}e^{-m^{2}r}\underbrace{H_{r}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})[H_{m^{-2}}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}}_{\leq 1\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:combining the Hn functions}}}\operatorname{d}\!r\bigg)\\
&\leq\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}N^{2}\left(\frac{1}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right)(m^{-2})^{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}.\end{split}

(2.132)

For
t\geq s\geq m^{-2}
we use

\bigg(\int_{s}^{t}re^{-m^{2}r}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{r}(k,l)\operatorname{d}\!r\bigg)e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}^{m}(k,l)}\leq n^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-m^{2}r}\operatorname{d}\!r\leq N^{2}m^{-2},

(2.133)

where we used
rC_{r}(x,y)\leq 1
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and
r>0
. Now we have all the preliminary ingredients to consider the functions
g_{t}^{n}
.

Applying the induction hypothesis and using the established existence of the functions
h_{t}^{n}
, we define the functions
g_{t}^{n}
recursively. First we have for
t<m^{-2}

\begin{split}g_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)&:=\beta m\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-m^{2}s}\left(s+\frac{\beta}{2}\frac{\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}N^{2}}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}t^{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\quad+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-m^{2}s}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times[g_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)+h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)g_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)]\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\,\,=:\sum_{i=1}^{4}g_{t}^{n,i}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m),\end{split}

(2.134)

where the
i=1
term corresponds to the
s
in the brackets on the first line,
i=2
corresponds to the remaining part, and the
i=3
and
i=4
terms are symmetric under
I\leftrightarrow I^{c}
, since the sum runs over all ordered bipartitions of
[n]
. If
t\geq m^{-2}
only the
i=2
term must be modified. In this case we define

\begin{split}g_{t}^{n,2}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)&:=\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}N^{2}\left(\frac{1}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right)m(m^{-2})^{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\!\!\times\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{0}^{m^{-2}}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}e^{-m^{2}s}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}N^{2}m^{-1}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{m^{-2}}^{t}e^{-m^{2}s}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)\operatorname{d}\!s\end{split}

(2.135)

Permutation invariance follows exactly as in the case of the functions
h_{t}^{n}
.

Let us begin the estimation with the case
t<m^{-2}
. Consider first the contribution from
g_{t}^{n,1}
. This can be estimated similarly to the case
n>N+1
for the functions
h_{t}^{n}
. More precisely, starting analogously to
Equation

2.103
(with
H_{t}^{n}\to 1
), using
Lemma

2.15
, proceeding in a similar vain as in the analysis from
Equation

2.104
onwards, and using the combinatorial identity
Equation

2.110
, we have

\begin{split}\left\lVert g_{t}^{n,1}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}&\leq 2\beta\tilde{C}C_{1}[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}m\int_{0}^{t}s^{-\frac{1}{2}+n(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi})}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\leq 2\tilde{C}C_{1}\beta\left(\sup_{n\leq N}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n}{n+1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}n^{n-2}mt^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n},\end{split}

(2.136)

where the
s
-integral converges for all
\beta<(1+1/n)4\pi
. The last estimate on the right-hand side is of the correct form.

For
g_{t}^{n,2}
we obtain by proceeding similarly as with
g_{t}^{n,1}
above

\begin{split}\left\lVert g_{t}^{n,2}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}&\leq\frac{\beta^{2}\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}\tilde{C}C_{1}n^{2}}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}mt^{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\int_{0}^{t}s^{-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\left(s^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\leq\frac{\beta^{2}\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}\tilde{C}C_{1}N^{2}}{\left(1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}n^{n-2}mt^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n},\end{split}

(2.137)

for all
\beta<4\pi
. Again, we obtained an estimate of the correct form.

Finally, as already mentioned, by symmetry we only need to treat
g_{t}^{n,3}
of the remaining terms. We proceed similarly to the above, noting that after the step analogous to
Equation

2.104
, the term corresponding to
I
will be
\left\lVert g_{t}^{|I|}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})\right\rVert_{|I|}
(noting that
H_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})\leq 1
) and we need to apply the induction hypothesis to it. Thus, we obtain

\begin{split}\left\lVert g_{t}^{n,3}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}&\leq\beta\tilde{C}C_{1}[\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N)]^{|I|-1}[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{|I^{c}|-1}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}m\int_{0}^{t}s^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(s^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\leq\beta\tilde{C}C_{1}\left(\sup_{n\leq N}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n}{n+1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)\max\left(\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N),B_{1}(\beta,N)\right)^{n-2}C^{n}n^{n-2}mt^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(t^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n}\end{split}

(2.138)

The last estimate above is again of the correct form.
Thus, choosing

\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N):=\tilde{B}_{1}^{\prime}(\beta,N):=\max\left(B_{1}(\beta,N),4\beta\tilde{C}C_{1}\sup_{n\leq N}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n}{n+1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+\frac{\beta^{2}\tilde{C}C_{1}\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}N^{2}}{\left(1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\right),

(2.139)

where
B_{1}(\beta,N)
is the constant from the estimates for the
h_{t}^{n}
functions, yields the result for
t<m^{-2}
.

Consider then the case
t\geq m^{-2}
. In this case we need to split the
s
-integral at
m^{-2}
; otherwise the treatment is similar to the above, with appropriate modifications to the term with
s>m^{-2}
. First, we have

\begin{split}\left\lVert g_{t}^{n,1}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}&\leq 2\beta\tilde{C}C_{1}[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}\\
&\quad\times m\left(\int_{0}^{m^{-2}}s^{-\frac{1}{2}+n(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi})}\operatorname{d}\!s+(m^{-2})^{-1+n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\int_{m^{-2}}^{t}s^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-m^{2}s}\operatorname{d}\!s\right)\\
&\leq 2C_{1}\tilde{C}\beta\left(\sup_{n\leq N}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n}{n+1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+(N-1)\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2},1\right)\right)[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}n^{n-2}(m^{-2})^{n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}.\end{split}

(2.140)

The second case yields

\begin{split}\left\lVert g_{t}^{n,2}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}&\leq\beta^{2}\tilde{C}C_{1}N^{2}[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}\\
&\quad\times\bigg(\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}\left(\frac{1}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right)m(m^{-2})^{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\int_{0}^{m^{-2}}s^{-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}s^{n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad+m(m^{-2})(m^{-2})^{-1+n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\int_{m^{-2}}^{t}\frac{e^{-m^{2}s}}{\sqrt{s}}\operatorname{d}\!s\bigg)\\
&\leq\beta^{2}\tilde{C}C_{1}N^{2}\left(\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}\left(\frac{1}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right)\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+(N-1)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)\right)\\
&\quad\times[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}C^{n}n^{n-2}(m^{-2})^{n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\end{split}

(2.141)

Finally, we have for the last case

\begin{split}\left\lVert g_{t}^{n,3}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}&\leq\beta\tilde{C}C_{1}[\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N)]^{|I|-1}[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{|I^{c}|-1}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}\\
&\quad\times\left(m\int_{0}^{m^{-2}}s^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(s^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n}\operatorname{d}\!s+m(m^{-2})^{n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\int_{m^{-2}}^{t}\frac{e^{-m^{2}s}}{\sqrt{s}}\operatorname{d}\!s\right)\\
&\leq\beta\tilde{C}C_{1}\left(\sup_{n\leq N}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n}{n+1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+(N-1)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)\right)\\
&\quad\times[\max(\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N),B_{1}(\beta,N))]^{n-2}C^{n}n^{n-2}(m^{-2})^{n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}.\end{split}

(2.142)

All of the above estimates are of the correct form. Thus, the result follows by choosing
\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N)
to be the maximum of
\tilde{B}_{1}^{\prime}(\beta,N)
(the constant from the
t<m^{-2}
case defined in
Equation

2.139
) and

\begin{split}\beta\tilde{C}C_{1}&\bigg[4\sup_{n\leq N}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n}{n+1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+\beta\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}N^{2}\left(\frac{1}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right)\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\\
&\quad+2(N-1)\left(\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2},1\right)+\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)\right)+N^{2}(N-1)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)\bigg]\end{split}

(2.143)

2.2.5
Proof of
Corollary

2.10
for the case
d=1

Recall that the case
d\geq 2
was discussed after the statement of
Corollary

2.10
. Thus, here we only need to prove the case
d=1
. We first establish
Equations

2.28
and
2.29
, which shows that, if the limit
Equation

2.32
exists, it is finite as mentioned in
Remark

2.11
. We then derive an explicit expression for the error term in
R_{t}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
in the decomposition in
Equation

2.33
, and finally we prove that the appropriate integral of
|R_{t}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})|
vanishes in the limit
m\to 0
.

First, note that

\begin{split}\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)&=\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&=\int_{t}^{\infty}\bigg[\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}^{m}(i,j)\bigg)\tilde{V}_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m,\epsilon)\tilde{V}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m,\epsilon)\\
&\qquad\quad-\frac{\beta}{2}\bigg(\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{s}^{m}(k,l)\bigg)\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\bigg]\operatorname{d}\!s.\end{split}

(2.144)

We then use the majorants
h_{t}^{n}
from
Proposition

2.7
together with the notation from
Equation

2.75
to obtain

\begin{split}|\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)|&\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-m^{2}s}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\quad+\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-m^{2}s}\left(\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{s}(k,l)\right)h_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&=:F_{t}^{1,n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m).\end{split}

(2.145)

We now derive upper bounds for the terms above separately.

Consider the contribution from the first term. We may assume that
t<m^{-2}
since if
t\geq m^{-2}
we may replace the lower bound
t
with
m^{-2}
by positivity, in which case we only have the second term below. We again use
Lemma

2.15
, split the
s
-integral at
m^{-2}
, and treat the spatial integrals as in
Equation

2.104
. However, for the term with
s\in(t,m^{-2})
, instead of scaling the integral corresponding to
x_{j}
in this step, we need to use

\int_{\Lambda}\underbrace{f_{d}\left(\frac{|x_{j}-x_{i}|}{\sqrt{s}}\right)}_{\leq 1}\operatorname{d}\!x_{j}\leq|\Lambda|.

(2.146)

The term with
s\in(m^{-2},\infty)
is treated as the terms with
s\in(m^{-2},t)
in
Equation

2.126
.
Thus, we have

\begin{split}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\Lambda^{n}}\frac{\beta}{2}&\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-m^{2}s}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)\operatorname{d}\!s\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&\leq\beta\tilde{C}[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}2^{n}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}|\Lambda|\\
&\quad\times\bigg(|\Lambda|\int_{t}^{m^{-2}}s^{-2}\left(s^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\right)^{n}\operatorname{d}\!s+C_{1}(m^{-2})^{-1}(m^{-2})^{n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\int_{m^{-2}}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-m^{2}s}}{\sqrt{s}}\operatorname{d}\!s\bigg)\\
&\leq\beta\tilde{C}[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}2^{n}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}|\Lambda|\\
&\quad\times\left(|\Lambda|\frac{m^{n\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+2}}{(n-2)\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n\beta}{8\pi}}+C_{1}m^{n\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+1}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)\right).\end{split}

(2.147)

Here, the factor
2^{n}
accounts for the number of terms in the
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
-sum. The first factor
|\Lambda|
arises from replacing the
x_{1}
-integral by a supremum, which allows us to use the norm estimates from
Proposition

2.7
for the functions
h_{t}^{n}
. Note that if we did not use the estimate
Equation

2.146
and proceeded as before, the exponent of
s
would be
-3/2+n(1/2-\beta/(8\pi))
. Although such an integral would still be convergent, it would lead to a logarithmic dependence on the mass in the case
\beta=\beta_{N}
and
n=N
, which is unbounded for
m\in(0,1)
. For the second
s
-integral after the first inequality in
Equation

2.147
, we can scale the integral so that it becomes independent of the mass
m
. In this case, the
\beta=\beta_{N}
and
n=N
situation corresponds to the mass exponent being zero in the second term on the last line of
Equation

2.147
, which remains bounded for
m\in(0,1)
.

Consequently, we obtain a uniform (in
m
) upper bound for this term provided that the exponents of
m
in the last line of
Equation

2.147
are non-negative. This condition yields the constraints

\beta\geq\left(1-\frac{2}{n}\right)4\pi\qquad\text{and}\qquad\beta\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)4\pi,\quad\text{for all }n\leq N,

(2.148)

which are satisfied under the assumption
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
.

For the second term, we obtain using
Propositions

2.7
and
2.16
and similar manipulations as with the first term

\begin{split}&\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\Lambda^{n}}\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-m^{2}s}\left(\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{s}(k,l)\right)h_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)\operatorname{d}\!s\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&\quad\leq[B_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-1}2^{n}C^{n}n^{n}|\Lambda|\bigg(C(t,\Lambda)\int_{t}^{m^{-2}}s^{-2+n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s+\tilde{B}(m^{-2})^{(n-1)\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n\beta}{8\pi}}\int_{m^{-2}}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-m^{2}s}}{s}\operatorname{d}\!s\bigg).\end{split}

(2.149)

Here, we needed to use the second claim of
Lemma

2.16
for the part where
s\in(t,m^{-2})
. This provides the extra
s^{-1/2}
-factor, which makes the resulting mass exponents non-negative, but again enforces a stronger dependence on
\Lambda
. That is, the constant
C(t,\Lambda)
plays the role of the second
|\Lambda|
-factor in the analysis of the first term above. For the part with
s\in(m^{-2},\infty)
it sufficed to use the first claim of
Lemma

2.16
. The first term on the right-hand side of
Equation

2.149
has the same
s
-integral as the first term in the second row of
Equation

2.147
and the second
s
-integral in
Equation

2.149
is independent of mass by scaling. Thus, we obtain the same constraints for
\beta
as in
Equation

2.148
.

Remark 2.20
.

The strong
\Lambda
-dependence in the above estimates is caused by taking the limit
m\to 0
. Thus, the situation reflects a trade-off between keeping the mass non-zero and allowing stronger dependence of the estimates on the finite volume. Both mechanisms effectively act as an IR-cutoff.

Since the precise form of the bound is unimportant, by maximizing all of the above estimates over
n\leq N
, we conclude that

\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\Lambda^{n}}F_{t}^{1,n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq C(\beta,t,N,\Lambda)<\infty

(2.150)

for all
n\leq N
and some constant
C(\beta,t,N,\Lambda)>0
that is independent of
m
. This proves
Equation

2.29
.

The majorant in the above estimate is independent of
\epsilon
, so the limit
\epsilon\to 0
can be taken through the spatial integrals, and the pointwise limit

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}(\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon))=:\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)

(2.151)

exists as explained in
Remark

2.6
. Thus, we turn to deriving a useful representation for the error function
R_{t}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
such that

\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)=\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})+R_{t}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}),

(2.152)

where
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)
for
t\in(0,\infty]
and these limits again exist by
Remark

2.6
. Furthermore, by
Lemma

2.4
we also have
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m=0,\epsilon=0)
.

First we can write

\begin{split}\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)&-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)\\
&=\int_{t}^{\infty}\bigg\{\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}(i,j)\bigg)\left(\tilde{V}_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})-\left[\tilde{V}_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})-e^{-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}s}\tilde{V}_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)\right]\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad\times\left(\tilde{V}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})-\left[\tilde{V}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})-e^{-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}s}\tilde{V}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)\right]\right)\bigg]\\
&\qquad-\frac{\beta}{2}\bigg(\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{s}(k,l)\bigg)\left(\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\left[\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})-e^{-m^{2}s}\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)\right]\right)\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&=\int_{t}^{\infty}\bigg\{\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}(i,j)\bigg)\tilde{V}_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})\tilde{V}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\bigg]\\
&\qquad\quad-\frac{\beta}{2}\left(\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{s}(k,l)\right)\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!s+R_{t}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\\
&=\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})+R_{t}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}),\end{split}

(2.153)

where the error term can be written as

\begin{split}R_{t}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})&:=-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{t}^{\infty}\bigg\{\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in I^{c}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{ij}C_{s}(i,j)\bigg)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times\bigg(\left[\tilde{W}_{s}^{|I|}(1/2,\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})-\tilde{W}_{s}^{|I|}(1/2,\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)\right]\tilde{W}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(1/2,\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\tilde{W}_{s}^{|I|}(1/2,\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)\left[\tilde{W}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(1/2,\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})-\tilde{W}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(1/2,\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)\right]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left[\tilde{W}_{s}^{|I|}(1/2,\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})-\tilde{W}_{s}^{|I|}(1/2,\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)\right]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times\left[\tilde{W}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(1/2,\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})-\tilde{W}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(1/2,\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)\right]\bigg)\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\qquad+\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{t}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{s}(k,l)\right)\left[\tilde{W}_{s}^{n}(1,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)-\tilde{W}_{s}^{n}(1,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right]\operatorname{d}\!s.\end{split}

(2.154)

Here
\tilde{W}_{s}^{n}(a,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m):=e^{-am^{2}s}\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)
and

\tilde{W}_{s}^{n}(a,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=\tilde{W}_{s}^{n}(a,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m=0)\equiv\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
for any
a>0
.

The final goal is to prove
Equation

2.34
. For this we need good estimates for the error term.
First we can write

\begin{split}\tilde{W}_{s}^{n}(a,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)-\tilde{W}_{s}^{n}(a,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})&=\int_{0}^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\tilde{W}_{s}^{n}(a,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu\\
&=-2as\int_{0}^{m}\mu e^{-a\mu^{2}s}\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu+\int_{0}^{m}e^{-a\mu^{2}s}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\tilde{V}_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu,\end{split}

(2.155)

which is justified by
Lemma

2.4
.

Then, we use
Equation

2.155
and the majorant functions
h_{t}^{n}
and
g_{t}^{n}
together with their estimates from
Proposition

2.7
, to bound
|R_{t}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})|
.

Thus, we first obtain

\begin{split}|R_{t}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})|&\leq\frac{1}{2}\beta\int_{0}^{m}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{t}^{\infty}\!\!G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})e^{-\frac{1}{2}(m^{2}+\mu^{2})s}\bigg\{\left[\mu sh_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|\mu)+g_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|\mu)\right]h_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|m)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+h_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|m)\left[\mu sh_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|\mu)+g_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|\mu)\right]\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!s\,\mathrm{d}\mu\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\beta\int_{0}^{m}\int_{0}^{m}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{t}^{\infty}\!\!G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\mu^{2}+\lambda^{2})s}\bigg\{\left[\lambda sh_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|\lambda)+g_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I}|\lambda)\right]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left[\mu sh_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|\mu)+g_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}}|\mu)\right]\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!s\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\beta\int_{0}^{m}\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-\mu^{2}s}\left(\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{s}(k,l)\right)\left[2\mu sh_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|\mu)+g_{s}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|\mu)\right]\operatorname{d}\!s\,\mathrm{d}\mu\\
&=:\int_{0}^{m}\!\![R_{1}(m,\mu,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})+R_{2}(m,\mu,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]\,\mathrm{d}\mu+\int_{0}^{m}\int_{0}^{m}\!\!R_{3}(\mu,\lambda,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+\int_{0}^{m}\!\!R_{4}(\mu,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\,\mathrm{d}\mu,\end{split}

(2.156)

where we have suppressed the
t
and
n
dependence from the terms
R_{i}
,
i=1,2,3,4
, since these will not be used elsewhere with different values, unlike the full error term
R_{t}^{n}
. Furthermore, the exchange of the mass scale integrals with respect to
\mu
and
\lambda
, and the
s
-integrals above, as well as the exchange of any of these with the spatial integrals, is justified since everything is positive. We will always evaluate the mass scale integrals last. Furthermore, without loss of generality we assume that
t<m^{-2},\mu^{-2},\lambda^{-2}
, since otherwise some of the terms simply would not appear and the task would be simpler.

Consider the
R_{4}
term first, since it involves only one mass scale. Using
Proposition

2.7
, the second claim of
Lemma

2.16
, splitting the
s
-integral at the mass scale
\mu^{-2}
and using
e^{-\mu^{2}s}\leq 1
for the part with
s\in(t,\mu^{-2})
, we obtain

\begin{split}&\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\Lambda^{n}}R_{4}(\mu,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\operatorname{d}\!x\\
&\quad\leq\frac{1}{2}\beta C(t,\Lambda)|\Lambda|[\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}2^{n}C^{n}n^{n}\bigg[3\mu\int_{t}^{\mu^{-2}}s^{-1+n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+2\mu(\mu^{-2})^{(n-1)\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n\beta}{8\pi}}\int_{\mu^{-2}}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\mu^{2}s}}{\sqrt{s}}\operatorname{d}\!s+(\mu^{-2})^{n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\int_{\mu^{-2}}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\mu^{2}s}}{s^{\frac{3}{2}}}\operatorname{d}\!s\bigg]\\
&\quad\leq\frac{1}{2}\beta C(t,\Lambda)|\Lambda|[\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}2^{n}C^{n}n^{n}\left(\frac{3}{n}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+2\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)+\Gamma\left(-\frac{1}{2},1\right)\right)\mu^{n\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+1},\end{split}

(2.157)

where we used
\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N)\geq B_{1}(\beta,N)
by definition. The factor
|\Lambda|
