again came from taking a supremum over
x_{1}
and
2^{n}
is just the number of terms in the
\sigma
-sum. Noting that
0\leq\mu\leq m<1
we find that

\mu^{n(\beta/(4\pi)-1)+1}\leq 1
provided that

n\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+1\geq 0\;\;\Leftrightarrow\;\;\beta\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)4\pi,

(2.158)

which is satisfied for all
n\leq N
under the assumption
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. Hence, after using this, the integral
\int_{0}^{m}\,\mathrm{d}\mu
only produces an overall factor
m
, which forces this contribution to vanish as
m\to 0
. Note that here we had to make the trade-off between non-zero mass and stronger dependence on the compact set
\Lambda
, as discussed in
Remark

2.20
, for all terms.

Consider next the terms with
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
. These are symmetric under
I\leftrightarrow I^{c}
, so we only estimate the term with
R_{1}
. We can again use
Propositions

2.7
and
2.15
. Here we split the
s
-integral at both mass scales
m^{-2}
and
\mu^{-2}
. We will also use the estimate
Equation

2.146
at the step analogous to
Equation

2.104
, for all terms. This produces again the second factor of
|\Lambda|
. Thus, we obtain

\begin{split}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}&\int_{\Lambda^{n}}R_{1}(m,\mu,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2}\beta\tilde{C}[\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}2^{n}C^{n}|\Lambda|^{2}\bigg\{4(n-1)n^{n-2}\underbrace{\mu\int_{t}^{m^{-2}}s^{-1+n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s}_{I_{1}(\mu,m)}\\
&\quad+\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}|I|^{|I|-1}|I^{c}|^{|I^{c}|-1}\bigg[2\underbrace{\mu(m^{-2})^{-\frac{1}{2}+|I^{c}|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\int_{m^{-2}}^{\mu^{-2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(m^{2}+\mu^{2})s}s^{-\frac{1}{2}+|I|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s}_{=:I_{2}(\mu,m)}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\underbrace{\mu(m^{-2})^{-\frac{1}{2}+|I^{c}|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}(\mu^{-2})^{-\frac{1}{2}+|I|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\int_{\mu^{-2}}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(m^{2}+\mu^{2})s}\operatorname{d}\!s}_{=:I_{3}(\mu,m)}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\underbrace{(m^{-2})^{-\frac{1}{2}+|I^{c}|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}(\mu^{-2})^{|I|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\int_{\mu^{-2}}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(m^{2}+\mu^{2})s}}{s}\operatorname{d}\!s}_{=:I_{4}(\mu,m)}\bigg]\bigg\},\end{split}

(2.159)

where we have used
e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\mu^{2}+m^{2})s}\leq 1
for the term
I_{1}(\mu,m)
and combined the contributions coming from
h_{s}^{|I|}(\cdot,\cdot|\mu)
and
g_{s}^{|I|}(\cdot,\cdot|\mu)
on the scales
s<\mu^{-2}
, where their
\mu
and
s
dependence is the same (we will also do this later with the term involving
R_{3}
).

Since we are only interested in the dependence on
m
and
\mu
as
m\to 0
, we only estimate the
s
-integrals and
\mu
-integrals separately for each term with appropriate prefactors depending on
m
, and ignore all the constants and combinatorial factors. Thus, our task reduces to showing that

\int_{0}^{m}I_{i}(\mu,m)\,\mathrm{d}\mu\overset{m\to 0}{\longrightarrow}0

(2.160)

for all
i=1,2,3,4
.

For the first term the
\mu
- and
s
-integral decouple, and we obtain

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{m}I_{1}(\mu,m)\,\mathrm{d}\mu&\leq Bm^{2}(m^{-2})^{n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\leq Bm^{n\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+2}\overset{m\to 0}{\longrightarrow}0\end{split}

(2.161)

for all
\beta>(1-2/n)4\pi
, which is again satisfied for all
n\leq N
by the assumption
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. The constant
B>0
is independent of
m
and
\mu
, and it is allowed to change from line to line (we will continue this convention in future estimates).

For the second term we obtain

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{m}I_{2}(\mu,m)\,\mathrm{d}\mu&=Bm^{|I^{c}|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+1}\int_{0}^{m}\mu[(m^{2}+\mu^{2})^{-1}]^{\frac{1}{2}+|I|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\int_{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{m^{2}}{\mu^{2}}}e^{-u}u^{-\frac{1}{2}+|I|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!u\,\mathrm{d}\mu\\
&\leq Bm^{|I^{c}|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+1}\int_{0}^{m}\underbrace{\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{\mu^{2}+m^{2}}}}_{\leq 1}\underbrace{\left(\frac{1}{m^{2}+\mu^{2}}\right)^{|I|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}}_{\leq(m^{-2})^{|I|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\\
&\leq B\underbrace{m^{n\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+1}}_{\leq 1}\int_{0}^{m}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\overset{m\to 0}{\longrightarrow}0,\end{split}

(2.162)

where the
u
-integral is bounded uniformly in
m
and
\mu
. The exponent of the mass term before the integral on the last line is non-negative for
\beta\geq(1-1/n)4\pi
and this is guaranteed for all
n\leq N
by the assumption
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
.

The third term yields

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{m}I_{3}(\mu,m)\,\mathrm{d}\mu&\leq Bm^{|I^{c}|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+1}\int_{0}^{m}\mu^{|I|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+2}\underbrace{(\mu^{2}+m^{2})^{-1}}_{\leq m^{-2}}\underbrace{\int_{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{m^{2}}{\mu^{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-u}\operatorname{d}\!u}_{\leq 1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\\
&\leq Bm^{n\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+2}\overset{m\to 0}{\longrightarrow}0\end{split}

(2.163)

where the last
\mu
-integral, after using the estimates written below the terms, is convergent for
\beta>(1-3/|I|)4\pi
, and the exponent of the mass on the last row is positive for
\beta>(1-2/n)4\pi
; these are satisfied for all
n\leq N
and
\emptyset\neq I\varsubsetneq[n]
, again by the assumption
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
.

Finally, the contribution from the last term is

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{m}I_{4}(\mu,m)\,\mathrm{d}\mu&=m^{|I^{c}|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+1}\int_{0}^{m}\mu^{|I|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)}\int_{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{m^{2}}{\mu^{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-u}\frac{\operatorname{d}\!u}{u}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\\
&\leq Bm^{n\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+2}\overset{m\to 0}{\longrightarrow}0\end{split}

(2.164)

where the
u
-integral is again bounded uniformly in
m
and
\mu
, the last
\mu
-integral is convergent for all
\beta>(1-1/|I|)4\pi
and the exponent of the mass on the last row is positive for all
\beta>(1-2/n)4\pi
, and these are again satisfied for all relevant cases by the assumption
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
.

Let us continue with the term involving
R_{3}
. By the symmetry
I\leftrightarrow I^{c}
we only need to consider the case
\mu\leq\lambda
, which means that the integration region for the mass scale integrals with respect to
(\lambda,\mu)
is
[0,m]\times[0,\lambda]
. Analogously to
Equation

2.159
we obtain

\begin{split}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}&\int_{\Lambda^{n}}R_{3}(\mu,\lambda,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\operatorname{d}\!x\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2}\beta\tilde{C}[\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N)]^{n-2}2^{n}C^{n}|\Lambda|^{2}\bigg\{8(n-1)n^{n-2}\mu\lambda\int_{t}^{\lambda^{-2}}s^{n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\quad+\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}|I|^{|I|-1}|I^{c}|^{|I^{c}|-1}\bigg[\int_{\lambda^{-2}}^{\mu^{-2}}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2})s}}{s}\left(\lambda s(\lambda^{-2})^{-\frac{1}{2}+|I|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}+(\lambda^{-2})^{|I|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times 2\mu ss^{-\frac{1}{2}+|I^{c}|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\int_{\mu^{-2}}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2})s}}{s}\left(\lambda s(\lambda^{-2})^{-\frac{1}{2}+|I|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}+(\lambda^{-2})^{|I|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left(\mu s(\mu^{-2})^{-\frac{1}{2}+|I^{c}|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}+(\mu^{-2})^{|I^{c}|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\right)\operatorname{d}\!s\bigg]\bigg\}.\\
\end{split}

(2.165)

We may write for the part inside the
\{\}
-brackets

8(n-1)n^{n-2}J_{1}(\lambda,\mu)+\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}|I|^{|I|-1}|I^{c}|^{|I^{c}|-1}\left(2J_{2}(\lambda,\mu)+J_{3}(\lambda,\mu)\right)

(2.166)

with obvious identifications for
J_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
. Thus, the final task is to prove

\int_{0}^{m}\int_{0}^{\lambda}J_{i}(\lambda,\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\overset{m\to 0}{\longrightarrow}0

(2.167)

for all
i=1,2,3
. Recall that we assumed
\mu\leq\lambda
.

First we have,

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{m}\int_{0}^{\lambda}J_{1}(\lambda,\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\lambda&\leq B\int_{0}^{m}\lambda(\lambda^{-2})^{n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)+1}\int_{0}^{\lambda}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\leq B\int_{0}^{m}\lambda^{n\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+1}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\overset{m\to 0}{\longrightarrow}0\end{split}

(2.168)

if
\beta>(1-2/n)4\pi
, which is satisfied for all
n\leq N
. In the first step we have simply set
t=0
in the
s
-integral since it is convergent for all
\beta<4\pi
in the neighborghood of the origin.

The second term yields

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{m}\int_{0}^{\lambda}&J_{2}(\lambda,\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\\
&\leq C\int_{0}^{m}\lambda^{|I|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+2}\int_{0}^{\lambda}\mu([\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2}]^{-1})^{\frac{3}{2}+|I^{c}|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\int_{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mu^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\mu^{2}}}e^{-u}u^{\frac{1}{2}+|I^{c}|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!u\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\\
&\quad+B\int_{0}^{m}\lambda^{|I|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)}\int_{0}^{\lambda}\mu([\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2}]^{-1})^{\frac{1}{2}+|I^{c}|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\int_{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mu^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\mu^{2}}}e^{-u}u^{\frac{1}{2}+|I^{c}|\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!u\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\\
&\leq B\int_{0}^{m}\lambda^{|I|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)}\int_{0}^{\lambda}\mu^{|I^{c}|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\\
&\leq Bm^{n\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+2}\overset{m\to 0}{\longrightarrow}0,\end{split}

(2.169)

if
\beta>(1-1/|I^{c}|)4\pi
,
\beta>(1-2/|I|)4\pi
and
\beta>(1-2/n)4\pi
, and all of these are satisfied for all
n\leq N
and
\emptyset\neq I\varsubsetneq[n]
as before. The
u
-integrals are bounded uniformly in
\lambda
and
\mu
, and we have used
\mu/\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2}}\leq 1
and
(\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2})^{-1}\leq\lambda^{-2}
or
(\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2})^{-1}\leq\mu^{-2}
appropriately to get the same exponents for
\lambda
and
\mu
for both terms.

Finally, the last term yields

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{m}\int_{0}^{\lambda}&J_{3}(\lambda,\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\\
&=\int_{0}^{m}\int_{0}^{\lambda}\int_{\mu^{-2}}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2})s}(s\lambda^{|I|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+2}+\lambda^{|I|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)})(s\mu^{|I^{c}|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)+2}+\mu^{|I^{c}|\left(\frac{\beta}{4\pi}-1\right)})\frac{\operatorname{d}\!s}{s}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}\lambda.\end{split}

(2.170)

Each of the terms resulting from expanding the brackets above can be reduced to the cases treated in the estimation of the previous term by similar techniques. Thus, we are done.

2.2.6
Proof of
Lemma

2.4

In the proofs of
Propositions

2.7
and
2.10
we simply assumed that the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)
are differentiable with respect to the mass
m
and that the mass derivative can be computed under the
s
-integral in the definition
Equation

2.8
.
In this section, we justify this assumption.

We begin the proof with the estimates for the functions
\tilde{h}_{t}^{n}
. The proof proceeds similarly to that of
Proposition

2.7
, and we therefore keep the exposition brief. For the base case, we can simply choose
\tilde{h}_{t}^{1}\equiv h_{t}^{n}
for
t<1<m^{-2}
and
\tilde{h}_{t}^{1}=C^{\prime}
from
Equation

2.13
in
Lemma

2.3
for
t\geq 1
. We then assume that the claim holds for all
k<n\leq N
and define
\tilde{h}_{t}^{n}\equiv h_{t}^{n}
for
t<1<m^{-2}
(since
m\in(0,1)
). For
t\geq 1
, we define

\begin{split}\tilde{h}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})&:=\frac{\beta}{2}\bar{B}_{N}[H_{1}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{0}^{1}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\tilde{h}_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})\tilde{h}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\quad+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{1}^{t}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\tilde{h}_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})\tilde{h}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\operatorname{d}\!s,\end{split}

(2.171)

where we have split the integral at
s=1
rather than at
s=m^{-2}
. The analysis for the case
t<1
is identical to the analysis for
t<m^{-2}
in the proof of
Proposition

2.7
, modulo the substitution
m^{-2}\to 1
, and yields the same contribution to the constant
B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)
, which also coincides with the one obtained for the first term below.

For
t\geq 1
we first use
Lemma

2.15
and then proceed analogously to
Equation

2.104
, except that we again use
Equation

2.146
for the
x_{j}
-integral if
s\geq 1
(this is necessary to obtain the correct
t
-dependence). We may assume that
t>e
since otherwise the logarithmic part below would not appear at all and the resulting estimates are uniform in
t
. Then, after using the induction hypothesis, we need to further split the term with
s\in(1,t)
at
s=e
since the estimate of the claim changes at this value. Indeed, for
s\in(1,e)
the induction hypothesis produces uniform estimates as opposed to the logarithmic ones for
s\geq e
. Thus, we obtain

\begin{split}\left\lVert H_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\tilde{h}_{t}^{n}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}&\leq\beta\tilde{C}[B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)]^{n-2}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}\\
&\quad\times\bigg(\bar{B}_{N}C_{1}\int_{0}^{1}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}-\frac{3}{2}+n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s+|\Lambda|\left[\int_{1}^{e}\frac{\operatorname{d}\!s}{s}+\int_{e}^{t}s^{-1}[\log(s)]^{n-2}\operatorname{d}\!s\right]\bigg)\\
&\leq\beta\tilde{C}[B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)]^{n-2}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}\\
&\quad\times\left(\frac{C_{1}\bar{B}_{N}}{(n-1)\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}+|\Lambda|+|\Lambda|\frac{[\log(t)]^{n-1}}{n-1}\right)\\
&\leq\beta\tilde{C}\left(\frac{C_{1}\bar{B}_{N}}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+N|\Lambda|\right)[B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)]^{n-2}C^{n}n^{n-2}\max(1,[\log(t)]^{n-1}),\end{split}

(2.172)

where we also used the bound

\int_{e}^{t}s^{-1}[\log(t)]^{n-2}\operatorname{d}\!s\leq\frac{1}{n-1}[\log(t)]^{n-1}.

(2.173)

Thus, the claim follows by choosing

B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda):=\beta\tilde{C}\left(\frac{C_{1}\bar{B}_{N}}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+N|\Lambda|\right).

(2.174)

We now turn to the differentiability claim and the functions
\tilde{g}_{t}^{n}
.
The base case
n=1
follows from
Equation

2.128
using
m\leq 1
for the prefactor,
e^{-m^{2}s}\leq 1
and
|\tilde{V}_{t}^{1}(m,\epsilon)|\leq\tilde{h}_{t}^{1}
. We then assume that
\tilde{V}_{t}^{k}
is differentiable for all
k<n\leq N
and that the functions
\tilde{g}_{t}^{k}
with the stated properties exist. Under this assumption, the formal computation in
Equation

2.129
is justified once the induction step is completed.

Next, we again mimic the proof of
Proposition

2.7
and decompose

\tilde{g}_{t}^{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\tilde{g}_{t}^{n,i}
. The terms with
i=1,3,4
are obtained directly from the functions
g_{t}^{n,i}
by replacing

h_{s}^{|I|}
with
\tilde{h}_{s}^{|I|}
,

g_{s}^{|I|}
with
\tilde{g}_{s}^{|I|}
(and analogously for
I^{c}
),
the prefactor
m
in
g_{t}^{n,1}
with
1
, and
e^{-m^{2}s}
with
1
.
The analysis of these terms for
t<1
is completely analogous to that of the terms
g_{t}^{n,i}
with
t<m^{-2}
, producing the same result.

The term
\tilde{g}_{t}^{n,2}
is obtained by the same transformations from
g_{t}^{n,2}
in the case
t<1
. For
t\geq 1
, we modify the estimates

Equations

2.132
and
2.133

by splitting the integrals at
s=1
rather than at
s=m^{-2}
. For
s\leq 1\leq t
, we obtain

\begin{split}\left(\int_{s}^{t}re^{-m^{2}r}\left(\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{r}(k,l)\right)\operatorname{d}\!r\right)e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{kl}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}^{m}(k,l)}&\leq\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}N^{2}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\left(\int_{s}^{1}r^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\operatorname{d}\!r+\int_{1}^{t}\operatorname{d}\!r\right)\\
&\leq\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}N^{2}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\int_{0}^{t}\left(r^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right)\operatorname{d}\!r\\
&\leq\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}N^{2}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}t\left(\frac{1}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right),\end{split}

(2.175)

where, in the last line, we used the fact that
t^{-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\leq 1
for
t\geq 1
.
For
t\geq s\geq 1
, we instead use

\left(\int_{s}^{t}re^{-m^{2}r}\left(\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\sigma_{kl}C_{r}(k,l)\right)\operatorname{d}\!r\right)e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{kl}\sigma_{kl}K_{s,t}^{m}(k,l)}\leq n^{2}\int_{s}^{t}\operatorname{d}\!r\leq N^{2}t.

(2.176)

For
t\geq 1
, we then define

\begin{split}\tilde{g}_{t}^{n,2}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})&:=\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}N^{2}\left(\frac{1}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right)t\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{0}^{1}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\tilde{h}_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})\tilde{h}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}N^{2}t\sum_{I\varsubsetneq[n]}\int_{1}^{t}G_{s}^{I}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\tilde{h}_{s}^{|I|}(\mathbf{x}_{I},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I})\tilde{h}_{s}^{|I^{c}|}(\mathbf{x}_{I^{c}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{I^{c}})\operatorname{d}\!s.\end{split}

(2.177)

We may again assume that
t\geq e
since otherwise the logarithmic contribution will again not appear and the task becomes simpler. We can now estimate the contributions from the range
t\geq 1
.

Analogously to the estimates for the functions
\tilde{h}_{t}^{n}
, we obtain for the first term

\begin{split}\left\lVert\tilde{g}_{t}^{n,1}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}&\leq 2\beta\tilde{C}[B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)]^{n-2}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}\\
&\quad\times\bigg(C_{1}\int_{0}^{1}s^{-\frac{1}{2}+n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s+|\Lambda|\int_{1}^{e}\operatorname{d}\!s+|\Lambda|\int_{e}^{t}[\log(s)]^{n-2}\operatorname{d}\!s\bigg)\\
&\leq 2\beta\tilde{C}\left(C_{1}\sup_{n\leq N}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n}{n+1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+N|\Lambda|\right)[B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)]^{n-2}C^{n}n^{n-2}t\max(1,[\log(t)]^{n-1}),\end{split}

(2.178)

where
B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)
is the constant obtained in the estimate for
\tilde{h}_{t}^{n}
. In this estimate, we multiplied the first term in the second row of
Equation

2.178
by
t\geq 1
and used

\int_{1}^{e}\operatorname{d}\!s+\int_{e}^{t}[\log(s)]^{n-2}\operatorname{d}\!s\leq\int_{0}^{t}\operatorname{d}\!s+t\int_{1}^{t}s^{-1}[\log(s)]^{n-2}\operatorname{d}\!s=\left(1+(n-1)^{-1}[\log(t)]^{n-1}\right)t.

(2.179)

Note also that the first
s
-integral in the second row of
Equation

2.178
is convergent for all
\beta<(1+1/n)4\pi
.

For the second case, we obtain analogously

\begin{split}\left\lVert\tilde{g}_{t}^{n,2}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}&\leq\beta^{2}\tilde{C}N^{2}[B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)]^{n-2}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}t\\
&\quad\times\bigg(\bar{B}_{N}\tilde{B}C_{1}\left(\frac{1}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right)\int_{0}^{1}s^{\frac{\beta}{8\pi}-\frac{3}{2}+n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&\qquad\qquad+|\Lambda|\left(\int_{1}^{e}\frac{\operatorname{d}\!s}{s}+\int_{e}^{t}s^{-1}[\log(s)]^{n-2}\operatorname{d}\!s\right)\bigg)\\
&\leq\beta^{2}\tilde{C}N^{2}\left[\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}\left(\frac{1}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right)\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+|\Lambda|N\right]\\
&\quad\times[B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)]^{n-2}C^{n}n^{n-2}t\max(1,[\log(t)]^{n-1}),\end{split}

(2.180)

where we used manipulations analogous to those in the estimate for
\tilde{h}_{t}^{n}
.
Finally, we have

\begin{split}\left\lVert\tilde{g}_{t}^{n,3}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right\rVert_{n,\Lambda}&\leq\beta\tilde{C}[B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)]^{|I|-1}[\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)]^{|I^{c}|-1}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}\\
&\quad\times\bigg(C_{1}\int_{0}^{1}s^{-\frac{1}{2}+n\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}\right)}\operatorname{d}\!s+|\Lambda|\int_{1}^{e}\operatorname{d}\!s+|\Lambda|\int_{e}^{t}[\log(s)]^{n-2}\operatorname{d}\!s\bigg)\\
&\leq\beta\tilde{C}\left(C_{1}\sup_{n\leq N}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n}{n+1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+N|\Lambda|\right)\left[\max(B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda),\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda))\right]^{n-2}C^{n}(n-1)n^{n-2}\\
&\quad\times t\max(1,[\log(t)]^{n-1}),\end{split}

(2.181)

where we used the same manipulations as for the term
\tilde{g}_{t}^{n,1}
.

We conclude by choosing
\tilde{B}_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)
as the maximum of

B_{1}(\beta,N,\Lambda)
,
\tilde{B}_{1}^{\prime}(\beta,N)
(the constant arising from the
t<1
contribution, which is, as mentioned, the same as
Equation

2.139
in the proof of
Proposition

2.7
), and

\begin{split}4\beta\tilde{C}\left(C_{1}\sup_{n\leq N}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n}{n+1}\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+N|\Lambda|\right)+\beta^{2}\tilde{C}N^{2}\left[\tilde{B}\bar{B}_{N}C_{1}\left(\frac{1}{1-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+1\right)\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}+N|\Lambda|\right].\end{split}

(2.182)

This completes the proof of differentiability and of the norm estimates. Note that the majorants
\tilde{g}_{t}^{n}
are independent of
\epsilon
, so the estimates are also applicable in the case
\epsilon=0
. Thus,
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m):=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)
is also differentiable with respect to
m
and respects the same estimates. Differentiability on
(0,1)
implies continuity on
(0,1)
, and by the discussion in
Remark

2.6
, continuity at
m=0
holds by definition. This concludes the proof.

3
Analysis of the renormalized partition function

The main tasks of this section are to prove
A
and to provide further tools for the proof of
B
and
C
. Recall from
Section

1.1
that
A
is equivalent to parts 1–3 of
Theorem

3.1
(with the choice
\eta=-z\mathbf{1}_{x\in\Lambda}
), which is stated below. Part 4 of
Theorem

3.1
is needed in the proofs of
B
and
C
. The three subsections below provide preliminaries for the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. In
Section

3.1
we prove uniform bounds for the renormalized partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{R}
,
Section

3.2
provides an everywhere convergent power series representation for
\mathcal{Z}_{R}
, and in
Section

3.3
we prove that this expansion also has a limit as we first take
\epsilon\to 0
and then
m\to 0
.

In what follows, we treat all dimensions on equal footing until the point where estimates derived in the previous section are applied. We first fix
\beta\in(0,4d\pi)
and choose
N(\beta)\equiv N
even and such that

\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
, where
\beta_{N}:=(1-1/N)4d\pi
. The restriction

\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
is also in force in this section for
d\geq 2
. Nevertheless, we perform all computations in arbitrary dimension. Thus, if we were to obtain more refined estimates, analogous to the results in
Section

2
and the techniques used in their proofs, in the full parameter range
(0,8\pi)
for
d=2
, then all remaining arguments would carry over without difficulty as long as these estimates satisfy the assumptions of
Lemma

3.2
below. All of the results in this section have their analogues in
[
6
, Section 5]
.

We begin by defining the renormalized generalized partition function analogously to

Equations

1.17
and
1.16
: Let
\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
and

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)&:=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}Z_{k}(\eta)\\
&:=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)\\
&\qquad\times\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}e^{-\frac{1}{(2k)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2kd}}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{2k}\eta_{i}\Bigr)\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}\mid j\in[2k]\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}\\
&\,\,=\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[e^{-S_{t}^{R}(\beta,\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)}\right],\end{split}

(3.1)

where
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
was defined in
Equation

2.2
, and the last equality follows from
Theorem

2.1
. Recall that the renormalized series
S_{t}^{R}
was defined in
Equation

2.10
. We have also used the shorthand notations
\eta_{i}:=\eta(x_{i},\sigma_{i})
and
\varphi_{j}:=\varphi(x_{j})
as before.

We now state the main convergence result for the partition function.

Theorem 3.1

(Convergence of the partition function)

Let
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
if
d=1
and
\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
if
d\geq 2
, and let

\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
. Then the following statements hold.

1.

The limits

\begin{split}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)&=:\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta|m),\\
\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)&=:\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta)\end{split}

(3.2)

exist and are finite.

2.

The maps
z\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m)
and
z\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta)
define entire functions of
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta)
is an even function, i.e.,

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta)=\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,-z\eta)
.

3.

If
\eta(x,1)=\overline{\eta(x,-1)}
for a.a.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta|m)>0
and
\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta)>0
.

4.

Suppose that
\eta_{\alpha}
depends on complex parameters
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and that

\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot|m,\epsilon)
depends on
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
and the same parameters in such a way that, for every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{C}^{k}
, there exists a compact set
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the following three statements hold

\bigcup_{\alpha\in K}\supp(\eta_{\alpha})\subset\Lambda\times\{-1,1\}\quad\text{ and }\quad\bigcup_{\alpha\in K}\bigcup_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}\supp(\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon))\subset\Lambda\times\{-1,1\},

(3.3)

\sup_{\alpha\in K}\left\lVert\eta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})}<\infty\quad\text{ and }\quad\sup_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{\alpha\in K}\left\lVert\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})}<\infty,

(3.4)

and

\lim_{m\to 0}\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{\alpha\in K}\left\lVert\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)-\eta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})}=0.

(3.5)

Then

\lim_{m\to 0}\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{\alpha\in K}\bigl|\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)-\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha})\bigr|=0.

(3.6)

An analogous statement also holds for fixed
m\in(0,1)
.

For the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, we require several preliminary results. As a first step, one could invoke
[
6
, Lemma 5.2]
, since the proof given there readily extends to dimensions other than
d=2
. However, we anticipate that in higher dimensions, and for
\beta
beyond
(d+1)2\pi
—in particular for
\beta\in[6\pi,8\pi)
when
d=2
—additional input will be necessary. We therefore present a slightly more general statement in arbitrary dimension, postponing its proof to Appendix
C
, since it closely follows the proof of
[
6
, Lemma 5.2]
.

Recall that
\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
is the centered Gaussian process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with covariance

K_{\sqrt{t}}^{m}(x,y):=\int_{t}^{\infty}C_{s}^{m}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!s,

(3.7)

whose law is supported on
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We denote expectation with respect to this law by
\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}
. In the lemma below, we restrict
\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
to a compact set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, but for simplicity, we write

\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}|_{\Lambda}\equiv\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
.

For a sufficiently smooth function
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we define

\begin{split}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{k}(\Lambda)}&:=\sum_{|\nu|\leq k}p_{\nu,\Lambda}(f),\\
p_{\nu,\Lambda}(f)&:=\sup_{x\in\Lambda}\bigl|(\partial^{\nu}f)(x)\bigr|,\end{split}

(3.8)

where
\nu
is a multi-index and the sum runs over all multi-indices of order at most
k\geq 1
.

We then have the following generalization of
[
6
, Lemma 5.2]
.

Lemma 3.2

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be compact,
B>0
, and
1\leq\alpha<2
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in(0,\infty)
. Then there exists a constant

C_{k,B,\alpha,t,\Lambda}<\infty
such that

\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[e^{B\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{C^{k}(\Lambda)}^{\alpha}}\right]\leq C_{k,B,\alpha,t,\Lambda},

(3.9)

where the constant
C_{k,B,\alpha,t,\Lambda}>0
is independent of
m
.

Remark 3.3
.

By
Equation

2.74
,

K_{\sqrt{t}}^{m,\nu}(x,y):=(\partial_{1}^{\nu}\partial_{2}^{\nu}K_{\sqrt{t}}^{m})(x,y)=\int_{t}^{\infty}(\partial_{1}^{\nu}\partial_{2}^{\nu}C_{s}^{m})(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!s.

(3.10)

Thus,
\partial^{\nu}\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
is a smooth centered Gaussian field with covariance

Equation

3.10
, by Kolmogorov–Chentsov type arguments (see, for example,
[
34
, Appendix B]
).

Remark 3.4
.

Note that
\lim_{t\to 0}C_{k,B,\alpha,t,\Lambda}
is not finite, since the field ceases to be differentiable, or even an ordinary function, in this limit. Similarly,
\lim_{\alpha\to 2}C_{k,B,\alpha,t,\Lambda}
cannot be finite for all values of
B
by Gaussianity. Our proof also breaks down in the limit
|\Lambda|\to\infty
. However, none of these divergences is restrictive for our application.

3.1
Uniform bounds for
\mathcal{Z}_{R}

We now turn to proving uniform bounds for
\mathcal{Z}_{R}
.

Proposition 3.5

(Uniform bounds)

Fix a compact set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
for
d=1
and

\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
for
d\geq 2
. Then the following statements hold.

1.

For any fixed
M>0
,

\sup_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\}):\\
\supp(\eta)\subset\Lambda\times\{-1,1\}\\
\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq M\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)|<\infty.

(3.11)

2.

Let
\overline{L_{c}^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
denote the set of

\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
satisfying

\overline{\eta(\cdot,1)}=\eta(\cdot,-1)
. Then
\mathcal{Z}_{R}
is positive, and for all
M>0
we have

\inf_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in\overline{L_{c}^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\}):\\
\supp(\eta)\subset\Lambda\times\{-1,1\}\\
\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq M\end{subarray}}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)>0.

(3.12)

3.

There exists
\delta\equiv\delta_{\Lambda,\beta}>0
, independent of
\epsilon
and
m
, such that

\inf_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\}):\\
\supp(\eta)\subset\Lambda\times\{-1,1\}\\
\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\delta\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)|>0.

(3.13)

Proof.

For the first claim, fix
M>0
, choose
\beta
in the appropriate interval, and let

N\equiv N(\beta)
as usual. Then, by the bounds
Equations

2.46
and
2.47
derived in the proof of the first claim in

Theorem

2.1
, we obtain

\begin{split}|\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)|&=\left|\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[e^{-S_{t}^{R}(\beta,\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)}\right]\right|\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[e^{|S_{t}^{R}(\beta,\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)|}\right]\\
&\leq\exp\!\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N}|\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\infty}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)|\right)\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[\exp\!\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\varphi}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)|\right)\right]\\
&\leq\exp\!\left(NC(\beta,N,t,\eta)\right)\\
&\quad\times\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[\exp\!\left(\bigl(2[\sqrt{\beta t}\left\lVert\gradient\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\Lambda})}\vee 1]+1\bigr)\frac{t^{-\frac{d}{2}}}{B_{d}(\beta,N)}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigl(\hat{B}(\beta,N)Mt^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}\bigr)^{n}\right)\right].\end{split}

(3.14)

The renormalized series
S_{t}^{R}
and the summands
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\infty}
and
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\varphi}

were defined in
Equation

2.10
and
Equation

2.36
,
respectively. The constants
B_{d}(\beta,N)
,

\hat{B}(\beta,N)=2eB_{d}(\beta,N)C
, and
C(\beta,N,t,\eta)
were derived in the proofs
of
Propositions

2.7
and
2.10
and are
independent of
m
and
\epsilon
. More precisely,
B_{d}
is defined in
Equation

2.127
and
Equation

2.121
for the cases
d=1
and
d\geq 2
, respectively, and the precise form of
C(\beta,N,t,\eta)
is irrelevant to our analysis. We also used the assumption
\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq M
. Recall that
B_{\Lambda}:=\overline{B_{2R(\Lambda)}(0)}
, where
R(\Lambda)
is chosen so that

\Lambda\subset B_{R(\Lambda)}(0)
and
\Lambda
is compact with

\supp(\eta)\subset\Lambda
. We now choose
t>0
such that

\hat{B}(\beta,N)Mt^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\beta}{8\pi}}<1
, so that the series above converges geometrically. The first claim then follows from
Lemma

3.2
.

For the second claim, observe that if

\overline{\eta(\cdot,1)}=\eta(\cdot,-1)
, then the exponent in

\mathcal{Z}_{R}=\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}[e^{-S_{t}^{R}}]
is real-valued. Hence,

|e^{-x}|\geq e^{-|x|}
, and arguing as above, together with

Lemma

3.2
and Jensen’s inequality in the form

\mathbb{E}[X^{-1}]\geq\mathbb{E}[X]^{-1}
, yields the desired lower bound.

Finally, we turn to the third claim. The proof is completely analogous to the proof of
[
6
, Proposition 5.3 (ii)]
, but we reproduce it here for the convenience of the reader. First fix
\eta
with
\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}=1
and consider
the function
z\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)
. Note that

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)=\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\!\left(\exp(-V_0(\beta,\eta,\vp|\eps))\right)
, where,
for fixed
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
,

V_{0}(\beta,\eta,\varphi_{m,\epsilon}|\epsilon):=\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta(x,\sigma)e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma\varphi_{m,\epsilon}(x)}\operatorname{d}\!x

(3.15)

is a deterministically bounded random variable. By Fubini’s theorem and Morera’s
theorem, it follows that
z\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)
is entire (for every

\omega
in the underlying probability space).

Let
K\subset\mathbb{C}
be compact and choose
R>0
such that
K\subset B_{R}(0)
. Then, for

z\in K
, the Cauchy integral formula yields

\frac{d}{dz}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\partial B_{R}(0)}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\zeta\eta|m,\epsilon)}{(\zeta-z)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta.

(3.16)

By the first part,
\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\zeta\eta|m,\epsilon)
is uniformly bounded (in
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
\zeta\in\overline{B_{R}(0)}
), and the
denominator is uniformly bounded away from zero by the choice of
K
and
R
. Therefore, the derivative of the map
z\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)
is uniformly
bounded on compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}
and also in
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Since
\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,0|m,\epsilon)=1
, this implies
that there exists
\delta>0
, independent of
m
and
\epsilon
, such that

\inf_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}\inf_{|z|<\delta}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\}):\\
\supp(\eta)\subset\Lambda\times\{-1,1\}\\
\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}=1\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)|>0

(3.17)

by continuity of the map
z\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)
. By scaling, this implies the claim.
∎

Note that the first two claims of
Proposition

3.5
are valid for arbitrary
\eta
, which here plays the role of the coupling constant, whereas the third claim holds only for sufficiently
small coupling.

3.2
Expansion of
\mathcal{Z}_{R}

The strategy for proving that the limit

\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)
exists is to first establish the
existence of an everywhere convergent series expansion for

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)
. More precisely, we have the following result.

Proposition 3.6

For fixed
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
, the map

z\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)
is an entire function with the Taylor
expansion

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)&=\sum_{L=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{L}}{L!}\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)\\
&=\sum_{L=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{L}}{L!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{L}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{Ld}}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{L}\eta_{i}\Bigr)\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta|m,\epsilon)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},\end{split}

(3.18)

where
\eta_{i}:=\eta(x_{i},\sigma_{i})
as before. Recall also that
\varphi_{j}:=\varphi(x_{j})
. The kernels
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}
admit two representations. The first is

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta|m,\epsilon)&=\sum_{\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{L}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{N/2}\geq 0\\
L=n+\sum_{k=1}^{N/2}2kj_{k}\end{subarray}}\binom{L}{n,2j_{1},4j_{2},\dots,Nj_{N/2}}(-1)^{n}\prod_{l=1}^{N/2}\frac{(-1)^{j_{l}}}{[(2l)!]^{j_{l}}j_{l}!}\\
&\quad\times\Biggl(\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}\prod_{l_{k}=1}^{j_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{\bigl\{\sum_{j\in\pi([[\,n+(l_{k}-1)2k+1,\;n+2l_{k}k\,]])}\sigma_{j}=0\bigr\}}\Biggr)\\
&\quad\times\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\prod_{a=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{\pi(a)}\varphi_{\pi(a)}}\right]\\
&\quad\times\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}\prod_{l_{k}=1}^{j_{k}}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{p_{l_{k}}}\varphi_{p_{l_{k}}}}\mid p_{l_{k}}\in\pi([[\,n+2(l_{k}-1)k+1,\;n+2l_{k}k\,]])\right],\end{split}

(3.19)

where
\mathbb{S}_{L}
denotes the permutation group of
[L]
and we set
[[k,l]]:=\{k,k+1,\dots,l\}
for integers
k<l
.

The second representation is

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta|m,\epsilon)&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,\,\text{$p$ even}\\
p+q=L\end{subarray}}\binom{L}{p}\bigg(\sum_{\Pi_{1}\in\mathfrak{P}_{q}}(-1)^{|\Pi_{1}|}\prod_{\pi_{1}\in\Pi_{1}}\tilde{V}_{t}^{|\pi_{1}|}(\mathbf{x}_{\pi_{1}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\pi_{1}}|m,\epsilon)\\
&\qquad\times\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[\prod_{\pi_{1}\in\Pi_{1}}\Bigl(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j\in\pi_{1}}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}-\mathbf{1}_{|\pi_{1}|\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{l\in\pi_{1}}\sigma_{l}=0}\Bigr)\right]\bigg)\\
&\quad\times\bigg(\sum_{\Pi_{2}\in\mathfrak{P}_{[[q+1,L]],\,\mathrm{even}}^{\leq N/2}}(-1)^{|\Pi_{2}|}\prod_{\pi_{2}\in\Pi_{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i\in\pi_{2}}\sigma_{i}=0}\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\left(\tilde{V}_{t}^{|\pi_{2}|}(\mathbf{x}_{\pi_{2}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\pi_{2}}|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{|\pi_{2}|}(\mathbf{x}_{\pi_{2}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\pi_{2}}|m,\epsilon)\right)\bigg),\end{split}

(3.20)

where
\mathfrak{P}_{A}
denotes the collection of all partitions of a set
A
, and
we write
\mathfrak{P}_{q}\equiv\mathfrak{P}_{[q]}
. Moreover,

\mathfrak{P}_{A,\mathrm{even}}^{\leq N}:=\Bigl\{\Pi\text{ is a partition of }A\;\Big|\;\text{each block $\pi$ of $\Pi$ has cardinality $2k$ for some $\mathbb{N}\ni k\leq N/2$}\Bigr\}.

(3.21)

Finally, for every
M,\delta>0
and compact
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exists a
constant
0<B(\delta,M,\Lambda)<\infty
, independent of
\epsilon
,
m
,
\eta
, and

L
, such that

\sup_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})\\
\supp(\eta)\subset\Lambda\times\{-1,1\}\\
\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq M\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)|\leq B(\delta,M,\Lambda)\,\delta^{L}\,L!.

(3.22)

Proof.

To derive the first representation of the kernels, we expand the expression

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)&=\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[e^{-V_{0}(\beta,z\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)}\right]\\
&\quad\times\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}e^{-\frac{z^{2k}}{(2k)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2kd}}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{2k}\eta_{i}\Bigr)\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}\mid j\in[2k]\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}.\end{split}

(3.23)

into Taylor series in the variable
z
. Since all relevant random variables are
deterministically bounded, each factor is an entire function (for all

\omega\in\Omega
) by Fubini’s and Morera’s theorems. Consequently,

\mathcal{Z}_{R}
itself is an entire function. We may therefore expand each exponential
separately and collect powers of
z
. In the first factor we may also exchange the
expectation and summation. This yields

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)&=\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-z)^{n}}{n!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{nd}}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}\Bigr)\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\bigg)\\
&\quad\times\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}\bigg\{\sum_{j_{k}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j_{k}}z^{2kj_{k}}}{[(2k)!]^{j_{k}}j_{k}!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2kj_{k}}}\Bigl(\prod_{b=0}^{j_{k}-1}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{l_{b}\in[[2bk+1,(b+1)2k]]}\sigma_{l_{b}}=0}\Bigr)\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2kj_{k}d}}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{2kj_{k}}\eta_{i}\Bigr)\prod_{a=0}^{j_{k}-1}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{p_{a}}\varphi_{p_{a}}}\mid p_{a}\in[[2ak+1,(a+1)2k]]\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\bigg\}\\
&=:\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}F_{0,n}z^{n}\bigg)\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}\sum_{j_{k}=0}^{\infty}F_{k,j_{k}}z^{2kj_{k}},\end{split}

(3.24)

where we use the notation
[[k,l]]:=\{k,k+1,\dots,l\}
for integers
k<l
. The final
line serves only to simplify notation when combining the sums.

We now sum over
L=n+\sum_{k=1}^{N/2}2kj_{k}
to obtain

\sum_{L=0}^{\infty}z^{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L=n+\sum_{k=1}^{N/2}2kj_{k}\\
n,j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{N/2}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\binom{L}{n,2j_{1},4j_{2},\dots,Nj_{N/2}}F_{0,n}\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}F_{k,j_{k}}.

(3.25)

Next, we extract the integrals from the factors
F_{0,n}
and
F_{k,j_{k}}
, exchange
them with the inner sum, and symmetrize the resulting expression to introduce the
factor
1/L!
. This yields the formula

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)&=\sum_{L=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{L}}{L!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{L}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{Ld}}\Biggl\{\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{L}\eta_{i}\Bigr)\sum_{\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{L}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L=n+\sum_{k=1}^{N/2}2kj_{k}\\
n,j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{N/2}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\binom{L}{n,2j_{1},4j_{2},\dots,Nj_{N/2}}\\
&\quad\times(-1)^{n}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{N/2}\frac{(-1)^{j_{l}}}{[(2l)!]^{j_{l}}j_{l}!}\right)\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}\prod_{l_{k}=1}^{j_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{\bigl\{\sum_{j\in\pi([[\,n+(l_{k}-1)2k+1,\;n+2l_{k}k\,]])}\sigma_{j}=0\bigr\}}\\
&\quad\times\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\prod_{a=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{\pi(a)}\varphi_{\pi(a)}}\right]\\
&\quad\times\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}\prod_{l_{k}=1}^{j_{k}}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{p_{l_{k}}}\varphi_{p_{l_{k}}}}\mid p_{l_{k}}\in\pi([[\,n+2(l_{k}-1)k+1,\;n+2l_{k}k\,]])\right]\Biggr\}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},\end{split}

(3.26)

from which the desired representation can be seen.

To derive the second representation, we write

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)=F_{1}(z)F_{2}(z),

(3.27)

where the entire functions
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are given by

F_{1}(z):=\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[\exp(
-\widetilde{S}_R^t(\beta,z\eta,\vp|m,\eps)
)\right],

(3.28)

with

\widetilde{S}_{R}^{t}(\beta,z\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon):=S_{t}(\beta,z\eta,\varphi|m,\epsilon)-\sum_{k=1}^{N/2}\frac{z^{2k}}{(2k)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2kd}}\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{2k}\eta_{j}\Bigr)\tilde{V}_{t}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},

(3.29)

where
S_{t}
is the series defined in
Equation

2.6
,
and

\begin{split}F_{2}(z)&:=\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}\exp\!\bigg(-\frac{z^{2k}}{(2k)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2kd}}\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{2k}\eta_{j}\Bigr)\left[\tilde{V}_{t}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\bigg)\\
&=:\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}\exp(-z^{2k}A_{2k}),\end{split}

(3.30)

where
A_{2k}\equiv\mathcal{A}_{2k}^{\infty}(\beta,t|m,\epsilon)
as defined in

Equation

2.36
. For the purposes of this computation we use this shorthand and suppress the dependence of
A_{2k}
on the parameters.

Since
z\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)
is entire, we have

\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)=\frac{d^{L}}{dz^{L}}\bigg|_{z=0}\!\bigl(F_{1}(z)F_{2}(z)\bigr)=\sum_{p=0}^{L}\binom{L}{p}F_{1}^{(L-p)}(0)F_{2}^{(p)}(0).

(3.31)

The surviving terms in
F_{2}^{(p)}(0)
are most easily identified using
Faà di Bruno’s formula,

\frac{d^{p}}{dz^{p}}f(g(z))=\sum_{\Pi\in\mathfrak{P}_{p}}f^{(|\Pi|)}(g(z))\prod_{\pi\in\Pi}g^{(|\pi|)}(z),

(3.32)

where the sum runs over all partitions
\mathfrak{P}_{p}
of
[p]
, and
|\cdot|

denotes cardinality. In the present case,

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle=e^{-z},

(3.33)

\displaystyle g(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N/2}A_{2k}z^{2k}.

(3.34)

We readily observe that

g^{(n)}(0)=\begin{cases}(2k)!A_{2k},&\text{if }n=2k\text{ for some }k\leq N/2,\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(3.35)

and that
f^{(n)}(g(0))=f^{(n)}(0)=(-1)^{n}
. Hence,

F_{2}^{(p)}(0)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{\Pi\in\mathfrak{P}_{p,\mathrm{even}}^{\leq N}}(-1)^{|\Pi|}\prod_{\pi\in\Pi}|\pi|!\,A_{|\pi|},&\text{if $p$ is even},\\[4.30554pt]
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(3.36)

Recall that
\mathfrak{P}_{p,\mathrm{even}}^{\leq N}
is the set of partitions of
[p]
with blocks that have even number (less than or equal to
N
) of elements. Suppose now that
p
is even. We may then rewrite

\begin{split}F_{2}^{(p)}(0)&=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{p}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{pd}}\Biggl\{\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{p}\eta_{j}\Bigr)\sum_{\Pi\in\mathfrak{P}_{p,\mathrm{even}}^{\leq N}}(-1)^{|\Pi|}\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i\in\pi}\sigma_{i}=0}\left[\tilde{V}_{t}^{|\pi|}(\mathbf{x}_{\pi},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\pi}|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{|\pi|}(\mathbf{x}_{\pi},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\pi}|m,\epsilon)\right]\Biggr\}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.\end{split}

(3.37)

For
F_{1}
we need the Cauchy integral representation of the derivatives to justify the interchange of differentiation and expectation. For any
R>0
we have

F_{1}^{(q)}(0)=\frac{q!}{2\pi i}\oint_{\partial B_{R}(0)}\frac{F_{1}(z)}{z^{q+1}}\operatorname{d}\!z.

(3.38)

Furthermore, if
R<t^{*}
, where
t^{*}
is defined in
Equation

2.66
, then Fubini’s theorem
(justified by the proof of the first claim in
Theorem

2.1
, more precisely the discussion around
Equation

2.47
, and

Lemma

3.2
) yields

\begin{split}F_{1}^{(q)}(0)&=\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\bigg[\frac{d^{q}}{dz^{q}}\bigg|_{z=0}\exp\!\bigg(-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad\times\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{kd}}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{k}\eta_{i}\Bigr)\left[e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}-\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{l=1}^{k}\sigma_{l}=0}\mathbf{1}_{k\leq N}\right]\tilde{V}_{t}^{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\bigg)\bigg],\end{split}

(3.39)

where we used the Cauchy representation in the reverse direction again after interchanging the expectation and the complex line integral from
Equation

3.38
.
As in the computation for
F_{2}
, the derivatives can now be evaluated using
Faà di Bruno’s formula, without restrictions on the partitions. Consequently, after computing the derivatives we can interchange the expectation with the spatial integrals by Fubini’s theorem to obtain

\begin{split}F_{1}^{(q)}(0)&=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{q}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{qd}}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{q}\eta_{i}\Bigr)\sum_{\Pi\in\mathfrak{P}_{q}}(-1)^{|\Pi|}\prod_{\pi\in\Pi}\tilde{V}_{t}^{|\pi|}(\mathbf{x}_{\pi},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\pi}|m,\epsilon)\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[\prod_{\pi\in\Pi}\Bigl(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j\in\pi}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}-\mathbf{1}_{|\pi|\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{l\in\pi}\sigma_{l}=0}\Bigr)\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.\end{split}

(3.40)

By a suitable relabelling of the integration variables, we finally arrive at

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)&=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{L}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{Ld}}\Biggl\{\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{L}\eta_{i}\Bigr)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q+p=L\\
\text{$p$ is even}\end{subarray}}\binom{L}{p}\\
&\quad\times\bigg(\sum_{\Pi_{1}\in\mathfrak{P}_{q}}(-1)^{|\Pi_{1}|}\prod_{\pi_{1}\in\Pi_{1}}\tilde{V}_{t}^{|\pi_{1}|}(\mathbf{x}_{\pi_{1}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\pi_{1}}|m,\epsilon)\\
&\qquad\times\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[\prod_{\pi_{1}\in\Pi_{1}}\Bigl(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{j\in\pi_{1}}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}}-\mathbf{1}_{|\pi_{1}|\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{l\in\pi_{1}}\sigma_{l}=0}\Bigr)\right]\bigg)\\
&\quad\times\bigg(\sum_{\Pi_{2}\in\mathfrak{P}_{[[q+1,L]],\,\mathrm{even}}^{\leq N}}(-1)^{|\Pi_{2}|}\prod_{\pi_{2}\in\Pi_{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i\in\pi_{2}}\sigma_{i}=0}\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\left[\tilde{V}_{t}^{|\pi_{2}|}(\mathbf{x}_{\pi_{2}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\pi_{2}}|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{|\pi_{2}|}(\mathbf{x}_{\pi_{2}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\pi_{2}}|m,\epsilon)\right]\bigg)\Biggr\}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.\end{split}

(3.41)

Finally, the uniform bound on the integrals
\mathcal{M}_{L}
follows from the fact that

z\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta|m,\epsilon)
is entire, and previous uniform bounds. By the Cauchy integral formula, for
any
R>0
,

\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)=\frac{L!}{2\pi i}\oint_{\partial B_{R}(0)}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,w\eta|m,\epsilon)}{w^{L+1}}\,\mathrm{d}w.

(3.42)

Choosing
R=\delta^{-1}
for
\delta>0
, we obtain

|\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)|\leq\frac{L!}{2\pi}\oint_{\partial B_{\delta^{-1}}(0)}\frac{|\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,w\eta|m,\epsilon)|}{|w|^{L+1}}\,\mathrm{d}w\leq B(\delta,M,\Lambda)\,\delta^{L}\,L!,

(3.43)

where we used the first part of
Proposition

3.5
. Furthermore,
M>0
and the compact set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
are such that
\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})}<M
and
\supp(\eta)\subset\Lambda\times\{-1,1\}
.
∎

3.3
Convergence of the renormalized partition function: proof of
Theorem

3.1

The last ingredient needed for the proof of
Theorem

3.1

is the existence of the pointwise limits of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
at distinct points, that is,
for
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
whenever
i\neq j
in

(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{L},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{L})
, as well as the convergence
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)
to the corresponding integrals of these pointwise limits.

More precisely, define

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta)&:=\sum_{\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{L}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L=n+\sum_{k=1}^{N/2}2kj_{k}\\
n,j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{k}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\binom{L}{n,2j_{1},4j_{2},\dots,Nj_{N/2}}(-1)^{n}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{N/2}\frac{(-1)^{j_{l}}}{[(2l)!]^{j_{l}}j_{l}!}\right)\\
&\quad\times\left(\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}\prod_{l_{k}=1}^{j_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sum_{j\in\pi([[n+(l_{k}-1)2k+1,n+2l_{k}k]])}\sigma_{j}=0\}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{a=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{\pi(a)}\varphi_{\pi(a)}}:\right]\\
&\quad\times\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}\prod_{l_{k}=1}^{j_{k}}\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{p_{l_{k}}}\varphi_{p_{l_{k}}}}:\mid p_{l_{k}}\in\pi([[n+2(l_{k}-1)k+1,n+2l_{k}k]])\right],\end{split}

(3.44)

where

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\right]:=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\right]

(3.45)

and the corresponding truncated expectation is defined via the analogous limit.

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta|m)
is defined similarly by replacing
\mathbb{E}
and
\mathbb{E}^{T}

above with
\mathbb{E}_{m}
and
\mathbb{E}_{m}^{T}
, respectively, and omitting the limit
m\to 0
.

Lemma 3.7

For
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
whenever
i\neq j
, we have

\begin{split}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta|m,\epsilon)&=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta|m),\\
\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta|m,\epsilon)&=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta),\end{split}

(3.46)

and
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta)=0
for odd
L
.

Furthermore, if
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
for
d=1
, or
\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
for
d\geq 2
,
the limit

\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta):=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta|m,\epsilon)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{L}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{Ld}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{L}\eta_{i}\right)\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

(3.47)

exists and is finite.

Finally, suppose that
\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)

depend on complex parameters
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, in a manner similar to that in the 4th statement of
Theorem

3.1
. That is, for every compact
K\subset\mathbb{C}^{k}
, there exists a compact set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the following three statements hold:

\bigcup_{\alpha\in K}\supp(\eta_{\alpha})\subset\Lambda\times\{-1,1\}\quad\text{ and }\quad\bigcup_{\alpha\in K}\bigcup_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}\supp(\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon))\subset\Lambda\times\{-1,1\},

(3.48)

\sup_{\alpha\in K}\left\lVert\eta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})}<\infty\quad\text{ and }\quad\sup_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{\alpha\in K}\left\lVert\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})}<\infty,

(3.49)

and

\lim_{m\to 0}\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{\alpha\in K}\left\lVert\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)-\eta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})}=0.

(3.50)

Then

\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{\alpha\in K}|\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)-\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha})|=0

(3.51)

with

\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha}):=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{L}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{Ld}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{L}\eta_{\alpha}(x_{i},\sigma_{i})\right)\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},

(3.52)

and this quantity is finite. Analogous results hold for fixed
m
.

Proof.

The first claim follows directly from
Lemmas

B.3
and
B.4
,
and the representation

Equation

3.19
. The parity claim also follows from

Lemma

B.3
and from the fact that

n
in the expression is odd whenever
L
is odd. Indeed, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{a=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{a}\varphi_{a}}:\right]=0

(3.53)

unless
\sum_{a=1}^{n}\sigma_{a}=0
.

We now turn to the remaining claims and first consider the case
d\geq 2
.
The strategy in this case is to derive an integrable majorant and then apply
dominated convergence. Using the second representation

Equation

3.20
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}
, we see that

\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}\right|
is bounded by a finite combinatorial sum of terms of the
form

\begin{split}\prod_{i}&\left|\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{|A_{i}|}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{t}^{|A_{i}|}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}|m,\epsilon)\right|\\
&\times\prod_{j}\left|\tilde{V}_{t}^{|\tilde{A}_{j}|}(\mathbf{x}_{\tilde{A}_{j}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\tilde{A}_{j}}|m,\epsilon)\right|\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[\prod_{j}\left|e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l_{j}\in\tilde{A}_{j}}\sigma_{l_{j}}\varphi_{l_{j}}}-\mathbf{1}_{|\tilde{A}_{j}|\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{k_{j}\in\tilde{A}_{j}}\sigma_{k_{j}}=0}\right|\right],\end{split}

(3.54)

where

\left(\biguplus_{i}A_{i}\right)\biguplus\left(\biguplus_{j}\tilde{A}_{j}\right)=[L]

(3.55)

and
\uplus
denotes disjoint union.

Recall that we work within a compact set (since
\eta
is compactly supported). An integrable majorant is then obtained using the bounds
Equations

2.30
and
2.31
from
Corollary

2.10
for the terms corresponding to the sets
A_{i}
, and using
Lemma

2.13
followed by Hölder inequality applied to the expectation, the functions
h_{t}^{|\tilde{A}_{j}|}
from
Proposition

2.7
and
Lemma

3.2
for the terms corresponding to the sets
\tilde{A}_{j}
.
Lemma

3.2
ensures that
\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}[\left\lVert\gradient\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(F\times\{-1,1\})}]
is uniformly bounded in
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
for any compact
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since the sets
A_{i}
and
\tilde{A}_{j}
are distinct the above argument yields an integrable majorant for each of the sets separately. Consequently, we may take the pointwise limit in the first representation

Equation

3.19
and obtain the second claim from the
first one. The same argument applies to the final claim with the parameters
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
due to the assumptions.

For the case
d=1
, more care is required, since there is no integrable majorant
for the terms corresponding to the sets
A_{i}
in the above discussion for
d\geq 2
, as discussed after

Corollary

2.10
. Nevertheless, even without taking absolute values, the integrals factor into those corresponding to the sets
A_{i}
and those corresponding to
\tilde{A}_{j}
as above, and so do the limits
m,\epsilon\to 0
. Furthermore, the part corresponding to the sets
A_{i}
factors further for each separate set
A_{i}
. Then
Corollary

2.10
implies that the integral
corresponding to each set
A_{i}
converges. The limit is given by the integral in which we simply take pointwise limits of the integrands, and these pointwise limits exist by the discussion in

Remark

2.6
.

Moreover, the terms corresponding to the
sets
\tilde{A}_{j}
do admit integrable majorants by the same argument as in the
case
d\geq 2
above. Note that here we can work with
t<m^{-2}
. It therefore remains to show that the pointwise limits of these terms exist.
The pointwise limits of the factors
\tilde{V}_{t}^{|\tilde{A}_{j}|}
exist by

Remark

2.6
. For the remaining term, we expand the
product in order to define the limit. Specifically, we have

\begin{split}\lim_{m\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}&\left[\prod_{j=1}^{M}\Bigl(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l_{j}\in\tilde{A}_{j}}\sigma_{l_{j}}\varphi_{l_{j}}}-\mathbf{1}_{|\tilde{A}_{j}|\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{a_{j}\in\tilde{A}_{j}}\sigma_{a_{j}}=0}\Bigr)\right]\\
&:=\sum_{F\uplus G=[M]}(-1)^{|F|}\left(\prod_{k\in F}\mathbf{1}_{|\tilde{A}_{k}|\leq N}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i\in\tilde{A}_{k}}\sigma_{i}=0}\right)\lim_{m\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l\in G}\sum_{a_{l}\in\tilde{A}_{l}}\sigma_{a_{l}}\varphi_{a_{l}}}\right],\end{split}

(3.56)

where the sum runs over all ordered bipartitions
\{F,G\}
of the set
[M]
,
including the trivial ones
\{[M],\emptyset\}
and
\{\emptyset,[M]\}
.
The final limit exists by

Lemma

B.5
.

By linearity, we therefore obtain

\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{L}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{Ld}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{L}\eta_{i}\right)\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta|m,\epsilon)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{L}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{Ld}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{L}\eta_{i}\right)\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},

(3.57)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta)
denotes the limit of

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\beta|m,\epsilon)
taken termwise in the second
representation
Equation

3.20
, as described above.
Since the two representations agree for every
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
, their limits must
also coincide, that is,
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{*}\equiv\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
. This completes the proof. The case involving parameters
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
follows again
immediately from the assumptions as in the case
d\geq 2
.
∎

With this at hand, we can finally prove
Theorem

3.1
. Now that we have our estimates in place, the proof is analogous to that of
[
6
, Theorem 5.1]
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

It is enough to consider only the statements with both limits, since the cases with fixed
m
are analogous, and it also suffices to consider the case with parameters
\alpha
. We can modify the proof of the bound
Equation

3.22
to the case with
\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)
by using the first claim of
Proposition

3.5
with

\sup_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{\alpha\in K}\left\lVert\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})}<M

(3.58)

instead of
\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})}<M
for
\alpha\in K
with fixed compact set
K\subset\mathbb{C}^{k}
. Note that the condition
Equation

3.58
makes sense by our assumptions. Then for all
\delta>0
and
M>0
such that
Equation

3.58
holds we have the bound
\sup_{\alpha\in K}|\mathcal{M}_{L}(\eta_{\alpha},\beta)|\leq C(\delta,M,K)\delta^{L}L!
. In particular, by choosing
0<\delta\equiv\delta(z)
small enough we obtain that

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta_{\alpha}):=\sum_{L=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{L}}{L!}\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha}),

(3.59)

where
\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha})
is defined in
Lemma

3.7
, converges absolutely (and uniformly in
\alpha\in K
) for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
, and so defines an analytic function such that for fixed
r>0
we also have

\sup_{|z|<r}\sup_{\alpha\in K}|\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta_{\alpha})|<\infty.

(3.60)

The proof of the second claim is finished by noting that
\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z\eta_{\alpha})
is even, because
\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha})
is zero if
L
is odd. The positivity in the third claim is implied by the second part of
Proposition

3.5
using
Equation

3.58
.

Finally, to obtain convergence, let
A\geq 1
and fix again a compact
K\subset\mathbb{C}^{k}
and assume
\alpha\in K
. Then, using
Equation

3.59
as a Taylor expansion at zero with
z=1
, the Cauchy formula for derivatives, and simple triangle-inequality estimates, we have

\begin{split}|\mathcal{Z}_{R}&(\beta,\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)-\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha})|\\
&\leq\sum_{L=0}^{A}\frac{1}{L!}|\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)-\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha})|\\
&\quad+\sum_{L=A+1}^{\infty}\bigg(\oint_{\partial B_{r}(0)}\frac{|\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,w\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)|}{|w|^{L+1}}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}|w|}{2\pi}+\oint_{\partial B_{r}(0)}\frac{|\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,w\eta_{\alpha})|}{|w|^{L+1}}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}|w|}{2\pi}\bigg)\\
&\leq\sum_{L=1}^{A}\frac{1}{L!}|\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)-\mathcal{M}_{L}(\beta,\eta_{\alpha})|\\
&\quad+\left(\sup_{m,\epsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{|w|=r}|\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,w\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)|+\sup_{|w|=r}|\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,w\eta_{\alpha})|\right)r^{-A}\end{split}

(3.61)

for any
r>1
. The uniform convergence follows from the uniform bounds for the partition function and the uniform convergence of
\mathcal{M}_{L}
in
Proposition

3.5
(augmented with
Equation

3.58
) and
Lemma

3.7
respectively , by first taking the limits
m,\epsilon\to 0
and then
A\to\infty
. This proves the last claim.
∎

4
Correlation functions and the existence of the field

In this section, we consider our actual sine-Gordon model, that is, we put

\eta=-z\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}
, where
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a compact set, in the generalized formalism of
Sections

2
and
3
. After the crucial
Cameron–Martin–Girsanov transformation, we choose an
\eta
depending on certain complex
parameters and write everything in terms of
\mathcal{Z}_{R}
so that we can utilize results
from the previous section. Indeed, after this, everything will follow from

Theorem

3.1
and the results in

Section

2
and Appendix

B
.

We can carry out the preliminary analysis for the proofs of

C
and
B

simultaneously. Consider the mappings
\mathbb{C}^{n+k+1}\to\mathbb{C}

\begin{split}&(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z)\mapsto\log(\E_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\eps)}\abr{\exp(\sum_{j=1}^n\mu_j\eps^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_j\vp}(f_j)+\sum_{l=1}^k\nu_l[D_l\vp](g_l))})\\
&:=\log(\!\![\Zcal_0(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\eps)]^{-1}\E_{m,\eps}\!\! \abr{\exp(\sum_{j=1}^n\mu_j\eps^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_j\vp}(f_j)+\sum_{l=1}^k\nu_l[D_l\vp](g_l))e^{2z\int_{\Lambda}\eps^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\cos(\sqrt{\beta}\vp(x))\dx}}),\end{split}

(4.1)

and
\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}

(w,z)\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}\left[e^{w\varphi(f)}\right]:=[\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)]^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[e^{w\varphi(f)}e^{2z\int_{\Lambda}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\cos(\sqrt{\beta}\vp(x))\operatorname{d}\!x}\right]

(4.2)

Since the field
\varphi_{m,\epsilon}
, its derivatives, and the integral against a test
function
\varphi(f)
are Gaussian, and other relevant random variables are bounded,
these functions are analytic separately in each variable
\mu_{j},\nu_{l},w
in some
open disc centered at the origin of
\mathbb{C}
, which may a priori depend on
m,\epsilon,z
, and
\Lambda
. Indeed, the argument of the logarithm in the first map is analytic and especially continuous. Furthermore, it equals
1
at
\mu_{j}=0=\nu_{l}
for all
j,l
and
z=0
. Therefore, for small enough disc it is especially strictly positive so that we may choose an analytic branch of the logarithm. Thus, the first
mapping is also a jointly analytic function in some neighborhood of the origin in

\mathbb{C}^{n+k}
. Differentiating it with respect to the parameters
\mu_{j},\nu_{l}
and
setting them to zero yields the truncated correlation functions (cumulants).

We wish to find a larger domain, independent of
m
and
\epsilon
, in which the
functions are also analytic, and such that the convergence obtained by first
taking
\epsilon\to 0
and then
m\to 0
is uniform in all variables

\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},w
, and
z
. This implies that the limiting
function, together with all of its derivatives, is also analytic, now also with
respect to
z
.

We now apply the Cameron–Martin–Girsanov theorem as in the proofs of

[
6
, Lemma 2.6, Theorem 3.1]
. The application here is analogous to the
proof of
[
6
, Theorem 3.1]
. For justification of its use in the complex case, see also
the proof of
[
6
, Lemma 2.6]
. We need it in the following form. Let

(\varphi(x))_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
be a smooth Gaussian field and
Y
a complex-valued Gaussian random variable
that is measurable with respect to
\sigma(\varphi)
, that is, the sigma-algebra
generated by the field
\varphi
. Then, for a suitable function
F
of the field for
which the expectation exists, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[F(\varphi)e^{Y-\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}]}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[F(\varphi+\mathbb{E}(Y\varphi))\right].

(4.3)

An obvious generalization holds for more than one random variable
Y
. Above,

Y\varphi
denotes the random function
x\mapsto Y\varphi(x)
. This case is covered by

[
15
, Theorem 2.8]
.

Thus, we obtain for the expectation in the first mapping

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}&\left[\exp(\sum_{j=1}^n\mu_j\eps^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_j\vp}(f_j)+\sum_{l=1}^k\nu_l[D_l\vp](g_l))\right]\\
&=\exp(\half\E_{m,\eps}\abr{\rbr{\sum_{l=1}^k\nu_l[D_l\vp](g_l)}^2})\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)^{-1}\\
&\quad\times\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\bigg[\exp\bigg(\int_{\R^d}
\bigg\{z\1_{\Lambda}(x)\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\eps^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma\vp(x)}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma\sum_{l=1}^n\nu_l\E_{m,\eps}[\vp(x)[D_l\vp](g_l)]}
\\
\hfil\lx@alignment@column@after&\qquad\qquad+\sum_{j=1}^n\mu_jf_j(x)\eps^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_j\vp(x)}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_j\sum_{l=1}^k\nu_l\E_{m,\eps}[\vp(x)[D_l\vp](g_l)])\bigg\}\operatorname{d}\!x\bigg)\bigg],\end{split}}

(4.4)

where we have set

Y:=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\nu_{l}[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})

(4.5)

and

F(\varphi):=\exp(\int_{\R^d}\eps^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}
\rbr{\sum_{j=1}^n\mu_jf_j(x)e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_j\vp_{m,\eps}(x)}
+\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}z\1_\Lambda(x) e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma\vp_{m,\eps}(x)}}\dx).

(4.6)

We can now rewrite the exponential inside the expectation in the last factor of

Equation

4.4
in a form that
allows us to use the last part of
Theorem

3.1
,
namely,

\exp(-\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\int_{\R^d}
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(x,\sigma|m,\eps)
\eps^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma\vp_{m,\eps}(x)}\dx),

(4.7)

where

\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(x,\sigma|m,\epsilon):=-z\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}(x)e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma\sum_{l=1}^{k}\nu_{l}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x)[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{\sigma,\sigma_{j}}\mu_{j}f_{j}(x)e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\nu_{l}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x)[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]}.

(4.8)

For the second mapping, using
Y:=w\varphi_{m,\epsilon}(f)
and

F(\varphi):=\exp(z\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\int_{\Lambda}
e^{i\sigma\sqrt{\beta}\vp_{m,\eps}(x)}\dx)
, we obtain similarly

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}\left[e^{w\varphi(f)}\right]&=\exp(\half w^2\E_{m,\eps}[(\vp(f))^2])\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)^{-1}\\
&\quad\times\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\exp(-\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\int_{\R^d}
\eps^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\eta_{w,z,\Lambda}(x,\sigma|m,\eps)
e^{i\sigma\sqrt{\beta}\vp(x)}\dx)\right],\end{split}

(4.9)

where

\eta_{w,z,\Lambda}(x,\sigma|m,\epsilon):=-z\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}(x)e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma w\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x)\varphi(f)]}.

(4.10)

We are now ready for the proofs of

B
and
C
.

4.1
Proof of
B

We have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{k}[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})\right]\\
&=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{j}}\bigg|_{\mu_{j}=0}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{l}}\bigg|_{\nu_{l}=0}\log\mathopen{\left\{\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.}\mathcal{Z}_{0}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)^{-1}\\
&\quad\times\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\exp(-\!\!\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\int_{\R^d}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(x,\sigma|m,\eps)\eps^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma\vp(x)}\dx)\right]\exp(\half\E_{m,\eps}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^k\nu_l[D_l\vp](g_l)\bigg)^2\bigg])\mathclose{\left\}\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.}.\end{split}

(4.11)

We can also rewrite the argument of the logarithm above in terms of the renormalized partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{R}

\begin{split}&\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)}{\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}\\
&\times\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}e^{\frac{1}{(2k)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2kd}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2k}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(x_{i},\sigma_{i}|\epsilon)\right)\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mid j\in[2k]\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}\\
&\times\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}e^{\frac{-z^{2k}}{(2k)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\Lambda^{2k}}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mid j\in[2k]\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}\\
&\times\exp(\half\E_{m,\eps}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^k\nu_l[D_l\vp](g_l)\bigg)^2\bigg]).\end{split}

(4.12)

To use
Theorem

3.1
we need the limit

\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(x,\sigma):=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(x,\sigma|m,\epsilon),

which exists, and is given by making the replacement

\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\varphi(x)[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})\right]\to\mathbb{E}\left[\varphi(x)[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})\right]=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[D_{l}g_{l}](y)\log(|x-y|)\operatorname{d}\!y,

(4.13)

to
Equation

4.8
. The fixed
m>0
case is analogous.

The convergence of
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}
is in

L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
by
Lemma

B.1

and it is uniform in
\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z\in K\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+k+1}
for compact
K
. Furthermore, we have

\bigcup_{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z)\in K}\supp(\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon))\subset\overline{\Lambda\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\supp(f_{j})},

(4.14)

which is compact, and

\sup_{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z)\in K}\left\lVert\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})}\leq\sup_{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z)\in K}\left(|z|+\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\mu_{j}|\left\lVert f_{j}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})}\right)<\infty.

(4.15)

Therefore, by
Theorem

3.1
we have

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)\;\longrightarrow\;\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot)).

(4.16)

Furthermore, by the same argument as in the beginning of this section, that is,
boundedness of relevant random variables, the function

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)

extends to an entire function in
\mathbb{C}^{n+k+1}
.
The uniform convergence implies that the limit

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot))
is also entire.

For
n>N
, the only relevant contribution in
Equation

4.12
comes from the term

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)

since other terms vanish when we take logarithmic derivatives and set the variables to zero.
Thus, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}^{T}&\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{k}\varphi^{\prime}(g_{l})\right]\\
&=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{j}}\bigg|_{\mu_{j}=0}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{l}}\bigg|_{\nu_{l}=0}\log\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)\right).\end{split}

(4.17)

Part 3 of
Proposition

3.5
implies that

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{0,0,z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)>0

uniformly in
m
and
\epsilon
and for all
|z|<M
with arbitrary fixed
M>0
.
Therefore, there exists some open neighborhood
U
of
the real axis
\mathbb{R}
in
\mathbb{C}
such that

\log\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)\right)\;\longrightarrow\;\log\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot))\right),

(4.18)

when we first take
\epsilon\to 0
and then
m\to 0
,
uniformly in
[D(K)]^{n+k}\times K
, where
K\subset U
is compact and
D(K)
is some open disk in
\mathbb{C}
centered at the origin.

This also implies that the limit is analytic in
[B_{r(R)}(0)]^{n+k}\times(B_{R}(0)\cap U)\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+k+1}
for some
r(R)>0
with all
R>0
.
The derivatives are then also analytic and converge in the same manner in the same domain.
Thus, we have proven that the correlation functions corresponding to
n>N
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

converge and are given by

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda)}^{n,k}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{j}}\bigg|_{\mu_{j}=0}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{l}}\bigg|_{\nu_{l}=0}\log\!\left(\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda})\right).

(4.19)

Furthermore, they are analytic in all of
U
since the above argument holds for all
R>0
.

We can also compute the derivative of arbitrary order with respect to
z
at zero of these
correlation functions.
Indeed, since

\log(\Zcal_R(\beta,\eta_{\Bold{\mu},\Bold{\nu},z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\eps)|m,\eps))

is analytic, the derivatives exist, and by retracing our argument we can compute these
directly using the representation of cumulants in terms of moments

Equation

A.5
and the boundedness of the relevant random variables.
Thus, we obtain

\begin{split}\frac{d^{q}}{dz^{q}}\bigg|_{z=0}&\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{q}[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})\right]\\
&=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\{-1,1\}^{q}}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{q}[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}\prod_{a=1}^{q}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\tau_{a}\varphi}(\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda})\right]\\
&\overset{m\to 0,\;\epsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}(f_{j}):\prod_{l=1}^{q}[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})\left(:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\varphi}(\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}):+:e^{-i\sqrt{\beta}\varphi}(\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}):\right)^{q}\right],\end{split}

(4.20)

where the expectation is with respect to the law of the free field and the convergence
follows by
Proposition

B.9
.
Thus, we are done with the proof of the three parts of

B
for
n>N
.

Let us then consider the trickier cases
n\leq N
. The case
d\geq 2
and
N=2
is
completely analogous to the proof of
[
6
, Theorem 3.1 (i)–(iii)]
for the cases

n=0,1,2
, provided we use our

Lemmas

B.1
and
B.9

instead of
[
6
, Lemmas 2.9 and 2.10]
. When we consider the case
d=1
, we retain all
notations for general dimension
d
, even though the computations are only relevant for

d=1
in this paper. The computations remain valid in higher dimensions, in particular for

d=2
and
\beta\in[6\pi,8\pi)
before taking the limit. Thus, if in the future the
convergence in

Proposition

B.9

can be improved to a wider range of
\beta
, our computations here will yield the
correlation functions also in higher dimensions.

Now we consider the case
d=1
. When
1\leq n\leq N
, we must also take logarithmic
derivatives of the factor on the second row of

Equation

4.12
. For
n=0
, the factor on the last row of
Equation

4.12
also contributes. However, using
Lemma

B.1
, we readily see that the
contribution from this last factor has a finite limit.

We first treat the cases
n=1,2,\dots,N
. We need
to compute
