\begin{split}\prod_{j=1}^{n}&\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{j}}\bigg|_{\mu_{j}=0}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{l}}\bigg|_{\nu_{l}=0}\sum_{p=1}^{N/2}\frac{1}{(2p)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2p}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2p}\sigma_{i}=0}\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2pd}}\Bigl(\prod_{a=1}^{2p}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(x_{a},\sigma_{a}|m,\epsilon)\Bigr)\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{b}\varphi(x_{b})}\mid b\in[2p]\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.\end{split}

(4.21)

Since for fixed
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
everything is bounded and
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}
has compact support, the
main task is to compute

\begin{split}\prod_{j=1}^{n}&\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{j}}\bigg|_{\mu_{j}=0}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{l}}\bigg|_{\nu_{l}=0}\prod_{a=1}^{2p}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(x_{a},\sigma_{a}|m,\epsilon)\\
&=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{j}}\bigg|_{\mu_{j}=0}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{l}}\bigg|_{\nu_{l}=0}\sum_{J\subset[2p]}\Bigl(\prod_{a\in J}z\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}(x_{a})e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{a}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\nu_{l}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{a})[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]}\Bigr)\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\Bigl(\prod_{b\in J^{c}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{\sigma_{b},\sigma_{j}}\mu_{j}f_{j}(x_{b})e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\nu_{l}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{b})[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]}\Bigr).\end{split}

(4.22)

Unless
|J^{c}|=n
, the resulting term is zero. Moreover, each
\mu_{j}
must appear exactly
once. Computing the
\mu
-derivatives first, we obtain

\begin{split}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{j}}\bigg|_{\mu_{j}=0}&\Bigl(\prod_{b\in J^{c},\ |J^{c}|=n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{\sigma_{b},\sigma_{j}}\mu_{j}f_{j}(x_{b})e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\nu_{l}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{b})[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]}\Bigr)\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathbb{S}_{J^{c}}\\
J^{c}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{\sigma_{\tau(b_{j})},\sigma_{j}}f_{j}(x_{\tau(b_{j})})e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\nu_{l}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{\tau(b_{j})})[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]}.\end{split}

(4.23)

Next we compute the
\nu
-derivatives to obtain

\begin{split}\prod_{j=1}^{n}&\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{j}}\bigg|_{\mu_{j}=0}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{l}}\bigg|_{\nu_{l}=0}\prod_{a=1}^{2p}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}(x_{a},\sigma_{a}|m,\epsilon)\\
&=z^{2p-n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subset[2p]\\
|J^{c}|=n\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathbb{S}_{J^{c}}\\
J^{c}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})\end{subarray}}\Bigl(\prod_{a\in J}\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}(x_{a})\Bigr)\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{\sigma_{\tau(b_{j})},\sigma_{j}}f_{j}(x_{\tau(b_{j})})\Bigr)\\
&\qquad\times\prod_{l=1}^{k}i\sqrt{\beta}\Bigl(\sum_{a\in J}\sigma_{a}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{a})[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{\tau(b_{j})})[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]\Bigr).\end{split}

(4.24)

Finally, by linearity and

Proposition

B.9
,
for
1\leq n\leq N
we obtain

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda)}^{n,k}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})&=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{j}}\bigg|_{\mu_{j}=0}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{l}}\bigg|_{\nu_{l}=0}\log\!\bigl(\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda})\bigr)\\
&\quad+\sum_{p=1}^{N/2}\frac{z^{2p-n}\mathbf{1}_{2p-n\geq 0}}{(2p)!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subset[2p]\\
|J^{c}|=n\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathbb{S}_{J^{c}}\\
J^{c}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2p}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2p}\sigma_{i}=0}\\
&\qquad\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2pd}}\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{q}\varphi(x_{q})}:\mid q\in[2p]\right]\Bigl(\prod_{a\in J}\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}(x_{a})\Bigr)\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{\tau(b_{j})}\sigma_{j}f_{j}(x_{\tau(b_{j})})\Bigr)\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{l=1}^{k}i\sqrt{\beta}\Bigl(\sum_{a\in J}\sigma_{a}\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{a})[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{\tau(b_{j})})[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.\end{split}

(4.25)

The term starting from the second row above is just a polynomial in
z
with complicated coefficients, hence it is entire in
z
. The
third claim follows exactly as in the case
n>N
.

For
n=0
, the factor on the second row of
Equation

4.12
contributes

\begin{split}\prod_{l=1}^{k}&\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{l}}\bigg|_{\nu_{l}=0}\Biggl(\sum_{p=1}^{N/2}\frac{1}{(2p)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2p}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2p}\sigma_{i}=0}\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2pd}}\biggl\{\left(\prod_{a=1}^{2p}(-z)\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda}(x_{a})e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{a}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\nu_{l}\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{a})[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{b}\varphi(x_{b})}\mid b\in[2p]\right]\biggr\}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\Biggr)\\
&=\sum_{p=1}^{N/2}\frac{z^{2p}}{(2p)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2p}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2p}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\Lambda^{2p}}\biggl\{\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{b}\varphi(x_{b})}\mid b\in[2p]\right]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{l=1}^{k}i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{a=1}^{2p}\sigma_{a}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{a})[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]\biggr\}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\end{split}

(4.26)

since

\eta_{0,\nu,z,\Lambda}(x,\sigma|m,\epsilon)=-z\mathbf{1}_{\{x\in\Lambda\}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma\sum_{l=1}^{k}\nu_{l}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x)[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]}.

(4.27)

The term on the last row of
Equation

4.12
contributes

\prod_{l=1}^{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{l}}\bigg|_{\nu_{l}=0}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\Bigl[\Bigl(\sum_{a=1}^{k}\nu_{a}[D_{a}\varphi](g_{a})\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr]=\mathbf{1}_{k=2}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[[D_{1}\varphi](g_{1})[D_{2}\varphi](g_{2})],

(4.28)

which converges by

Lemma

B.1
as already mentioned.

Thus, again by linearity and

Proposition

B.9
,
the
n=0
correlation function becomes

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,z,\Lambda)}^{T}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{k}[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})\right]&=\prod_{l=1}^{k}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{l}}\bigg|_{\nu_{l}=0}\log(\Zcal_R(\beta,\eta_{0,\boldsymbol{\nu},z,\Lambda}))\\
&\quad+\sum_{p=1}^{N/2}\frac{z^{2p}}{(2p)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2p}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2p}\sigma_{i}=0}\\
&\qquad\times\int_{\Lambda^{2p}}\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{a}\varphi(x_{a})}:\mid a\in[2p]\right]\prod_{l=1}^{k}i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{b=1}^{2p}\sigma_{b}\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{b})[D_{l}\varphi](g_{l})]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&\quad+\mathbf{1}_{k=2}\mathbb{E}[[D_{1}\varphi](g_{1})[D_{2}\varphi](g_{2})].\end{split}

(4.29)

The analyticity and the third claim follow analogously to the previous cases.
This concludes the proof.

4.2
Proof of
C

Analogously to the case of the correlation functions, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{sG}(z,\beta,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}\left[e^{w\varphi(f)}\right]&=\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{w,z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)}{\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,z,\Lambda|m,\epsilon)}\\
&\times\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}e^{\frac{1}{(2k)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2k}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2k}\eta_{w,z,\Lambda}(x_{i},\sigma_{i}|\epsilon)\right)\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mid j\in[2k]\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}\\
&\times\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}e^{\frac{-z^{2k}}{(2k)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\Lambda^{2k}}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mid j\in[2k]\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}\\
&\times\exp\!\left(\frac{w^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[(\varphi(f))^{2}]\right).\end{split}

(4.30)

Here
\eta_{w,z,\Lambda}(x,\sigma|m,\epsilon)
is given by
Equation

4.10
. We consider the limit
m,\epsilon\to 0
with
\epsilon\to 0
taken first; the fixed-mass case is analogous.

For
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\int f=0
, the factor on the last row of the right-hand side of
Equation

4.30
converges again by
Lemma

B.1
. Moreover, by the same lemma, the convergence
\eta_{w,z,\Lambda}(x,\sigma|m,\epsilon)\to\eta_{w,z,\Lambda}(x,\sigma)
holds in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})
, uniformly for
(w,z)
in compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
,

\supp(\eta_{w,z,\Lambda}|m,\epsilon)=\Lambda\times\{-1,1\},\,\,\forall w,z\in\mathbb{C}\text{ and }m,\epsilon\in(0,1)

(4.31)

and

\sup_{(w,z)\in K}\left\lVert\eta_{w,z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1,1\})}\leq\sup_{(w,z)\in K}|z|<\infty

(4.32)

for all compact
K\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
The limit
\eta_{w,z,\Lambda}
is defined by
Equation

4.10
with the replacement

\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x)\varphi(f)]\to\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x)\varphi(f)]:=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x)\varphi(f)]=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)\log(|x-y|)\operatorname{d}\!y,

(4.33)

which exists by
Lemma

B.1
. Thus, as in the previous section,
\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{w,z,\Lambda}(\cdot,\cdot|m,\epsilon)|m,\epsilon)\to\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\beta,\eta_{w,z,\Lambda})
by
Theorem

3.1
, and the limit is an entire function of
(w,z)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

Recalling the definition of
\eta_{w,z,\Lambda}
in
Equation

4.10
and combining the second and third rows on the right-hand side of
Equation

4.30
yields

\prod_{k=1}^{N/2}e^{\frac{z^{2k}}{(2k)!}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\Lambda^{2k}}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\sigma_{l}w\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}-1\right)\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mid j\in[2k]\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}.

(4.34)

The key step in proving that
Equation

4.34
has a limit is to show that the exponent converges. For
d\geq 2
, the argument is analogous to the proof of
[
6
, Theorem 3.2]
, since
N=2
.

For
d=1
, we use results from
Section

2
. The strategy parallels the proof of
Corollary

2.10
. First, for each
k=1,\dots,N/2
, we show that the integral in the exponent, including the sum over
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, is uniformly bounded for
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Then we decompose the integral into a limit candidate and an error term, and we prove that the error term vanishes. The limit candidate is

\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\Lambda^{2k}}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\sigma_{l}w\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}-1\right)\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\mid j\in[2k]\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

(4.35)

Here

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\mid j\in[2k]\right]&:=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mid j\in[2k]\right],\end{split}

(4.36)

and the limit exists by
Lemmas

B.3
and
B.4
. An analogous statement holds without taking the limit
m\to 0
.

To control the error we write everything in terms of the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}
defined in
Equations

2.7
and
2.8
. By the third statement of
Theorem

2.1
we may write

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mid j\in[2k]\right]&=(-1)^{2k-1}\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\\
&=(-1)^{2k-1}\left(\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)+\left[\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\right]\right).\end{split}

(4.37)

Note that
\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x)\varphi(f)]
is a smooth function. However, to use an estimate similar to
Equation

2.44
for

e^{i\sqrt{\beta}w\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}-1

(4.38)

we need

\left\lVert(\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(\cdot)\varphi(f)])^{\prime}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{\Lambda})}<B,

(4.39)

for some constant
B>0
that may depend on
f
but is independent of
m
and
\epsilon
. Above,
(f(\cdot,\dots))^{\prime}
denotes differentiation with respect to the variable in place of
\cdot
and
B_{\Lambda}
is a closed ball with radius
2R(\Lambda)
, where
R(\Lambda)
is such that
\Lambda\subset B_{R(\Lambda)}(0)
. To derive this uniform estimate, we write

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dx}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x)\varphi(f)]&=\frac{d}{dx}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(y)\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\infty}C_{s}^{m}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!y\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f^{\prime}(x-u)K_{0}(m\left|u\right|)\operatorname{d}\!u-\int_{\mathbb{R}}f^{\prime}(x-u)\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{2}}e^{-m^{2}s-\frac{u^{2}}{4s}}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}\operatorname{d}\!u\\
&=:E_{m}^{1}(x)+E_{m,\epsilon}^{2}(x),\end{split}

(4.40)

where in the first step we have made the translation
u=x-y
, differentiated under the integral sign, and then added and subtracted
\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{2}}e^{-m^{2}s-u^{2}/(4s)}/(4\pi s)\operatorname{d}\!s
. The differentiation under the integral sign is permitted for non-zero
m,\epsilon
since then
\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\infty}C_{s}^{m}(0)\operatorname{d}\!s<\infty
.
The modified Bessel function
K_{0}
has the well-known asymptotics

K_{0}(x)=-\gamma-\log(\half x)+\mathcal{O}(x).

(4.41)

This yields

E_{m}^{1}(x)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f^{\prime}(x-u)\log(\abs{u})\operatorname{d}\!u+\int_{\mathbb{R}}f^{\prime}(x-u)\mathcal{O}(m\left|u\right|)\operatorname{d}\!u,

(4.42)

where the term
(-\gamma-\log(m/2))\int_{\mathbb{R}}f^{\prime}(x-u)\operatorname{d}\!u
vanished since
f
has compact support. Since
m\in(0,1)
,
f(x-u)
vanishes outside a compact set for
x\in B_{\Lambda}
,
\sup_{x\in B_{\Lambda},u\in\mathbb{R}}|f^{\prime}(x-u)|<\infty
, and the maps
x\mapsto\log(x)
and
x\mapsto x
are locally integrable,
E_{m}^{1}
is uniformly bounded in
m\in(0,1)
and
x\in B_{\Lambda}
.

For the second term, we may use size estimates, since the logarithmic mass divergence is contained in the first term. We have

\begin{split}|E_{m,\epsilon}^{2}(x)|&\leq\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f^{\prime}(x-u)|\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{4s}}\frac{ds}{s}\operatorname{d}\!u\\
&\leq\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f^{\prime}(x-u)|\left(\gamma+\left|\log(\frac{u^2}{4})\right|+\mathcal{O}(u^{2})\right)\operatorname{d}\!u\\
&\leq\mathcal{O}(1).\end{split}

(4.43)

Here we have used

\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{4s}}\frac{\operatorname{d}\!s}{s}=\int_{\frac{u^{2}}{4}}^{\infty}e^{-r}\frac{\operatorname{d}\!r}{r}=E_{1}\left(\frac{u^{2}}{4}\right)=-\gamma-\log(\frac{u^2}{4})+\int_{0}^{\frac{u^{2}}{4}}\frac{1-e^{-r}}{r}\operatorname{d}\!r\leq\gamma+\left|\log(\frac{u^2}{4})\right|+\mathcal{O}(u^{2}),

(4.44)

where
E_{1}
is the exponential integral and we have used its well-known properties. The implied constant bound on the last row of
Equation

4.43
is independent of
m\in(0,1)
and
x\in B_{\Lambda}
by the same type of argument as with
E_{m}^{1}
above. Thus,
Equation

4.39
indeed holds.

Therefore, we may use an estimate similar to
Equation

2.44
for

e^{i\sqrt{\beta}w\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}-1

with
t=1
for the term corresponding to
\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}
. For the term corresponding to
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2k}-\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}
, we simply use

\left|e^{i\sqrt{\beta}w\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}-1\right|\leq 2.

Then
Propositions

2.7
and
2.10
yield the required uniform boundedness for the terms corresponding to
\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}
and
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2k}-\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}
, respectively. Furthermore, these results also imply that these terms admit integrable majorants independent of
\epsilon
. Therefore, passing to the limit
\epsilon\to 0
through the integrals is again justified by dominated convergence. In fact, since
1<m^{-2}
for
m\in(0,1)
, the above arguments show that we can also take the limit
m\to 0
through the integrals for the term corresponding to
\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}
. Indeed, the majorant for
\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}
given by
Proposition

2.7
is independent of
m
. The pointwise limits of the functions
\tilde{V}_{t}^{2k}
exist by
Remark

2.6
.

Thus, we only need to carry out the error analysis for the term corresponding to
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2k}-\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}
. By the above argument, we may assume in the analysis below that the limit
\epsilon\to 0
has already been taken. Recall that we denote

\tilde{V}_{t}^{l}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m):=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\tilde{V}_{t}^{l}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{V}_{t}^{l}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\tilde{V}_{t}^{l}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon).

(4.45)

Then we can write

\begin{split}&\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\Lambda^{2k}}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\sigma_{l}w\mathbb{E}_{m}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}-1\right)(-1)^{2k-1}\left(\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)-\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&\quad=-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\Lambda^{2k}}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\sigma_{l}w\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}-1\right)\left(\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)-\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&\qquad-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\Lambda^{2k}}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\sigma_{l}w\mathbb{E}_{m}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}-e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\sigma_{l}w\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left(\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)-\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&\quad=:I_{1}(m)+I_{2}(m).\end{split}

(4.46)

Since
e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\sigma_{l}w\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}-1
is uniformly bounded, we obtain from the proof of
Corollary

2.10
that

\lim_{m\to 0}I_{1}(m)=-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\Lambda^{2k}}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\sigma_{l}w\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}-1\right)\left(\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

(4.47)

Indeed, by
Equation

2.153
we can write

\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)-\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)=\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})+R_{1}^{2k}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}),

(4.48)

where
R_{1}^{2k}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
is the error term from
Equation

2.154
with
t=1
. By the uniform boundedness of the
\exp(\cdot)-1
term and the error analysis in the proof of
Corollary

2.10
, the error term converges to zero.

It therefore remains to show that
\lim_{m\to 0}I_{2}(m)=0
. Following the proof of
Corollary

2.10
, we write

\begin{split}I_{2}(m)&=-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\Lambda^{2k}}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\sigma_{l}w\mathbb{E}_{m}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}-e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\sigma_{l}w\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left(\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\tilde{V}_{1}^{2k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&\quad-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{2k}}\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\sigma_{i}=0}\int_{\Lambda^{2k}}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\sigma_{l}w\mathbb{E}_{m}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}-e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{l=1}^{2k}\sigma_{l}w\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{l})\varphi(f)]}\right)R_{1}^{2k}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&=:I_{2,1}(m)+I_{2,2}(m).\end{split}

(4.49)

Both terms satisfy
\lim_{m\to 0}I_{2,1}(m)=0=\lim_{m\to 0}I_{2,2}(m)
by dominated convergence and arguments similar to those in the analysis of
I_{1}
above, since the difference of the exponentials is uniformly bounded and converges to zero pointwise almost everywhere by
Lemma

B.1
.

This completes the proof that
Equation

4.34
has a finite limit when first letting
\epsilon\to 0
and then
m\to 0
, and thus also completes the proof of
C
.

Appendix A
Definition and properties of cumulants

Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be an arbitrary collection of random variables on a common probability space. Then we can define the joint cumulants recursively. For
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{N})=:A\subset I
with
N\in\mathbb{N}
we put

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[X_{i}\mid i\in A\right]:=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i\in A}X_{i}\right]-\sum_{\Pi\in\mathfrak{P}_{A}^{\prime}}\prod_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[X_{i}\mid i\in\pi\right],\end{split}

(A.1)

where
\mathfrak{P}_{A}^{\prime}
denotes the set of proper partitions of
A
, that is, all partitions except the trivial one consisting of
A
itself. If

\log(\E\abr{e^{\sum_{l=1}^Nt_lX_{l}}})

(A.2)

exists and is analytic (for real random variables, the existence of exponential moments suffices), then for cumulants involving only
\{X_{l}\}_{l=1}^{N}
(or analogously for arbitrary subsets of the index set
I
) we may write

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[X_{n}\mid n\in[N]\right]:=\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\dots\partial_{N}\log(\E\abr{e^{\sum_{l=1}^Nt_lX_{l}}})\bigg|_{t_{i}=0,\forall i\in A},\end{split}

(A.3)

with
[N]:=\{1,2,\dots,N\}
. Above we have written
\partial_{l}:=\partial/\partial_{t_{l}}
.

Cumulants and moments satisfy the so‑called Möbius inversion relations:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i\in A}X_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\Pi\in\mathfrak{P}_{A}}\prod_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[X_{i}\mid i\in\pi\right],

(A.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[X_{i}\mid i\in A\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\Pi\in\mathfrak{P}_{A}}(|\Pi|-1)!(-1)^{|\Pi|-1}\prod_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i\in\pi}X_{i}\right],

(A.5)

where
\mathfrak{P}_{A}
is now the set of all partitions of
A
(including the trivial one), and
|B|
denotes the cardinality of the set
B
. Thus, for
B=\Pi
, it counts the number of blocks
\pi
in the partition
\Pi
.

Appendix B
Correlation functions of the free field.

In this section, we collect statements concerning the correlation functions of the free field. These correlation functions play a crucial role in the proofs of our main theorems. In addition to the usual smeared correlation functions, we will also consider pointwise correlation functions of the free field. Finally, besides the full free field, we will need certain correlation functions of the free field restricted to different scales, as explained below.

We begin by recalling that our regularized free field is a smooth centered Gaussian field
\varphi_{m,\epsilon}
with covariance

\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{m,\epsilon}(x)\varphi_{m,\epsilon}(y)]\equiv\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x)\varphi(y)]:=\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\infty}C_{s}^{m}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!s,

(B.1)

where
C_{s}^{m}(x,y):=\exp(-m^2s-|x-y|^2/4s)/(4\pi s)
. Furthermore, we denote by
\varphi_{m}
(with corresponding expectation
\mathbb{E}_{m}
) the massive free field, that is, a centered Gaussian field with covariance

\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{m}(x)\varphi_{m}(y)]\equiv\mathbb{E}_{m}[\varphi(x)\varphi(y)]:=\int_{0}^{\infty}C_{s}^{m}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!s=\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{0}(m|x-y|),

(B.2)

where
K_{0}
is the modified Bessel function of the second kind of order zero. Lastly, as in the analysis of
Section

2
, we can express the regularized free field as a sum of two independent components. More precisely, let
\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
and
\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
be independent Gaussian fields (defined on the same probability space as
\varphi_{m,\epsilon}
) with covariances

\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}[\varphi(x)\varphi(y)]:=\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{t}C_{s}^{m}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!s\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}[\varphi(x)\varphi(y)]:=\int_{t}^{\infty}C_{s}^{m}(x,y)\operatorname{d}\!s

(B.3)

respectively. Then the regularized free field admits the decomposition

\varphi_{m,\epsilon}\overset{d}{=}\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}+\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}

(B.4)

The field
\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
contains (in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
) the UV-rough component on scales below
t
, while
\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
is UV-regular.

The correlation functions we are ultimately interested in are the truncated mixed charge and gradient correlation functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{m}^{n,k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{m}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\prod_{l=1}^{k}(D_{l}\varphi)(z_{l})\right]:=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\prod_{l=1}^{k}(D_{l}\varphi)(z_{l})\right],

(B.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{n,k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\prod_{l=1}^{k}(D_{l}\varphi)(z_{l})\right]:=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\prod_{l=1}^{k}(D_{l}\varphi)(z_{l})\right].

(B.6)

We also consider the corresponding smeared correlation functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{m}^{n,k}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{m}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}:(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{k}(D_{l}\varphi)(g_{l})\right]:=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{k}(D_{l}\varphi)(g_{l})\right],

(B.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{n,k}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}:(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{k}(D_{l}\varphi)(g_{l})\right]:=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{k}(D_{l}\varphi)(g_{l})\right].

(B.8)

Here
x_{j},z_{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are pairwise distinct points,
f_{j}\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
j=1,2,\dots,n
, and
g_{l}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
l=1,2,\dots,k
. Moreover, for each fixed
l
, the operator
D_{l}
denotes either a partial derivative
\partial_{a}:=\partial/\partial x_{a}
for some
a=1,2,\dots,d
, or, in the case
d=1
, simply the derivative
d/dx
(we will denote
\varphi^{\prime}(x):=d/dx\varphi(x)
). We also note that truncated correlation functions coincide with the cumulants of the corresponding random variables.

The results collected in this appendix are analogous to those in
[
6
, Section 2]
for
d=2
, with
D_{l}=\partial
or
\overline{\partial}
(the Wirtinger derivatives). The pointwise correlation functions in arbitrary dimension follow directly from the computations therein, with the only modification being the form of the differential operators, which is straightforward to account for. The analysis of the smeared correlation functions requires some additional work in the case
d=1
.

First, we record the following lemma concerning basic correlation functions.

Lemma B.1

For arbitrary dimension
d
we have

\begin{split}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[(\varphi(x))^{2}]+\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(\eps)&=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(m)-\frac{\gamma}{4\pi},\\
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x)\varphi(y)]&=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(m)-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(\half|x-y|)-\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}+\mathcal{O}(m|x-y|),\end{split}

(B.9)

where
\gamma
denotes the Euler–Mascheroni constant.

Moreover, for dimensions
d\geq 2
we have

\begin{split}\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\varphi(x)(\partial_{j}\varphi)(y)\right]&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{x_{j}-y_{j}}{|x-y|^{2}}=-\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[(\partial_{j}\varphi)(x)\varphi(y)\right],\\
\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[(\partial_{i}\varphi)(x)(\partial_{j}\varphi)(y)\right]&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}_{i=j}}{|x-y|^{2}}-2\frac{(x_{i}-y_{i})(x_{j}-y_{j})}{|x-y|^{4}}\right)=:\frac{1}{2\pi}A_{ij}(x-y).\end{split}

(B.10)

For
d=1
we instead obtain

\begin{split}\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\varphi(x)\varphi^{\prime}(y)\right]&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{x-y}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{\operatorname{sgn}(x-y)}{|x-y|}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{x-y}{|x-y|^{2}}=-\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\varphi^{\prime}(x)\varphi(y)\right],\\
\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\varphi^{\prime}(x)\varphi^{\prime}(y)\right]&=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}}.\end{split}

(B.11)

All limits are uniform on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
avoiding the diagonal
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid x=y\}
. Here
\operatorname{sgn}
denotes the sign function, taking the value
1
for nonnegative arguments and
-1
for negative ones.

For
g\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we further have

\begin{split}\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(u)(\partial_{j}\varphi)(g)]&:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(x)\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{u_{j}-x_{j}}{|u-x|^{2}}\operatorname{d}\!x,\qquad(d\geq 2),\\
\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(u)\varphi^{\prime}(g)]&:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(x)\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{u-x}{|u-x|^{2}}\operatorname{d}\!x,\qquad(d=1),\end{split}

(B.12)

(with differentiation understood in the classical sense) uniformly on compact subsets of
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
d\geq 2
and of
u\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\supp(g)
for
d=1
.

Similarly, for
f,g\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with disjoint supports,

\begin{split}\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[(\partial_{i}\varphi)(f)(\partial_{j}\varphi)(g)]&:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f(x)g(y)\frac{1}{2\pi}A_{ij}(x-y)\operatorname{d}\!x\operatorname{d}\!y,\qquad(d\geq 2),\\
\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi^{\prime}(f)\varphi^{\prime}(g)]&:=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f(x)g(y)\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}}\operatorname{d}\!x\operatorname{d}\!y,\qquad(d=1).\end{split}

(B.13)

To relax the assumption that
u
avoids the support of
g
in the one-dimensional case, or that
f
and
g
have disjoint supports, we may instead interpret derivatives in the distributional sense. In this case, for
f,g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(u)\varphi^{\prime}(g)]:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g^{\prime}(y)\log(|u-y|)\operatorname{d}\!y=\PV\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(x)\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{u-x}\operatorname{d}\!x,

(B.14)

uniformly on compact subsets of
u\in\mathbb{R}
, and

\begin{split}\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[(\partial_{i}\varphi)(f)(\partial_{j}\varphi)(g)]&:=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(\partial_{i}f)(x)(\partial_{j}g)(y)\log(|x-y|)\operatorname{d}\!x\operatorname{d}\!y\\
\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi^{\prime}(f)\varphi^{\prime}(g)]&:=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f^{\prime}(x)g^{\prime}(y)\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(|x-y|)\operatorname{d}\!x\operatorname{d}\!y,\qquad(d=1).\end{split}

(B.15)

Finally, for
f\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying
\int f=0
, we also have

\begin{split}\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(u)\varphi(f)]&=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(|x-u|)\operatorname{d}\!x,\\
\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[(\varphi(f))^{2}]&=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f(x)f(y)\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(|x-y|)\operatorname{d}\!x\operatorname{d}\!y,\end{split}

(B.16)

uniformly on compact subsets of
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Remark B.2
.

Analogous limits with fixed
m
also exist and enjoy the same local uniformity. These can be computed explicitly using the differentiation rules for the modified Bessel functions.

Proof.

The basic ingredients are the convergence

\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x)\varphi(y)]\overset{\epsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{0}(m|x-y|)

(B.17)

uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
avoiding the diagonal, together with the well-known asymptotics
Equation

4.41
of the modified Bessel function
K_{0}
. The first fact allows us to interchange the limit
\epsilon\to 0
with differentiation, while the asymptotic behavior renders the computation of the derivatives straightforward. The uniformity of the limits follows exactly as in the proof of
[
6
, Lemma 2.4]
, and in particular justifies the use of dominated convergence.

The statements involving distributional derivatives follow by integration by parts and basic distribution theory of the principal value of the Cauchy kernel in
B.14
.
∎

Then we have:

Lemma B.3

For any
\beta>0
and distinct
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
i=1,2,\dots,n
, the limits

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{m}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\right]&:=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\right],\\
\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\right]&:=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\right]\end{split}

(B.18)

exist. Moreover, the latter is given explicitly by

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\right]=\mathbf{1}_{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}=0}(4e^{-\gamma})^{\frac{n\beta}{4\pi}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{\sigma_{ij}\frac{\beta}{2\pi}},

(B.19)

where
\sigma_{ij}:=\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}
.

Remark B.4
.

The corresponding truncated correlation functions are obtained by applying the cumulants-to-moments formula
Equation

A.5
. We denote these truncated correlations by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{m}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{m}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\right]=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\right],

(B.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\right]=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\right].

(B.21)

Proof of
Lemma

B.3
.

The proof is exactly the same as that of
[
6
, Lemma 2.5]
. A straightforward Gaussian computation yields

\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\right]=\epsilon^{-\frac{n\beta}{4\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}n\beta K_{\epsilon}^{m}(0)}e^{-\beta\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\sigma_{ij}K_{\epsilon}^{m}(i,j)},

(B.22)

where we use the notation introduced in
Equation

1.24
. The claim then follows from the first two statements of
Lemma

B.1
.
∎

We also require charge correlation functions for the fields
\varphi_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}
and
\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
. More precisely, we have the following result.

Lemma B.5

For any
\beta,t>0
and distinct
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
i=1,2,\dots,n
, the limits

\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{i}\varphi(x_{i})}\right]

(B.23)

and

\lim_{m\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{i}\varphi(x_{i})}\right]

(B.24)

exist and are finite. Analogous statements hold for the corresponding truncated correlation functions by the cumulants to moments formula in
A.5
. All claims remain valid for fixed
m
.

Proof.

By independence, we may write

\begin{split}\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{i}\varphi(x_{i})}\right]&=\left(\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{i}\varphi(x_{i})}\right]\right)\\
&\quad\times\lim_{m\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\sqrt{t}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{i}\varphi(x_{i})}\right].\end{split}

(B.25)

Since the limit on the left-hand side exists by
Lemma

B.3
, it suffices to show that at least one of the limits on the right-hand side is finite and non-zero. We therefore establish the first limit.

A direct Gaussian computation yields

\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\right]=\epsilon^{-\frac{n\beta}{4\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}n\beta K_{\epsilon^{2},t}^{m}(0)}e^{-\beta\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\sigma_{ij}K_{\epsilon^{2},t}^{m}(i,j)},

(B.26)

where we again use the notation from
Equation

1.24
. We first consider

K_{\epsilon^{2},t}^{m}(0)=\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{t}e^{-m^{2}s}\frac{\operatorname{d}\!s}{4\pi s}\overset{u=m^{2}s}{=}\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{m^{2}\epsilon^{2}}^{m^{2}t}e^{-u}\frac{\operatorname{d}\!u}{u}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(E_{1}(m^{2}\epsilon^{2})-E_{1}(m^{2}t)\right),

(B.27)

where
E_{1}
is the exponential integral.
Using
Equation

4.44
we obtain

K_{\epsilon^{2},t}^{m}(0)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\!\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{t}\right)+\mathcal{O}(m^{2}t)+\mathcal{O}((m\epsilon)^{2})=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(\eps)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(t)+\mathcal{O}(m^{2}t)+\mathcal{O}((m\epsilon)^{2}).

(B.28)

Substituting this into the right-hand side of
Equation

B.26
yields for the first two factors

\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{-\frac{n\beta}{4\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}n\beta K_{\epsilon^{2},t}^{m}(0)}=t^{-\frac{n\beta}{8\pi}}

(B.29)

Next, we have

\begin{split}K_{\epsilon^{2},t}^{m}(i,j)&\leq\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}}{4s}}\frac{\operatorname{d}\!s}{s}=E_{1}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}}{4t}\right)<\infty\end{split}

(B.30)

for fixed
t>0
. Thus, by dominated convergence we obtain

\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,(\epsilon^{2},t)}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\right]=t^{-\frac{n\beta}{8\pi}}\exp(-\beta\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\sigma_{ij}E_1\rbr{\frac{|x_i-x_j|^2}{4t}})<\infty

(B.31)

for fixed
t>0
. This completes the proof.
∎

Now we can state the result for the pointwise truncated mixed charge and gradient correlation functions.

Lemma B.6

Let
\beta>0
,
n\geq 1
,
k\geq 0
,
x_{i},z_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
distinct, and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{n})\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
. Then the limits defining
\mathcal{C}_{m}^{n,k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\mathbf{z})
and
\mathcal{C}^{n,k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\mathbf{z})
in
Equation

B.5
and
Equation

B.6
respectively, exist uniformly on compact subsets of
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+k)d}
, where all
d
-dimensional components are distinct.

Furthermore, we have

\mathcal{C}^{n,k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\mathbf{z})=\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\right]\prod_{l=1}^{k}\left(i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{q=1}^{n}\sigma_{q}F_{d,l}(x_{q}-z_{l})\right),

(B.32)

where

F_{d,l}(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\begin{cases}\frac{x_{i}}{|x|^{2}},&\text{ if $d\geq 2$ and $D_{l}=\partial_{i}$,}\\
\frac{x}{|x|^{2}},&\text{ if $d=1$.}\end{cases}

(B.33)

Remark B.7
.

If
n=0
in the above lemma, by Gaussianity of the derivative fields only the
k=2
terms are non-zero; for the massless case, these are given in
Lemma

B.1
.

Proof.

The proof is exactly as in
[
6
, Lemma 2.6]
, taking into account the difference in the differential operators we use. Since we will need it later, we record the result of the Cameron-Martin-Girsanov transformation used in the proof of
[
6
, Lemma 2.6]
before taking the limits. We have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}&\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\prod_{l=1}^{k}(D_{l}\varphi)(z_{l})\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\right]\prod_{l=1}^{k}\Big(i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{q=1}^{n}\sigma_{q}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{q})(D_{l}\varphi)(z_{l})]\Big).\end{split}

(B.34)

The result then follows from
Lemmas

B.1
,
B.3
and
B.4
.
∎

Next, we consider the smeared correlation functions. For these, we again use distributional derivatives.

Proposition B.8

If
d=1
, fix
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
, and if
d\geq 2
, fix
\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
. Let
f_{i}\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\{-1,1\})
,
i=1,2,\dots,n
. Then for
n=1
or
n>N
, where
N\equiv N(\beta)
is even and such that
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbf{f})&:=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}(f_{j})\right]\\
&=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x_{i},\sigma_{i})\right)\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}<\infty,\end{split}

(B.35)

where
\mathcal{C}^{n}
is the pointwise truncated charge correlation function defined in
Remark

B.4
.

If
\supp(f_{i})\cap\supp(f_{j})=\emptyset
whenever
i\neq j
, the same statement holds for all
n\geq 1
and
\beta>0
. Similar claims hold for fixed
m\in(0,1)
.

The proof is completely analogous to that of
[
6
, Lemma 2.8]
, but we need to use the kernels
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
defined in
Proposition

3.6
, and
Lemma

3.7
for the limits of their integrals. Their proof can be readily extended to arbitrary dimension
d\geq 1
and to suitable kernels. Moreover,
[
6
, Lemma 5.6]
can be modified to our setting since it does not depend explicitly on the precise form of the kernels, and the Fourier argument for uniqueness applies equally well in arbitrary dimension.

Finally, we can state the result for the mixed smeared correlation functions.

Proposition B.9

Fix
\beta\in(0,4\pi)
for
d=1
and
\beta\in(0,(d+1)2\pi)
for
d\geq 2
, and let
N\equiv N(\beta)
be such that
\beta\in[\beta_{N},\beta_{N+2})
. Also suppose
f_{j}\in L_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\{-1,1\})
,
j=1,2,\dots,n
, and
g_{l}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
l=1,2,\dots,k
, and assume that if
n=2,3,\dots,N
, then
\supp(f_{j})\cap\supp(f_{j^{\prime}})=\emptyset
whenever
j\neq j^{\prime}
. Then the limits defining the mixed correlation functions
\mathcal{C}^{n,k}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})
and
\mathcal{C}_{m}^{n,k}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})
defined in
Equations

B.8
and
B.7
, respectively, exist and are finite. Furthermore, we have for
d\geq 2

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}^{n,k}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})&:=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{k}(D_{l}\varphi)(g_{l})\right]\\
&\,\,=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{(n+k)d}}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\bigg)\bigg(\prod_{l=1}^{k}g_{l}(z_{l})\bigg)\mathcal{C}^{n,k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}<\infty,\end{split}

(B.36)

where
\mathcal{C}^{n,k}
is the pointwise mixed truncated charge and gradient correlation function defined in
Equation

B.6
and given in closed form in
Lemma

B.6
. For
d=1
we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}^{n,k}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})&:=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{k}\varphi^{\prime}(g_{l})\right]\\
&\,\,=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\bigg)\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\,\big|\,j\in[n]\right]\prod_{l=1}^{k}\left[i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{q=1}^{n}\sigma_{q}\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi^{\prime}(z_{l})]\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&\,\,=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\bigg)\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\,\big|\,j\in[n]\right]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{l=1}^{k}\left[i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{q=1}^{n}\sigma_{q}\PV\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{g_{l}(z_{l})}{x_{q}-z_{l}}\operatorname{d}\!z_{l}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},\end{split}

(B.37)

where

\mathbb{E}^{T}\left[:e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}:\,\big|\,j\in[n]\right]\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi^{\prime}(z_{l})]

(B.38)

are defined by the limits in
Equation

B.18
and
Equation

B.11
respectively.

Furthermore, if
k\geq 1
, the claims above hold for
n=2,3,\dots,N
without assuming that the test functions have disjoint supports.

Proof.

The cases with
n=1,\,N+1,N+2,\dots
or when the test functions have disjoint supports are direct consequences of
Lemmas

B.1
,
B.2
,
B.6
and
B.8
.

The proof of the case
n=2=N
for
d\geq 2
is completely analogous to the proof of the
n=2
case in
[
6
, Lemma 2.10]
, and the constraint
\beta<(d+1)2\pi
can easily be deduced from
[
6
, equation (2.68)]
in its proof with the replacement
q+q^{\prime}\to k
.

For
d=1
the proof follows a similar strategy to the last part of the proof of
C
. First, we have by the third statement of
Theorem

2.1
and
Equation

B.34

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}^{T}&\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon^{-\frac{\beta}{4\pi}}e^{i\sqrt{\beta}\sigma_{j}\varphi}(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{k}(D_{l}\varphi)(g_{l})\right]\\
&=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\right)(-1)^{n-1}\left[\tilde{V}_{1}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)+[\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)-\tilde{V}_{1}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)]\right]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times(-1)^{k}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\left[i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{q=1}^{n}\sigma_{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{l}^{\prime}(z_{l})\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi(z_{l})]\operatorname{d}\!z_{l}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.\end{split}

(B.39)

We have applied integration by parts before and after using
Equation

B.34
to obtain the final form.

Assume that the configuration is neutral and, without loss of generality, that the first
p=n/2
charges are different from the rest. Then we can write

\begin{split}\sum_{q=1}^{n}\sigma_{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{l}^{\prime}(z_{l})\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi(z_{l})]\operatorname{d}\!z_{l}&=\sum_{q=1}^{n/2}\sigma_{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}}[g_{l}^{\prime}(x_{q}-z_{l})-g_{l}^{\prime}(x_{n/2+\rho(q)}-z_{l})]E_{m,\epsilon}(z_{l})\operatorname{d}\!z_{l},\\
&=\sum_{q=1}^{n/2}\sigma_{q}\bigg[(x_{q}-x_{n/2+\rho(q)})\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(g_{l}^{\prime\prime}(x_{n/2+\rho(q)}-z_{l})E_{m,\epsilon}(z_{l})\operatorname{d}\!z_{l}\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}}R(x_{q},x_{n/2+\rho(q)},z_{l})E_{m,\epsilon}(z_{l})\operatorname{d}\!z_{l}\bigg],\end{split}

(B.40)

where we have used Taylor’s theorem to first order and
R
denotes the resulting error term. Above,
\rho\in\mathbb{S}_{p}
is a permutation, and we may choose it to minimize the Wasserstein distance
Equation

2.21
. We have also denoted

E_{m,\epsilon}(z)=\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\infty}e^{-m^{2}s-\frac{z^{2}}{4s}}\frac{\operatorname{d}\!s}{4\pi s}.

(B.41)

Then we note that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}R(x_{q},x_{n/2+\rho(q)},z_{l})\operatorname{d}\!z_{l}=0
(to see this, write
R
as the difference of the other terms in the Taylor estimate), and furthermore we can always write

R(x_{q},x_{n/2+\rho(q)},z_{l})=(x_{q}-x_{n/2+\rho(q)})\tilde{R}(x_{q},x_{n/2+\rho(q)},z_{l}),

(B.42)

where
\tilde{R}
is uniformly bounded for
x_{q},x_{n/2+\rho(q)}
restricted to a compact set and locally integrable in
z_{l}
. If the charge configuration is not neutral or if
d(\mathbf{x})\geq 1
, we do not need to do the pairing on the right-hand side of
Equation

B.40
, and we can simply bound each integral uniformly. By these facts and the estimates in the proof of
Equation

4.39
, we may now write for
\mathbf{x}\in\prod_{j=1}^{n}\supp(f_{j})

\left|(-1)^{k}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\left[i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{q=1}^{n}\sigma_{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{l}^{\prime}(z_{l})\mathbb{E}_{m,\epsilon}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi(z_{l})]\operatorname{d}\!z_{l}\right]\right|\leq C[H_{1}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]^{k}\leq CH_{1}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\leq C,

(B.43)

for some constant
C>0
independent of
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
. We can use the constant bound for the term corresponding to
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}-\tilde{V}_{1}^{n}
and the second-to-last bound for the term corresponding to
\tilde{V}_{1}^{n}
. Then
Propositions

2.7
and
2.10
yield integrable majorants independent of
\epsilon
, so that the
\epsilon\to 0
limit can be taken through the
x
-integrals in both terms. In the term corresponding to
\tilde{V}_{1}^{n}
, even the
m\to 0
limit can be taken through the
x
-integrals since
1<m^{-2}
so that the majorant provided by
Proposition

2.7
is independent of
m
. Furthermore,
Corollary

2.10
shows that the term corresponding to
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}-\tilde{V}_{1}^{n}
is bounded uniformly in
m,\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Thus, we are left to perform the explicit error analysis for the term corresponding to
\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}-\tilde{V}_{1}^{n}
analogous to the proof of
Corollary

2.10
. Recall that
R_{1}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
denotes the error term from the proof of
Corollary

2.10
with
t=1
, that is defined in
Equation

2.154

We can write

\begin{split}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\right)(-1)^{n-1}\left[\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)-\tilde{V}_{1}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m)\right]\\
&\qquad\times(-1)^{k}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\left[i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{q=1}^{n}\sigma_{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{l}^{\prime}(z_{l})\mathbb{E}_{m}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi(z_{l})]\operatorname{d}\!z_{l}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\right)(-1)^{n-1}\left[\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\tilde{V}_{1}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right]\\
&\qquad\times(-1)^{k}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\left[i\sqrt{\beta}\sum_{q=1}^{n}\sigma_{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{l}^{\prime}(z_{l})\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi(z_{l})]\operatorname{d}\!z_{l}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
&\quad+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\right)\left[\mathcal{R}_{m}^{1}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})+\mathcal{R}_{m}^{2}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},\end{split}

(B.44)

where again
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=\lim_{m\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)
,
\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m):=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\tilde{V}_{t}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma}|m,\epsilon)
(and these exist by
Remark

2.6
),

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{m}^{1}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})=(-i\sqrt{\beta})^{k}(-1)^{n-1}R_{1}^{n}(m,\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left[\sum_{q=1}^{n}\sigma_{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{j}^{\prime}(z_{j})\mathbb{E}_{m}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi(z_{j})]\operatorname{d}\!z_{j}\right]\end{split}

(B.45)

and

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{m}^{2}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})&=(-i\sqrt{\beta})^{k}(-1)^{n-1}[\tilde{V}_{\infty}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\tilde{V}_{1}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]\\
&\qquad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\uplus G=[k]\\
G\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\bigg\{\left(\prod_{i\in F}\left[\sum_{q=1}^{n}\sigma_{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{i}^{\prime}(z_{i})\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi(z_{i})]\operatorname{d}\!z_{i}\right]\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times\left(\prod_{j\in G}\left[\sum_{q=1}^{n}\sigma_{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{j}^{\prime}(z_{j})\left(\mathbb{E}_{m}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi(z_{j})]-\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi(z_{j})]\right)\operatorname{d}\!z_{j}\right]\right)\bigg\}.\end{split}

(B.46)

The above sum goes through the ordered bipartitions
\{F,G\}
of
[k]
, except
\{[k],\emptyset\}
. Recall that
\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x)\varphi(y)]=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(|x-y|)
when integrated against a compactly supported smooth test function with vanishing integral. The integral of
g_{l}^{\prime}
indeed vanishes since
g_{l}
has compact support. Furthermore,
\mathbb{E}_{m}[\varphi(x)\varphi(y)]=\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{0}(m|x-y|)
. By the asymptotics
Equation

4.41
and the facts that the functions
g_{l}
have compact support and the logarithm is locally integrable, each integral above is uniformly bounded in
m\in(0,1)
and
x_{q}\in\supp(f_{q})
. Furthermore,
\mathbb{E}_{m}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi(z_{l})]-\mathbb{E}[\varphi(x_{q})\varphi(z_{l})]\to 0
as
m\to 0
pointwise for almost every
z_{l}
. Therefore, by the error analysis in the proof of
Corollary

2.10
starting from
Equation

2.153
, we have

\lim_{m\to 0}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\right)\left[|\mathcal{R}_{m}^{1}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})|+|\mathcal{R}_{m}^{2}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\sigma})|\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=0.

(B.47)

This completes the proof since the alternative forms follow directly from
Lemma

B.1
.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of
Lemma

3.2

First, we introduce the same tools that were used in
[
6
, Lemma 5.2]
, namely Dudley’s theorem (see, e.g.,
[
1
, Section 1.3]
) and the Borel–TIS inequality (see, e.g.,
[
1
, Section 2.1]
). These are

Theorem C.1

(Dudley’s theorem)

Let
T
be a metric space (such that
(T,d_{X})
as defined below is compact) and let
X
be a centered real-valued Gaussian process on
T
. Define another (pseudo) metric on
T
by

d_{X}(t,s):=\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\{X(t)-X(s)\}^{2}]}.

Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in T}X(t)\right]\leq C\int_{0}^{\diam(T)/2}\sqrt{H_{X}(\epsilon)}\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon,

(C.1)

where
H_{X}
is the log-entropy corresponding to the metric
d_{X}
. It is defined by
H_{X}(\epsilon):=\log(N_X(\eps))
, where
N_{X}(\epsilon)
is the minimal number of (closed)
d_{X}
-balls with radius
\epsilon
needed to cover
T
.

and

Theorem C.2

(Borel–TIS inequality)

Let
T
be as above and let
X
be an almost surely bounded, centered Gaussian process on
T
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in T}X(t)]<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}(\{\sup_{t\in T}X(t)-\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in T}X(t)]>u\})\leq e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma_{T}^{2}}},

(C.2)

where
\sigma_{T}:=\sup_{t\in T}\mathbb{E}[(X(t))^{2}]
.

Remark C.3
.

The following facts are also stated in
[
1
, Section 2.1]
. The Borel–TIS inequality has the simple corollary

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\sup_{t\in T}X(t)>u\right\}\right)\leq e^{-\frac{\left(u-\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in T}X(t)\right]\right)^{2}}{2\sigma_{T}^{2}}},

(C.3)

and by symmetry we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\sup_{t\in T}\left|X(t)\right|>u\right\}\right)\leq 2\,\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\sup_{t\in T}X(t)>u\right\}\right).

(C.4)

In the proof below, we simplify notation by writing
\varphi\equiv\varphi_{m,\sqrt{t}}
, and similarly we drop subscripts on expectations and probabilities, since there is no possibility for confusion.

Proof of
Lemma

3.2
.

First, by Jensen’s inequality, used in the form

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}\right)^{\alpha}=N^{\alpha}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{N}x_{i}\right)^{\alpha}\leq N^{\alpha-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{\alpha},

(C.5)

where
x_{i}\geq 0
for all
i
and
\alpha\in[1,2)
, and the general Hölder inequality for expectations, it suffices to prove the claim with
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{C_{k}(\Lambda)}
replaced by
p_{\nu,\Lambda}(\varphi)
for arbitrary
\nu
of order less than or equal to
k
.

We write

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{B[p_{\nu,\Lambda}(\varphi)]^{\alpha}}\right]&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{e^{B[p_{\nu,\Lambda}(\varphi)]^{\alpha}>u}\right\}\right)\operatorname{d}\!u\\
&=\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{e^{B[p_{\nu,\Lambda}(\varphi)]^{\alpha}>u}\right\}\right)\operatorname{d}\!u}_{=1}+\int_{1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{p_{\nu,\Lambda}(\varphi)>\left(\frac{\log(u)}{B}\right)^{1/\alpha}\right\}\right)\operatorname{d}\!u\\
&\leq 1+C(\alpha,B)\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{\alpha-1}e^{Br^{\alpha}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{p_{\nu,\Lambda}(\varphi)>r\right\}\right)\operatorname{d}\!r,\end{split}

(C.6)

where the first term equals
1
since the exponent is positive, and
C(\alpha,B)>0
is a finite constant.

Next, consider the induced (pseudo) metric
d_{\nu}\equiv d_{\partial^{\nu}\varphi}
from Dudley’s Theorem. We have by
Equation

2.74
together with the bounds
e^{-m^{2}s}\leq 1
and
f_{d}\leq 1
,

\begin{split}d_{\nu}(x,y)&:=\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{(\partial_{\nu}\varphi)(x)-(\partial_{\nu}\varphi)(y)\right\}^{2}\right]}\\
&=\sqrt{2\bigl(K_{\sqrt{t}}^{m,\nu}(0)-K_{\sqrt{t}}^{m,\nu}(x,y)\bigr)}\\
&\leq\sqrt{C_{t,\nu}}\,|x-y|^{\frac{1}{2}},\end{split}

(C.7)

where
C_{t,\nu}>0
is a constant independent of
m
and
K_{\sqrt{t}}^{m,\nu}(0)=K_{\sqrt{t}}^{m,\nu}(x,x)
analogously to
Equation

1.24
. Similarly to the metric above, we have denoted
N_{\partial^{\nu}\varphi}\equiv N_{\nu}
and
H_{\partial^{\nu}\varphi}\equiv H_{\nu}
.

Therefore,

\{y\in\Lambda:\sqrt{C_{t,\nu}}\,|x-y|^{1/2}<\epsilon\}\subset\{y\in\Lambda:d_{\nu}(x,y)<\epsilon\},

(C.8)

so the number of
d_{\nu}
-balls of radius
\epsilon
needed to cover
\Lambda
is bounded by the number of Euclidean balls of radius
\epsilon^{2}/C_{t,\nu}
needed to cover
\Lambda
. Since
(\Lambda,\cdot)
, with
\cdot
denoting the Euclidean metric, is compact, so is
(\Lambda,d_{\nu})
. If
\Lambda\subset[-L,L]^{d}
for some
L\in\mathbb{N}
, then the number of Euclidean balls of radius
\epsilon
needed to cover
\Lambda
is at most
(2L/\epsilon)^{d}
. Moreover, in this case
\Lambda\subset B_{\sqrt{d}L}(0)
, where
B_{\sqrt{d}L}(0)
denotes the Euclidean ball of radius
\sqrt{d}L
centered at the origin. Consequently,
N_{\nu}(\epsilon)=1
for
\epsilon>dL^{2}/C_{t,\nu}
.

It follows that

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\diam(\Lambda)/2}\sqrt{\log(N_\nu(\eps))}\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon&\leq\int_{0}^{dL^{2}/C_{t,\nu}}\sqrt{\log\!\left(\left[\frac{2L}{\epsilon^{2}/C_{t,\nu}}\right]^{d}\right)}\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon\\
&=\sqrt{4dLC_{t,\nu}}\int_{0}^{\frac{d}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\frac{L}{C_{t,\nu}}\right)^{3/2}}\sqrt{\log(u^{-1})}\operatorname{d}\!u,\end{split}

(C.9)

where
u=\epsilon/\sqrt{2LC_{t,\nu}}
. Substituting
u=e^{-x^{2}}
yields

2\sqrt{4dLC_{t,\nu}}\int_{-\infty}^{B_{t,\nu}(L)}x^{2}e^{-x^{2}}\operatorname{d}\!x<\infty,

(C.10)

with

B_{t,\nu}(L):=\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{d}\left[\frac{C_{t,\nu}}{L}\right]^{3/2}\right)}.

(C.11)

By Dudley’s theorem, we conclude that

\displaystyle E_{m,t,\nu,\Lambda}(\varphi):=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{x\in\Lambda}(\partial^{\nu}\varphi)(x)\right]\leq 2\sqrt{4dLC_{t,\nu}}\int_{-\infty}^{B_{t,\nu}(L)}x^{2}e^{-x^{2}}\operatorname{d}\!x=:\tilde{C}_{t,\nu,\Lambda}<\infty,

(C.12)

where
\tilde{C}_{t,\nu,\Lambda}
is manifestly independent of
m
. In particular,
\partial^{\nu}\varphi
is almost surely bounded, and we may apply the Borel–TIS inequality. Using
Remark

C.3
, we obtain

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{p_{\nu,\Lambda}(\varphi)>r\right\}\right)\leq 2\exp\!\left(-\frac{\left[r-E_{m,t,\nu,\Lambda}(\varphi)\right]^{2}}{2\sigma_{\nu}^{2}}\right),

(C.13)

where

\sigma_{\nu}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[(\partial^{\nu}\varphi(x))^{2}\right]=\int_{t}^{\infty}(\partial_{1}^{\nu}\partial_{2}^{\nu}C_{s}^{m})(0)\operatorname{d}\!s\leq\bar{B}_{t,\nu}<\infty.

(C.14)

for some constant
\bar{B}_{t,\nu}>0
independent of
m
again by
Equation

2.74
.

Thus, for
r>2\tilde{C}_{t,\nu,\Lambda}
we have

\frac{(r-E_{m,t,\nu,\Lambda}(\varphi))^{2}}{2\sigma_{\nu}^{2}}\geq\frac{(r-\tilde{C}_{t,\nu,\Lambda})^{2}}{2\bar{B}_{t,\nu}^{2}}\geq\frac{r^{2}}{8\bar{B}_{t,\nu}^{2}},

(C.15)

and consequently

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{p_{\nu,\Lambda}(\varphi)>r\right\}\right)\leq\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }r<2\tilde{C}_{t,\nu,\Lambda},\\[3.00003pt]
e^{-\frac{r^{2}}{8\bar{B}_{t,\nu}^{2}}},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(C.16)

Finally, we estimate the last integral in
Equation

C.6
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{\alpha-1}e^{Br^{\alpha}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{p_{\nu,\Lambda}(\varphi)>r\right\}\right)\operatorname{d}\!r

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{2\tilde{C}_{t,\nu,\Lambda}}r^{\alpha-1}e^{Br^{\alpha}}\operatorname{d}\!r

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{2\tilde{C}_{t,\nu,\Lambda}}^{\infty}r^{\alpha-1}e^{Br^{\alpha}}e^{-\frac{r^{2}}{8\bar{B}_{t,\nu}^{2}}}\operatorname{d}\!r

\displaystyle<\infty,

where we used that probabilities are bounded by
1
in the first term. Since
2>\alpha\geq 1
, the first integral is finite at the origin and the second at infinity. Thus, we obtain an upper bound independent of
m
.
∎
